# Preface
### of the
## Editor.

Since the present book appeared in its first edition containing many errors, the author may be satisfied that he was not more strictly reviewed; however, I am nonetheless surprised that a work touching upon so many sciences <note>original: "Wissenschaften". In the 18th century, this term referred broadly to any organized body of knowledge, including philosophy and natural history, not just the "hard sciences" as we define them today.</note>—which was bound to offend almost all system-builders <note>original: "Systematiker". This refers to philosophers who attempted to explain the entirety of existence through a single, rigid logical framework.</note>, yet certainly contains much that is new and ingenious—has been so little praised or criticized.

The gentlemen reviewers have been content to simply list the titles of the subject matter, to regret that the author is not a Kantian <note>A follower of Immanuel Kant (1724–1804). At the time of this writing, Kant’s "Critical Philosophy" was the dominant academic force in Germany, and reviewers often dismissed any work that did not conform to his specific methods and terminology.</note>, and to accuse him of mystical expressions, without considering that he was required to treat mystical subjects within his system. The only question is whether he has employed them well or poorly.

Presumably, people have shied away from the extensive labor required to properly analyze this book; and since it is written clearly, yet in an extremely condensed style, and very unsystematically due to the scattered nature of its sentences...

<meta>catchword: is [the German 'ist' would complete the sentence 'written unsystematically is']</meta>

<summary>The editor defends the author against critics who dismissed the first edition for being "mystical" or for not following the popular Kantian philosophy of the day. He argues that the book's density and unique structure likely discouraged a thorough analysis by its reviewers.</summary>
<keywords>preface, book reviews, Kantianism, mysticism, philosophical systems, 18th-century scholarship, scientific methodology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the editor's preface. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing the author's work as written clearly but unsystematically. This page picks up with the verb "is," completing the thought that the work's lack of a rigid structure led readers to skim it too quickly.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

is, read too cursorily and perhaps with dogmatic disfavor. I wished that our author had presented His system in a better and entirely different order; however, He had worked on each article at different times as special, small treatises, and perhaps it would have been hardly possible for Him to organize such an extensive system of teaching <term>Lehrgebäude: literally a "building of learning," this refers to a comprehensive and structured theoretical framework.</term> in such a way that one could have surveyed it comfortably and clearly, step by step. Everyone must therefore become acquainted with the whole piece by piece, in order to settle into it according to their own manner.

Yet I believe I am doing a service to our author and His readers by venturing to give them, in this preface, a short overview of the work in that order which seems most natural to me.

**Force** <term>Kraft: In 18th-century philosophy, "Kraft" (force or power) was a central concept used to explain how the soul interacts with the body and how nature operates at a fundamental level.</term> is the fundamental word upon which the entire system rests. Everyone has a concept of this expression, and though it may be indefinite and obscure for most, it is still always somewhat clearer and easier than that which one can associate with the word **Spirit**. One will see without effort that all properties of matter are forceful, and yet that force is nobler than matter and distinct from it.

---

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>of the Editor.</header>
<sig>* 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: from</meta>

The author therefore represents **Spirit** as a force and **Matter** as a composition of forces enclosed in an impenetrable circle, because resistance constitutes the essential character of matter.

On this occasion, He provides a pure concept of the simplicity of a spiritual being and of the composition of a bodily thing. He shows that the former, though divisible unto infinity, always retains its essence because it is simple; the latter, however, because it consists of parts, can be called indissoluble but not properly divisible, in that it is already, by its nature, divided in a specific way. At the same time, He investigates how many adventurous and incomprehensible opinions about the properties of a spirit—and especially those regarding monads <term>Monads: a term popularized by the philosopher Leibniz referring to the basic, indivisible units of reality that make up the universe.</term>—have arisen.

After this, the author asks (and with this, in my opinion, he should have begun) what **Force** actually is?

He traces this concept entirely back to motion, noting that immobility is a non-entity <note>original: "Unding". This suggests something that is logically impossible or does not exist in reality.</note>, while motion is universal, eternal, and independent; and that it has two essential directions, namely: from the center toward the circumference and

<summary>The editor explains that the author's work was written as separate essays, making it difficult to read as a single system. He introduces the core concepts of the book: Force (Kraft), Spirit (Geist), and Matter (Materie), defining matter as a "circle of forces."</summary>
<keywords>Force, Spirit, Matter, System of Teaching, Monads, Motion, Simplicity vs Composition</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s overview of the author’s system of natural philosophy. The previous page (vii) ended mid-sentence, describing how the author represents the progression of forces. This page picks up with the movement from the center outward.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>
<meta>catchword: few</meta>

from this toward the center, from which the two opposing forces, the **expansive** and **compressive**, have arisen, and shows how motion is so essentially connected with sensation <term>Fühlung: literally "feeling." In this context, it refers to the basic capacity of a substance to perceive or react to its environment, a precursor to consciousness.</term> that both together constitute the most essential force, and both together, acting against one another, form the chain of all nature. But because a contact is necessary between sensation and motion, and since generally a thing cannot well be conceived of without substance, the author concludes from this that force must be a substance. After the article on sensation follows one on truth, which deserves to be noted. Hereupon He examines the inner nature of fire, air, and light. To him, fire and light are sensible proofs of the existence of simple substances that are entirely made of force. Fire is also truly nothing other than the expansive force; however, the compressive force necessarily opposed to it is not so clearly demonstrated in nature. The author places it within salts and suspects its fundamental essence lies in light. Air seems to Him a watery body animated by both forces, which make themselves felt by us as heat and frost, and which now expand and now contract the air. Admittedly, the observations on these three—beings that have never been sufficiently investigated—contain many chemical and physical heresies <note>The editor uses "heresies" (Ketzereyen) to describe scientific theories that contradicted the established Newtonian or Enlightenment-era consensus of the late 1700s.</note>. A positive substance of cold, and light being entirely opposed to fire, will, in our century, find few

<column-break/>

<page-num>ix</page-num>
<header>of the Editor.</header>
<meta>catchword: each other</meta>

few admirers; yet naturalists <note>original: "Naturkündiger"</note> are very inconsistent in their preferences, and a time may well come when other experiences frustrate the current ones and declare that to be true which is now held to be false, or at most, possible.

Just as fire and light present us with a perfect image of a simple, powerful substance, so elasticity is an even more striking proof of the effect of an independent force. It possesses the double capacity of motion: to expand itself when it is pressed, and to narrow itself when it has been expanded by a force; but the most perfect expression of true and undivided force is seen in the living force <term>lebendige Kraft: while this term often referred to "kinetic energy" in physics (vis viva), here the author uses it to describe the "vital force" or soul-like power that animates living beings.</term> that inspires humans and animals. It combines its own sensation with its own motion, can excite the one through the other, and move itself by both expelling and narrowing.

In the first section of his work, our author has set forth an opinion based on tradition—of which we shall speak further below—in which He assumes that the simple primordial force <term>Urkraft: the original, fundamental power from which all other forces in the universe are derived.</term> was separated, dividing itself into two opposing forces which form the whole of nature, and that in both lie two natural drives according to which they must act. These are: The drive to [reach] each other

<summary>The editor describes the author's theory of "expansive" and "compressive" forces as the foundation of nature, identifying fire, light, and "living force" (in humans and animals) as primary examples of these forces in action. He acknowledges that the author's ideas, such as the existence of a "substance of cold," are scientifically controversial but potentially ahead of their time.</summary>
<keywords>expansive force, compressive force, substance, fire, light, air, elasticity, primordial force, natural philosophy, sensation, motion, living force</keywords>

<page-num>x</page-num>
# Preface

to contend with one another, if they are able, or to flee one another, if one is stronger than the other, and the drive to unite, which however only takes place among forces of the same kind and constitutes the chief law of all moral and physical beings. Accordingly, our author explains in a very singular and, to me, illuminating way various manifestations of force in matter, such as: the stimulus and the essence of pressure and counter-pressure—derived from general sensation <term>Fühlung: The author uses this to mean a primitive, physical "feeling" or sensitivity inherent in all matter, not just conscious beings.</term>; the centrifugal force <term>Fliehkraft: Literally "fleeing force."</term> of the throw and the impact—from the adversity of the separated forces; and gravity—from the law of union, according to which the fire locked within matter hastens to unite itself by the shortest path with the fire-magazine <note>original: "Feuermagazin." This refers to a central storehouse or source of primordial heat/energy at the center of the earth or universe.</note> lying at the center, as the greatest mass of itself.

After this, the author ventures to trace the powers of the soul, all its properties and effects, back to sensation and movement, insofar as all our knowledge is composed of comparisons and memories of sensory feelings.

What He says about memory and consciousness is certainly ingenious, and the latter becomes very understandable if one imagines the soul as a substance that has the power to press itself through its own movement, and consequently to feel itself.

<meta>catchword: Since</meta>

<page-num>xi</page-num>
# of the Editor.

Since our author now posits in the elastic substance an independent being that not only possesses all material forces but also qualifies for all spiritual ones, He has found the most suitable material for an **All-Being** <term>Allwesen: A philosophical term for a universal substance or God-like entity that encompasses everything in existence.</term> that can make everything out of itself, and discovered the great **Creative Unity**, from which all the numbers of creation could arise.

This He develops in the second volume, by showing that "Nothing" and "empty space" are ghostly errors—that one must put the All-Being and an elastic fullness in their place—that the world could not possibly have been created out of nothing—that God created directly from His substance and, in the following stages, indirectly—that creating means **separating**—that everything is eternal; a new proof for the Godhead—what is God?—what could have been the first object of the idea of God? A fortunate representation of a creation of ideas without a preceding representation. Finally, there comes a hypothesis about the formation of matter, which is supported by certain traditions.

This hypothesis should actually have led our author to those theories contained in the first volume, which are somewhat mystical, but necessarily so, because when one speaks of God, of spirits <note>The text breaks off here; the German word "Geistern" (spirits) is split by the catchword.</note>

<meta>catchword: spirits</meta>

<summary>In these pages, the editor explains the author's theory that physical forces like gravity and pressure arise from a universal "sensation" and that the soul is a substance capable of self-perception. The author argues against the vacuum, suggesting instead an "All-Being" or "Creative Unity" from which all matter was separated rather than created from nothing.</summary>
<keywords>All-Being, Creative Unity, sensation, gravity, soul as substance, creation as separation, rejection of vacuum, fire-magazine</keywords>

<page-num>xii</page-num>
# Preface

<meta>catchword: Kan-</meta>

stars and that which existed before time, one either must not speak at all or must speak that language which is marked with the diminishing epithet of mysticism <note>original: "Mystik." In the late 18th century, "mysticism" was often used as a derogatory label by Enlightenment rationalists for any philosophy that dealt with spiritual or non-empirical realities.</note>.

In this theosophical <term>Theosophy: Here referring to a philosophical system that seeks knowledge of God and the universe through individual spiritual insight or formal speculation, rather than just faith.</term> treatise, our author investigates, above all, the concept of the infinity of numbers, space, and time.

After he has established deeply considered reflections on immensity and eternity, he then examines the nature of time and space. Since the essence of time is a duration, and the instantaneous transition from the beginning to the end can only be hindered by a resistance, the first resistance in eternity must have produced time; just as the first separation from immensity must have formed the first space. This could only have happened through a circle <note>This suggests a geometric metaphor for creation: an infinite line cannot enclose a space, but a circle creates a boundary between an "inside" and an "outside" within infinity.</note>, because any other line drawn through immensity would not have been sufficient to perfectly separate something from it.

All of this led the author to the origin of evil. He proves that universal, ancient history—almost indispensable to human reflection—of a rebellion of powerful spirits through the existence of time and space; and here, certainly, is great material for a war with the

<column-break/>

<page-num>xiii</page-num>
# of the Editor.
<meta>catchword: konnte</meta>

Kantians <note>Kantianer: Followers of Immanuel Kant. The editor anticipates a conflict because Kant argued that things like "the beginning of the world" or "the origin of evil" were beyond the limits of human reason to prove.</note>. Then comes the great question: whether God foresaw the emergence of evil? This is answered in the negative: 1) because He would have had to think the evil first, and consequently would have had to be its inventor; 2) because the creation of a new source of causes and effects cannot happen other than through a creative power of free will, but the free thought of a free will could not be fathomed without being determined by it beforehand. Then comes an analysis of freedom that is conceived in extremely abstract terms, but well deserves the effort one takes to understand it. Following this are striking proofs that in all our actions we are guided by circumstances that do not depend on us. Finally, our author concludes this treatise with a sketch of divine providence; and I wish that He had concluded the entire book with this new and comforting hypothesis. He assumes: that we were all there before the Fall <note>The Fall of Man: The editor is describing a theory of "pre-existence," suggesting human souls existed in a spiritual state before being born into the physical world.</note>, and that through the misuse of our then-free will, we participated in it more or less and thereby more or less corrupted our moral nature. Improvement, and not eternal revenge, must be the purpose of a loving and all-wise Providence.

Only repentance can improve us, and only the bad consequences of unlawful actions can bring about a repentance that recognizes its own wrongdoing. Punishments and torments

<note>Vocabulary: Theosophy, Infinity, Space, Time, Origin of Evil, Kantians, Freedom, Divine Providence, the Fall, Repentance</note>

<summary>The editor describes the author's theory that time and space were created through a "resistance" in eternity and links the physical world to a pre-cosmic rebellion of spirits. It also argues that God did not foresee evil, as doing so would make Him its author, and suggests that human suffering is a means of divine providence to lead souls to repentance.</summary>

<keywords>metaphysics, cosmology, origin of evil, Immanuel Kant, free will, pre-existence of souls, divine providence, nature of time</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction. The previous pages discussed the author's views on the origin of time, space, and the necessity of "resistance" in creation. The editor is now addressing the author's "theodicy"—his explanation for why a good God allows evil and suffering to exist in the world.</meta>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>Preface</header>

...could be invented by a good God; rather, they are the most natural consequences of transgressing those laws that bring happiness. The exact relationship between these consequences and the laws reveals what we call the **justice of God**, whereas his **goodness** is the inventor of these laws as a means for improvement. The latter is organized as follows: the spirit—fettered as a human being in the prison of its sensory body by the chain of fate—commits all those crimes necessary to prepare for itself those ill consequences which suffice to awaken a ground of repentance within it, appropriate to its original corruption. Every role that is to appear on the world stage <note>original: "Welttheater." A common metaphor in this era comparing human life to a play where God acts as the director, assigning parts to souls for their spiritual development.</note> is necessary, foreseen, and determined; but every spirit that must play its part is chosen for it by the reforming wisdom of God and receives that role which is most useful for it. Thus, the most corrupted spirits animate the greatest evildoers, and the most innocent die as innocent children. I doubt that a better way will ever be found to reconcile the goodness of God with his justice, and both with the highly probable doctrine of **fatalism** <term>Fatalism: The philosophical view that all events are predetermined by necessity and that humans are subject to an inevitable destiny.</term>.

The sixth section contains miscellaneous, mostly physiological articles. The one dealing with the passions seems to me the most excellent, because

<meta>catchword: it</meta>

<page-num>xv</page-num>
<header>of the Editor.</header>

it provides the true ground of our moral goodness. The entirely new article inserted into this volume concerning hearing and seeing contains more questions than answers, and merely draws the researcher's attention to the secrets of the eye and especially the ear.

The final volume provides several treatises by our author—some already in print, others new—which have no particular connection to his system, but which we append to his main work because we wish to preserve them.

The first is certainly the most rational thing that can be said about the **Cabala** <note>original: "Cabale." Referring to Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism. In the 18th century, many Western philosophers studied "Christian Cabala" as a way to understand the hidden mechanics of the universe.</note> and **magic**, if one wishes to speak of them at all; few men have worked more in this field than our author. What he says, however, will only be fully understandable to those who, like him, have circulated through all the secret circles <note>The author likely refers to the various "Secret Societies" (like the Rosicrucians or Freemasons) that were hubs for mystical and philosophical study during the Enlightenment.</note>.

The second treatise, on the **art of music**, gives a very clear explanation of all the components of music, an entirely new definition of melody (which is so difficult to distinguish from harmony), reasons against our current musical expression, and means to invent a better one.

To every friend of animals, I recommend the extremely paradoxical article dedicated to them.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, the editor explains the author's view that "evil" is a necessary tool for spiritual reformation and outlines the supplementary essays on physiology, mysticism, music, and animal nature.</summary>
<keywords>justice of God, fatalism, world theater, passions, Cabala, magic, music theory, animal rights, spiritual reformation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the editor's preface, which previously detailed the author's "theodicy" and the predetermined roles of spirits. The editor is now addressing the final section of the book—a personal statement of faith—and giving instructions on how to approach the difficult text.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>Editor’s Preface</header>
<meta>bleed-through from reverse page visible</meta>

The final treatise on faith and the **method of faith** <term>Method of faith: A systematic way of approaching religious belief, often contrasted in this era with purely rational or scientific proofs.</term> contains our author’s **confession of faith**. We have appended it to his work at his express request, in order to protect him all the more from any suspicion of **materialism** <term>Materialism: The philosophical view that nothing exists except matter and its movements; in this period, it was often equated with atheism.</term> and **un-Christianity**.

In conclusion, I ask all readers—those who would rather think their way into something new with some effort than merely flip through a new book written about something old—to first read through our author’s work in its entirety, exactly as it is, and then to go through it once more in the order suggested by me. Their effort will not be without reward.

# Infinity.

The need of our intellect to seek a beginning of the beginning, and the impossibility of finding it, develops within us the greatest of all thoughts — — the idea of infinity.

We recognize three types of infinity: eternity, immeasurability, and numbers.

### Numbers.

A deep-seated conviction teaches us that the sequence of numbers goes into infinity. We have been able to obtain the certainty of this neither through the senses nor through experience, and yet we do not doubt it; it is a truth of which the opposite cannot be conceived at all.

Numbers provide the clearest concept that we can form of infinity, and are the most evident means to convince ourselves of its real existence.

If we consider numbers as units, we can accumulate them into infinity, and the expansion of the plurality has no limits.

<summary>This page lists the contents of the author's metaphysical essays and begins the first chapter on "Infinity," specifically exploring how the concept of infinite numbers is an undeniable truth of human reason.</summary>
<keywords>infinity, numbers, immeasurability, eternity, free will, providence, perfectibility, metaphysics, philosophy of mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of infinity from page 3. The previous text established that numerical expansion (addition) is infinite; this page begins by explaining that numerical division (fractions) is also infinite before moving on to the second type of infinity: immeasurability.</meta>

# Infinity.

If we view them <note>Referring to numbers, specifically units.</note> as fractions of a unit, we can divide the latter into infinity, and the reduction of these is likewise without limits. There is neither cause nor possibility to set a goal for numerical progression.

### Immeasurability.

Immeasurability is the strangest of all ideas that our soul can conceive.

Whenever we think of a space or a figure, an indefinite extension <note>German: *Ausdehnung*. In Enlightenment philosophy, this refers to the quality of occupying space.</note> lies at the foundation in which this idea occurs. We cut out pieces from the whole, so to speak, as much as we need, and the material never runs out. We can set sun-diameters <note>The diameter of the sun was a common 18th-century benchmark for an unimaginably large distance.</note> next to each other in all directions, and even if we were to move this monstrous scale forward for millennia, we are convinced by an irrefutable feeling that we would reach no boundaries.

What then is this incomprehensible idea, this feeling of a property encompassing all images and ratios, this trace of a quality belonging to a God?

Since some sharp thinkers <note>Likely a reference to rationalist philosophers who argued that human concepts of the infinite were merely "negative" ideas—the absence of limits—rather than "positive" insights.</note> have suspected that the absolute negation of certain real things has produced dark ideas of possibilities in us, to which we choose to attribute a reality

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Infinity.</header>
<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>The catchword "legen" from the previous page completes the word "beyzulegen" (to attribute or assign).</meta>

attribute <note>German: *beyzulegen*. The author is continuing the thought that we "assign" reality to these abstract possibilities.</note> and choose to adorn them with the venerable veil of the incomprehensible; so we want above all to investigate whether the thought of immeasurability did not perhaps arise merely from the absolute negation of measurability?

This abstract, non-pictorial operation of the soul could at most have produced the word *immeasurability*, but not an inner feeling of it, which we actually possess; for, when our imagination forms monstrous sizes in monstrous numbers alongside one another, we see, as it were, a ground in the interior which provides the material for it: We follow an impulse that seeks limits — — we sense the impossibility of finding them, and our understanding tells us in the end that it sees no reason why? or how? limits should exist.

But could not the idea of immeasurability be a cumulative juxtaposition of known sizes, which we think of successively and repeat in infinite numbers? This procedure does indeed lead us on the path to suspect immeasurability; for from this arises a failed attempt to measure that which cannot be measured. — But what is that which cannot be measured? Only the consideration of the fruitless continuation of an assumed scale and mainly the circumstance that it in infinite

<meta>This page continues the exploration of immeasurability (Unermeßlichkeit). The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how some philosophers view infinity as a "negative" idea. This page completes that thought, arguing instead that immeasurability is a positive, real feeling within the human soul.</meta>

[infinite]<insert>unend</insert>licher numbers upon the ground-plan of immeasurability, which lies within us, can be moved forward, it can lead us to a correct conception of this great idea.

Our soul has the capacity to enlarge and diminish an object until a lack of time or patience ends this laborious work. Is perhaps the ideal infinity of this capacity that which we imagine as immeasurability? Not at all! Just as the place where an action occurs is not the same as the acting force; so different is our capacity to enlarge from the space in which it works itself to exhaustion. This capacity is limited, but the limitless seems to be our soul itself.

The thought of immeasurability is thus neither a negation, nor an accumulation of known quantities, nor the capacity to enlarge infinitely, but the actual perception <note>German: *Vorstellung*. In this context, it refers to a mental representation or an internal "seeing" of a concept.</note> of an endless extension that our soul feels within itself.

Infinite progression of numbers is one of those truths that cannot be proven, in that they need no proof: it reveals the reality of an infinity, and this is the inner, essential secret of immeasurability.

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>Infinity.</header>
<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: fatigue.</meta>

When our imagination feels that it can string together the greatest measure it knows, without hope of reaching boundaries, upon a ground lying within it that it cannot conceive of as absent; then it must obtain the same conviction of immeasurability that the understanding has of the infinity of numbers.

Now when the soul applies these two convictions to the sum total of all things, it will confess that it perceives neither cause nor possibility for how the limited could encompass Everything, and must finally recognize that there exists a Being <note>The author uses *Wesen* (Being), likely pointing toward a Divine presence that transcends physical limits.</note> which stands in contradiction to a scale and defined space; for scale and immeasurability contradict each other: a scale is an arbitrary division without fracture of a recognized diameter; but since immeasurability has no diameter, its scale is an impossible thing.

### Eternity.

Guided by the image of immeasurability, we can more easily think our way into the higher nature of eternity. Of this, we have a less pictorial but very convincing feeling, in that we string together millions of millennia forward and backward without finding any cause to stand still other than fatigue.

<summary>The author argues that immeasurability is a real, internal perception of the soul rather than a mere lack of limits, using the infinity of numbers as a parallel. This concept is then used as a bridge to understand the nature of a boundless Being and the concept of eternity.</summary>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Infinity.</header>
<meta>catchword: in-</meta>

fatigue. From this mental image of eternity, we can cut out periods of time, however large we may imagine them, and unspeakable numbers of centuries become but a moment beside it.

We usually strive to soar up to the thought of eternity through the greatest possible expansion of time: yet time is as little of the nature of eternity as space is of the nature of immeasurability. Both relate to each other just as contradictorily as that which is essentially determined relates to that which is essentially undeterminable; and we err in that we do not distinguish purely enough between the **determined**, the **undetermined**, and the **impossibly determinable**.

There are determined and undetermined spaces and times, but the impossibility of being determined is the essence of eternity and immeasurability. Therefore, one must not think that enormous numbers of centuries could constitute a bit of eternity, or world systems a little piece of immeasurability; this would be just as absurd as if one wanted to make the smallest number out of many zeros, or the weakest light out of the thickest night. Nor may one permit oneself to attribute the impossibility of determination to time and space, and say:

<column-break/>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Infinity.</header>
<meta>catchword: -finite</meta>

infinite time is eternity, infinite space is immeasurability. This would be: to confuse words and concepts, to suppress two great thoughts stirring within us, to melt together the concepts of the determinable and the undeterminable and claim: that a thing can both be and not be at the same time.

From all this it follows: that even if eternity were a thought abstracted from time for many people, it is nevertheless not so by its nature, but rather that we have it from another side. It seems to be related to the idea of immeasurability, and also to lie in the divine nature of the soul.

Our capacity to pile up and enlarge time periods infinitely, and our incapacity to reach the depths of the Godhead through this piling and enlarging, generate in us the thought of the existence of an eternity. This thought appears less *figuratively* <note>German: *bildlich*. The author suggests that while we can visualize spatial "measures," time remains a more abstract, "darker" concept to the human mind.</note> in us than that of immeasurability, because the mental image of a period of time, which we carry around in the infinite abyss <note>original: *unendlichen Ungrund*. The term *Ungrund* is a significant philosophical and mystical concept, famously used by Jacob Böhme to describe the "groundless" or "abyssal" nature of the divine that precedes all being.</note>, is in itself dark and not nearly as sensory as that of great diameters that we string together in our inner, immeasurable extension.

This is why it is easier for us to link space rather than time with the thought of the infinite...

...infinitely large and small <note>The text begins with a fragment of the word "unendlich" (infinitely).</note>. But it seems remarkable to me that we have a clearer feeling for a thing that has no end than for one that has no beginning. We feel eternity better looking forward than backward; — perhaps a proof that we did indeed come into being, but will never cease to be.

Both a savage <note>original: "Wilder"; in the 18th-century context, this refers to people living in a "state of nature," often viewed by philosophers of the time as having more "natural" or uncorrupted instincts.</note> or a child are inclined to think that all things they have always seen around them will never end. The belief in an eternal duration seems natural to them; they would think of neither their own death nor the death of their fellow creatures if they had never seen a dead body and followed its destruction. The belief in the immortality of such animals, whose carcasses had never been found, would be very forgivable for savage peoples.

Before I conclude, I must correct one more thought concerning eternity. The nature of time and the chain of events have accustomed us to believe that the present must necessarily follow the past, and the future must follow the present. However, originally, the matter is quite different. The present must originally and essentially have been before the past; for how could it be past if it had not first been present?

Likewise, it is certain that the present alone makes the future real and, as it were, follows it. Whoever can truly think themselves into this true grammar will find that not only does the present alone possess reality, but also what absolute reality is; they will realize that it is presence without anything past: — eternal presence; they will learn to comprehend that only the Universal Being <note>original: "Allwesen"</note>, Universal Existence <note>original: "Allseyn"</note>, and general reality are eternal, and that eternity is nothing but pure presence. 1)

But how can this presence, which is supposed to constitute the essence of eternity, be reconciled with the manifold things that presumably must arise and vanish within it — things which, in relation to each other, can be called past and future?

1) It is remarkable that in the Hebrew language, which seems closest to the original language, only the past and future tenses can be expressed, but not the present. <note>The author refers to the Hebrew "aspect" system (perfect and imperfect), which early linguists often interpreted as lacking a true present tense.</note> Even more remarkable is the expression of the Psalmist: *You are my son, today I have begotten you.* <note>original: "Du bist mein Sohn, heute habe ich dich gezeuget"; a quote from Psalm 2:7.</note> The past borders so swiftly on the future that the point of the present can hardly be grasped by thought. It seems as if the enjoyment of this idea belongs to the essence of eternity.

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Infinity.</header>

Although there is and must be a succession <term>succession: the sequential following of one thing after another</term> in eternity, I nevertheless believe that those beings who enjoy it perceive neither a past nor a future within it. I believe that these beings do not occupy themselves with memories and fears, but will instead concern themselves solely with sensations, because feeling is better than having felt, or wanting to feel. If this is so, then the future and the past in eternity remain a mere relation between things that do not think and know nothing of it. This relation is not a truth that can be known, because there is no one there who wishes to know it. Since the state of those who dwell in eternity must be happier and more perfect than ours—we who live in time—and since present sensations are better than future or past ones, and actual enjoyment makes one happier than hope and memory, or remorse and fear: so we can surmise that beings who are placed in the most perfect state will likely content themselves with an ever-changing enjoyment, with a successive "present-ness" <note>original: "Gegenwärtigkeit"; the state of being perpetually in the present moment</note> of countless diversities, without ruminating on past enjoyments as we do, or wearing out future ones in the imagination, and even more, without occupying themselves with the knowledge of their successive order.

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>Infinity.</header>

occupying [themselves]. Therefore, and in this sense, I maintain that eternity and its enjoyment is nothing but "present-ness," even if infinite successions should occur within it.

It is evident that eternity must have existed before time, and that it will exist after time. It is perceptible that immensity <note>original: "Unermeßlichkeit"; literally "un-measurable-ness," often used as a synonym for spatial infinity.</note> encompasses all spaces; consequently, eternity and immensity were necessary for the emergence of time and space. The former are necessary, immutable, essential, and independent foundations;—time and space, however, are accidental, transient, and arbitrary modifications of them. We proceed more correctly, therefore, if we derive time and space from eternity and infinity, rather than if we proceed the other way around.

# Time and Space.

To understand the nature of eternity and immensity even more closely, we wish to examine the two outgrowths of these two great primal essences—Time and Space.

### What is Space?

One commonly associates two categories of concepts with this word. One regards space either:

As a possibility for movement, and therein lies the senseless idea of empty space <term>empty space: often referred to as a vacuum, which many 18th-century thinkers found philosophically impossible</term> as a foundation; for it is thought that the thinnest matter, as well as the universe itself, must be surrounded by a "nothing" in order to be able to move. I refer here to what I shall say in the following regarding nothingness, force, and a divine fullness; and I flatter myself that in the pressure and counter-pressure of elastic forces, and in the necessity that the infinite expandability of the Universal Being—hindered by nothing—must create its own infinite space, one will find a more rational possibility for movement than in the absurd

<column-break/>

<header>Time and Space.</header>
<page-num>15</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Sub-</meta>

assertion of a local, all-encompassing nothingness.

Or, one regards space merely as an enclosure; and then one imagines it like a hollow mold. One believes, for example, that a plaster mold is the most perfect idea one can form of a space. Certainly, the shaped image has its space within this form, but along with it, many thousands of dust motes, insects, air, and particles of light and fire also have their spaces within the same.

If one wishes to form a pure and correct concept of space, one must view it: as a separation of expandable forces and as an attachment of the same to impenetrable circles; space is the boundary of the infinite against the finite—a relationship between flexibility <note>original: "Nachgiebigkeit"; the quality of yielding or compliance in a physical sense</note> and impenetrability—the exact form of a figure.

From this it becomes clear that space is relative neither to our senses (if the body that occupies it can exist without us), nor to other bodies beside it; for the universe has no bodies beside itself, and between the space of two bodies there is always an elastic substance, or force, which fills it, and through its flexible contraction makes room for the bodies. Space is always relative to forces or elastic

### What is Time?

It is certainly not a composite of years; for years are the measure, and the measure is not the thing itself. One could also measure time in another way. Since determination is its nature, it certainly has its essential measure; but we do not know it, because we do not know its extent. We only measure parts of time with our assumed standard. 2)

---

<margin>
2) The distance between recurrences is the measure of time. It was likely first invented upon the second recurrence of a desired or feared thing. Long or short expectations are the first elements of a measure of time.
</margin>

Let us imagine a person who knows no hours and suffers a painful attack every hour. At the second, he fears a third, and at the third, he compares the similarity of the distance of the second from the first with the distance of the third from the second. If he finds such a similarity again at the fourth, he develops a desire to determine this duration more precisely, and then he will strive to seek out a thing outside himself with which he can liken and compare this duration.

<column-break/>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>Time and Space.</header>
<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: Time</meta>

But is there something distinct from the measure of time that can be called "time" and is not merely relative to us? Certainly: not only every event, but also every thing has its own time, which is not at all relative to us if the thing can exist without us. It has, for itself, a beginning and an end. What lies between the two, I call **duration**, and I believe it to be the essence of time.

One might perhaps say: duration is duration and not time; yet how does one determine each duration? Is it not through the measure of time, and can one measure anything other than duration with this measure? It follows from this that the measure of time would have to cease being a measure of time if duration and time were not one and the same.

The duration of each thing is its time, and the duration of the sum total <note>original: "Inbegriffs"</note> of all finite things is *the* Time.

Without duration, beginning and end would be together—nothing past, nothing future, and consequently no time, but purely the present or eternity.

If duration is only that which separates beginning and end, then it is undeniably a hindrance <term>hindrance: here the author uses "hindrance" and "resistance" (Widerstand) to describe a metaphysical force that prevents all moments from occurring simultaneously</term> so that beginning and end do not flow together, which cannot be thought of without a resistance.—Resistance, therefore, is that which [separates] time...

## How did Time and Space originate?

We have seen that time could not have originated otherwise than within eternity; further, that the nature of time is a duration, but the nature of duration is a resistance: and that thus, time must have been produced by a resistance within eternity.

Now the question arises: what resisted an almighty God, and what could have been able to resist Him?

We know only a single thing of which He is not the author, which conflicts with His perfection, which contradicts order, truth, and everything good—and this thing is—**the Evil**.

Only this [Evil], through its opposing, self-willed direction, could and had to interrupt and halt the sole operation of the <term>primal force</term> <note>original: "Urkraft". This refers to the fundamental, creative energy of God.</note> of all good. Here, a great epoch arose within eternity—a great event—something entirely new. A second <term>fundamental cause</term> <note>original: "Grundursache"</note> emerged, which, unlike the first fundamental cause of all good—

# How did Time and Space originate?

its emergence is the starting point of a time. Therefore, time proves the existence of evil, and the latter explains to us the essence of time.

The same can be said of space, which is related to time:

If a circumference constitutes the essence of a space, and a circumference is the only possible separation within the immeasurable; then the first separation brought forth the first space. Now, there can surely be no more probable cause for why God would have separated forces out of and from Himself than their resistance; thus, the first resistance of a force caused the first separation, the first figure—the first space. Consequently, space proves the reality of evil, and evil explains the nature of space.

Time and space are perhaps the two greatest means which God has employed in His masterpiece of creation to combat and remedy the resulting disorder without using violence. Thus, we see in mechanics that force is saved through time, and wisdom resembles the slow lever that would overcome the pressure of a world if one could set it up within infinite space. <note>The author is using an analogy from physics: just as a lever allows a person to move a heavy weight by spreading the effort over a longer distance and time, God uses the "long duration" of Time and the "reach" of Space to gradually overcome the "weight" of Evil without resorting to immediate, destructive force.</note>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

## Origin of Evil from Free Will.

What God worked when the first resistance expressed itself, and what the latter intended to counteract, are secrets that remain hidden from our reflection. However, we can see that the force that dared to resist God must have been a powerful being. Was it a tool of God, prepared by Him for thinking and willing, made from forces that are not conscious of themselves; or was it an offspring <note>original: "Ausgeburt". While this can mean "monstrosity" in modern German, in this philosophical context it refers to something birthed or emanated directly from God’s own substance.</note> of God, proceeded from His essence? This we do not know either: but we can assume as very probable that such a mighty spirit must have possessed the noblest and most distinguished qualities that we feel within ourselves, in a much stronger and higher degree. But what is the noblest and most powerful thing in a thinking being? Presumably the power that effects its actions:—the **will**; and in what does the power of the will consist? In **freedom**. Here, reason leads us to the well-known tradition: that evil arose through the misuse of a free will.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil. Having established in the previous section that Time and Space are divine "remedies" or structural frameworks created to contain the disorder caused by Evil, the author now turns to the metaphysical mechanism that allowed Evil to exist in the first place: Free Will.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

<header>Origin of Evil, etc.</header>
<meta>catchword: Has</meta>

Since everything we know is a series of consequences, and all our decisions have a motive—either present or unknown to us due to its distance—we cannot form a perfectly clear concept of a free will. However, if it is truly to be free, it seems certain to me that it cannot be bound by any cause. Now, a thing must be either a cause or a consequence; therefore, free will is, by its nature, a cause. It alone brings forth consequences without being a consequence itself. It alone establishes a ground; it is a source and a creator. Therefore, a free will in the strictest sense was necessary to bring forth a completely new thing, and to create the primary cause of such consequences which we might suspect are contrary to the will of the Best Being <note>The "Best Being" refers to God</note>. This free will was a creative power which God had given to that mighty spirit of whom we speak <note>The author likely refers to the figure of Lucifer or the first fallen intelligence</note>, either as His instrument or as His offspring; yet certainly for a use other than that to which the unpredictable nature of freedom misled him.

---

<page-num>25</page-num>

### Has God Foreseen the Origin of Evil?

Doubters are inclined to say: God knew of the emergence of evil and was unable to prevent it, or He knew of it and did not want to prevent it, or He did not know of it. In the first case, He is not omnipotent; in the second, He is not good; in the third, He is not omniscient. <note>This is a variation of the "Epicurean Trilemma," a classic philosophical problem regarding the existence of evil in a world created by an all-powerful, all-knowing, and perfectly good God.</note>

Suppose God had foreseen the possibility of evil and yet created free will; one could easily assume that He required free will in His instruments, or that such freedom was a necessary part of offspring emerging from His own essence. One might suspect that He knew of means to improve the consequences of a disorder that He fully understood, and to secure the freedom of the will against any further misuse. However, I believe I can prove that God did not foresee the emergence of evil.

If we restrict free will to a simple "Yes" or "No," it certainly seems as if God must have realized that a being to whom He wished to give the power to deny would also, at some point, say "No"...

<summary>The author defines Free Will as a unique "primary cause" that acts without being a consequence of something else, arguing that God granted this creative power to a high spirit who then misused it. He begins to address the theological problem of whether an omniscient God could have foreseen the emergence of Evil, suggesting he will prove that God did not foresee it.</summary>

<keywords>free will, cause and effect, origin of evil, divine foreknowledge, omnipotence, theodicy, creative power</keywords>

Furthermore, a purely affirming or denying will would not have been free; for it would have been bound to a reason that chooses the best. 1)

---

1) We are accustomed to regarding "free will" and "free choice" as equivalent terms. Free choice <note>original: "freie Wahl"</note> is an absurdity; it would always have to be irrational if it were truly free; and if it chooses what reason deems best, then it is no longer free. Thus, free will <note>original: "freier Wille"</note> is something entirely different. It does not want the "best," but rather the "new"; it creates thoughts, and these must also be free—of an entirely different kind than our own, which never depend on us but are stirred within us through the senses or through memories.

From the confused concepts we have of free choice and free will, there has arisen that error, equipped with a veneer of truth, in our school metaphysics <note>A reference to "Scholasticism" or the academic philosophy taught in 18th-century universities, such as the systems of Leibniz or Wolff</note>: that God has no free choice because He must always choose the best. — No choice is free, and God never chooses! He creates infinite varieties, each the best of its kind.

<column-break/>

<header>foreseen?</header>
<meta>catchword: thus</meta>

However, free will must be conceived in an entirely different way. It is a creative power, and it creates causes along with the entire series of their consequences. Its nature did not consist in doing good or evil; rather, it could, through the power of its nature, bring forth the source of evil. It, and not God, provided the possibility for it. 2) Free will is

---

-> How can one imagine a God who invents a multitude of bad things in order to compare them with better things—which He also invented—so that He might choose the latter? <-

2) The concern that God, as the author of a free will, might thereby appear as the ultimate cause of evil, arises from the confusion of the concepts of the "tool" and the "work." Would one correctly analyze a chain of causes and consequences by saying: the knife with which a murder was committed was the cause of it; the creator of the knife was the blacksmith; consequently, the blacksmith was the primary cause of this murder? This would certainly be a very false progression of logic. The true cause of the murder was undeniably the thought to commit it. We must trace this thought through all its motives, and the latter through their causes, and so we shall [be led] through a series of passions, errors, and follies...

3) To rectify the true concept of the genuine freedom of the will, it is necessary to soar to the dizzying heights where we must investigate: whether in a free being the will must exist before the thought, or the thought before the will? ...to have a great science <note>original: "Wissenschaft"; in this 18th-century context, it refers to a systematic body of knowledge or the discovery of a new field of thought</note>, or a powerful fact as its basis.

Here I can provide an even stronger and briefer proof that God could not have known the emergence of evil beforehand: Is the person who thinks of a thing for the first time not its inventor? Had God foreseen evil, He would have been the first to think of it—and consequently, its inventor. Such a monstrous foresight is therefore impossible, not only according to the nature of the matter but also according to the very essence of God.

Although I do not write for theologians, I nevertheless take the liberty of telling them: I do not see why omniscience should not be considered in proportion and similarity to omnipotence. The latter can do all things, except for evil; the former knows all things, except for the science of evil. Omniscience knows everything that omnipotence can do— —everything for which God has brought forth the primary causes—only not that of which He is not the author.

If we wish to occupy ourselves with expanding our concepts of omnipotence and omniscience, it is certainly more comforting and reasonable to assume: that omnipotence will make the evil good, and omniscience will be able to invent the means to do so. We see a conflict between good and evil;

<column-break/>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>foreseen?</header>

the victory cannot be in doubt. It is more beautiful and more appropriate to divine goodness if He improves and restores, rather than if He were to lose Himself in an eternal, fruitless revenge. 4)

4) Scripture uses the expression "from eternity to eternity." Since there is certainly only one eternity, this turn of phrase can mean nothing other than an immeasurable time—a time whose determination has not yet proceeded from God. Eternity seems to me the highest degree of perfection; how could evil, which is imperfection itself, be able to last eternally?

# Is there a real evil?

A puzzling, strange, and doubt-inducing spectacle is the mixture of good and evil, of order and disorder, of goodness and cruelty, which lies before our eyes: so violent a struggle between two opposing powers that entire nations have believed there to be a good and an evil God, both of equal power. On the other hand, long experience and careful observations have taught: that good arises from evil, that pain heals, decay creates new life, and that certain primary laws, unchangeably elevated above these adversities, can be disrupted by no disorder. This has led others, and especially the modern <term>philosophers</term><note>original: "Weltweisen," literally "world-wise ones," a common 18th-century German term for philosophers.</note>, to the thought: that there is no evil at all; that evil is relative, and everything is good in its own way. However, illness and death, passions and crimes, ignorance and folly are evils that one will hardly succeed in philosophizing out of our world.

It is not entirely wrong to say that evil is relative; but to what is it relative? To sentient

<column-break/>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>Is there a real evil?</header>
<sig>C</sig>
<meta>catchword: From</meta>

sentient beings. — But can anything more noble be thought of in nature than sentient beings? And are feelings that cause unhappiness not the worst and most important proofs that evil can give of its real existence? —

The whole of nature seems, composed of opposing forces, to move and develop itself through them. Whether opposing forces can descend from a single Creator, and indeed from the One God in whom no contradiction can be thought, and who perhaps can only act simply; this will hardly be demonstrable. It is more probable that these opposing forces come from the two primary sources of all possibilities, namely from the good and from the evil, and that they have been ordered toward a purpose by a higher power. This purpose, which guides everything evil toward the good, and the mighty goodness which forces the worst causes to produce the best consequences, are what confuse our <term>sophistry</term><note>original: "Spitzfündigkeit," referring to overly subtle or pedantic reasoning.</note> and nourish in us the opinion that everything is good in itself. What the Creator improves becomes good; but what he has to improve is evil, and we humans have certainly paid the price <note>original: "bezahlt"; used here idiomatically to mean that through our suffering, we have earned the right or the evidence to speak.</note> to say that our sufferings, which often plague us to the point of despair, are a real evil.

<page-num>34</page-num>

# On Perfectibility.

Here I must share my thoughts regarding the system of **perfectibility** <note>The idea that humans and nature have an inherent capacity for indefinite improvement.</note>, which is currently in fashion. Its main premise is as follows: **All beings proceed from the furthest stage of determined becoming and ascend the unceasing ladder of perfection.**

If one intends to claim by this sentence that beings are ennobled through a transition into higher **species** <term>Gattungen: often translated as "genera" or "kinds"; the author is arguing against the idea of one species changing into another</term>; then experience proves to us the contrary. No seed produces any kind other than its own, and all species are so firmly protected by physical laws that they cannot be confounded.

If it is said, on the other hand, that every being becomes more perfect through a slow progression within its own kind, then God must have begun with nothing but filth, poisonous plants, immobile animals, stupid devils, and villains, only to end with nothing but gold and diamonds, heavenly fruits, **cherubim**, and **seraphim** <note>The highest orders of angels in traditional theology.</note>.

But why should a God have begun as a **bungler** <note>original: "Stümper," meaning a clumsy or unskilled amateur.</note>, only to appear as a master in the end?

<meta>catchword: appear</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>On Perfectibility.</header>

appear? Perhaps because nature sometimes proceeds in this way? This very fact testifies to its decay and the influence of an opposing force.

The error of this system seems to me to lie in the fact that a false **ladder** <note>A reference to the "Great Chain of Being," a hierarchical structure of all matter and life.</note> is assumed instead of the true one. One descends from the concept of divine perfection down to the imperfect, without observing the infinite abyss that is fixed between God and his most perfect creatures; without considering that the lowest degree of perfection stands in contradiction to imperfection and can never border upon it; and finally, that not even the slightest perfection is "perfectible," because that which is perfect cannot increase, however lowly it may be in its kind. Perfectibility only takes place within the scales of imperfections.

One should say: God alone in His way is the Most Perfect. He could only bring forth species less perfect than His own. The varieties of the species and their individual details constitute the infinite diversity of all possible perfections. Each of these species was the best of its kind when it came from the hand of the Creator. They have all been corrupted and shall be improved in order to regain their former perfection.

<meta>catchword: This</meta>

Instead, he posits that all things were created perfect in their own kind and that current "improvement" is actually the restoration of a perfection lost through corruption.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded a critique of the "system of perfectibility," which suggests that all beings are slowly ascending a ladder toward perfection. The author mocked the idea that God would start as a "bungler" creating filth and villains only to finish with gold and angels, arguing instead that species are fixed by physical laws.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>On Perfectibility.</header>
<meta>catchword: On</meta>

This drive for improvement, which we perceive here and there, and the frequent application of evil toward good, have had to serve and assist in the establishment of the system of **perfectibility** <note>The Enlightenment-era theory that humanity and the universe are in a state of constant, progressive improvement.</note>. However, imagine two watchmakers: the first, who crafts the very worst watch and is always tinkering with it <note>original: "künstelt," implying fussy or amateurish work</note>; and the second, who delivers a masterpiece of a watch, and, because a child has damaged it, undertakes its complete restoration. Will one not regard the latter as the greater master, and view the former as merely a student who wishes to learn his trade upon this watch?

If the world were to progress successively toward perfection, then it must have been God's intention to have a perfect world; but why then did He have to begin it with so little perfection? And when will He achieve His purpose?

We see clearly here that through this system, people have sought to find something that might fill eternity—an eternal work of God. Yet the concept of "lesser perfection" is too small to allow itself to be stretched backward into an eternity without beginning; and looking forward, God could never reach His goal of creating a perfect world if its perfectibility were infinite.

---

<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: one</meta>

# On the Freedom of the Human Will.

We notice a certain and definite immutability not only in the course of the celestial bodies, in the seasons, in the diversity of the primary species, in the development of seeds, in the properties of matter, and generally in all physical laws; but also in our sensations and in the form of our **rational deductions** <term>Vernunftschlüsse: the logical steps the mind must take to reach a conclusion; the author suggests these are "hard-wired" and not free.</term>. All of this is determined, immutable, and bound: nothing seems to be free except for the resolution of thinking beings. But is it truly so? Is their freedom not an illusion? How can one imagine a rational resolution without some cause, and are we masters of that cause? Is a very distant motive any less compelling if we do not perceive it?

One should reflect on what I have already said above regarding free will; one should consider the mechanical necessity of a logical conclusion; one should read: *Alexander von Joch on Punishments and Rewards according to Turkish Laws* <note>Alexander von Joch was a pseudonym for the philosopher and theologian Christian August Crusius (1715–1775), who wrote extensively on the limits of human will and divine necessity.</note>, and

<header>On Freedom</header>

I wish only to cite a single observation from this thorough book <note>The author is likely referencing a specific Enlightenment text, potentially Paul-Henri Thiry, Baron d'Holbach’s "The System of Nature," which was a foundational text for 18th-century determinism.</note>, to reassure those who fear an overthrow of morality from this doctrine: "There would be no morality at all if the will were free. Punishments and rewards, bad and good consequences, drive all our moral decisions: If we could choose freely, then punishments and rewards must have no influence on our choice; and how would we conduct ourselves then?" We truly do not need a free will. It would rather be harmful to us and bring everything into disorder.

Yet punishments and rewards are not the only counterweights to our passions. We often act in secret, without regard for punishments or rewards.

Conscience and **love for humanity** <note>original: "Menschenliebe," often translated as philanthropy, but here referring to a natural, empathetic bond with others.</note> are two great and perhaps our only true moral driving forces. The person who performs the most beautiful deeds out of self-interest does not act well, but rather rationally: Only he is virtuous who follows his conscience, and he alone is good who is moved by a feeling of love. How noble is the man whom the happiness of others makes happy, and

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>of the human will.</header>
<sig>C 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: ac-</meta>

and how divine is the person who is willing to suffer for the well-being of humanity!

But both, love for humanity and conscience, are also not free.

The feeling of love for humanity is generated in one’s temperament, formed by upbringing, and practiced through circumstances; three things that do not depend on us.

We can master our conscience even less. — Conscience is the mental representation of a moral law with its consequences, which we set against a motive <note>original: "Bewegungsgrund," literally a "ground for movement."</note> lying within or outside of us. Neither this latter—for no motive is free—depends on our discretion <note>original: "Willkühr," the power of choosing at will.</note>, nor does the representation of the moral law. This is different for every person, because the laws are more or less known, present, agreeable, and practicable to each. If all people had the same intellectual powers, the same temperament, experience, and upbringing, if they could be in the exact same situation, then they would all have the same conscience. However, the slightest circumstance changes, strengthens, weakens, illuminates, or obscures their conscience, and no circumstance depends on them. We see, therefore, that we—both in regard to the state in which our conscience finds itself and in its influence on our ac- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "actions" on the next page.</note>

Yet the emotion that accompanies conscience, and perhaps constitutes its inner essence, is worthy of consideration. I speak of the sadness or remorse following a wicked deed; but even more of the joyful satisfaction that tends to follow a good action. This inner echo might well indicate the state in which the goodness or corruption of each soul finds itself; for it does not always seem to arise from the representation of good or bad consequences. One could, of course, attribute the anxiety attached to a hidden crime to a dark foreboding of possible punishment: but from where comes the pleasure that follows a quiet good deed—a pleasure that is greater the less the good deed expects a reward? From where the self-satisfaction felt by the highway robber when he has restrained his cruelty? The pleasure of benevolence, which every person feels in varying degrees, I consider to be a stirring of our **imprisoned free will** <note>The author uses the metaphor of "imprisoned" (eingekerkert) free will to suggest that while our actions are determined by circumstances, a latent spark of moral agency remains within the soul.</note> and the measure of its lesser corruption. I say that this emotion is a trace of free will because it is worked not by the ordinary coercive means of punishments and re-

To make the reality of an irresistible destiny even more striking, I wish to cite two opposing examples that will practically present the truths mentioned above:

The son of a highway robber is born in a murderer's den; he learns the trade of his parents with the same innocent confidence with which another child pursues the trade of his father. Raised for war against civil society, he knows only the laws of his small gang, which are based on **natural right** <note>original: "Naturrecht." In this context, it refers to a primitive or "law of the jungle" justice used by those outside of formal civilization.</note>. In the narrow circle of his companions, he can be honest, sympathetic, even heroic; for **Roman virtues** <note>The author refers to "Roman virtues" (strength, loyalty, and martial honor) to show that even a criminal can possess "noble" qualities within his own social framework.</note> are occasionally appropriate for him. The common utility, the revenge sworn for the loss of his friends, and his own danger excuse all his actions to him. the best possible highway robber [could be made] from him. If, on the other hand, his temperament had been violent, inflexible, and choleric <note>original: "gallfüchtig," referring to the ancient medical theory of "yellow bile" which was thought to cause a hot-tempered or irritable disposition.</note>, he would have practiced nothing but murder, and the highest degree of cruelty would have become the pinnacle of his development. In both cases, ignorance, preconceived notions, habit, and association with his own kind make it impossible for him to recognize his injustice against humanity, to agree with our laws, and to condemn his own life. Should a ray of religion finally penetrate his soul, or a wish to live more peacefully arise, the powerful feeling of self-preservation and the fear of punishment—or at least of contempt—will always prevent him from surrendering himself to the authorities for better or worse. For if he were to accept a reward for betraying his own people, he would not be improved; rather, the lowest vice would then seethe in his soul alongside the terrible duties of his previous association, leaving him without even the pure feeling of having done his duty—a comfort that even a robber can possess.

Another person is born to excellent parents; his temperament is perfectly moderate, his intellect clear and powerful;

<meta>catchword: a</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>of the human will.</header>

a rational religion ennobles the moral principles instilled in him; in his education, the difficult "middle way" is struck: applying these principles imperceptibly and merely guiding his own natural inclination. Virtuous friends shape him even more lastingly in his second education <note>The author likely refers to the "social education" one receives from peers and society after leaving the parental home.</note>; special opportunities practice him in doing good; gratitude and friendly applause further stimulate his love for humanity, and the latter becomes his sole inclination amidst the silence of all passions. The course of his life never leads him to the crossroads of virtue and vice, and so, protected by all circumstances, he wanders with light steps—without effort or struggle—through the human career in a state of **necessary irreproachability.** <note>The term "nothwendigen Unsträflichkeit" suggests that his goodness is not a result of free choice, but a "necessary" outcome of his perfect environment and nature.</note>

This privileged man of virtue and that born highway robber will likely feel the consequences of their actions before and after death and will have different fates. Both had to exist because they existed; they had to act as they acted because they acted so. But how did they, as thinking, feeling, self-aware beings, come to such different destinies? What had the spirit of the one done to become a highway robber,

## On Providence.
<note>Providence refers to the protective care and preparation of God or nature for the future of humanity.</note>

If free-thinking and willing beings stirred up a resistance in eternity, and through that very resistance brought forth time and invented the knowledge of evil <note>original: "Wissenschaft des Bösen"; here meaning an intimate awareness or "science" of wrongdoing</note>; then other such beings were possible who, through error or seduction, could cling to this invented evil. If we are not the first [of these beings], then we are presumably and probably the last. I assume, therefore, that we were all present at the Fall <note>The "Fall" refers to the original descent of humanity from a state of grace or perfection into a world of sin and suffering</note> that caused our incarnation <note>original: "Menschwerdung"; the process of a soul taking on human form</note>; that we all took part in it, some more, others less; and that the perfection of our being suffered in varying degrees because of it. 1)

In a rebellion, there are ringleaders, recruiters, and advisors who are often worse than their leader. The diversity of intentions multiplies the transgressions of the rebels. Many follow along out of lightheartedness, lack of understanding, or weakness; others resist and endeavor to prevent the matter, but are carried away or forced.

Presumably, things occurred in the same way during the Fall of the Spirits <note>This refers to the "Pre-existence of Souls," a theory that human souls existed as spirits before birth and fell from grace in a celestial rebellion</note>, and presumably we corrupted ourselves in very different measures through the nature of our participation. Improvement must remain the intention of the merciful God, just as the general restoration of order was the primary purpose of His perfection. But how can moral beings improve themselves if repentance does not come first? Repentance, however, can only be brought about through the sensation of unhappy consequences. If we have corrupted ourselves in different degrees, then our improvement must be [achieved] through different magnitudes

---

[of...] our current nature would be unbearable; perhaps this knowledge would presuppose others that we cannot or may not have in our material state. For both, the wisdom and goodness of God has provided by withdrawing from us a memory that would have been all too painful or harmful to us.

<meta>catchword: magnitudes</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>On Providence.</header>

magnitudes and types of repentance; these presuppose painful sensations whose strength and nature must be appropriate to the manifold types of corruption. God therefore required a specific, gathered measure of painful consequences in order to awaken repentance and bring about improvement. Yet it seems inconceivable and absurd that He, the source and author of all good, should have invented torment and misfortune. On the contrary, it is evident that the will of God always has the happiness of His creatures as its purpose; that every departure from His will removes one from this purpose in equal proportion; and that the variety of transgressed laws must likewise produce different, misery-inducing consequences.

We see, therefore, how a specific measure of painful means—which God uses to awaken repentance—can be gathered without Him being the author or inventor of these torments; how He rather seeks to improve evil through the very effect of the evil itself. This proportional arrangement I call the **Justice of God**, because I can find no other concept for this expression that would be more compatible with divine goodness.

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of Providence, specifically addressing how divine justice differs from the limitations of human justice, and how our physical existence serves as a protective barrier for the soul.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>On Providence.</header>
<meta>catchword: built</meta>

Our justice would like to improve things, but it cannot. It does not mend the evil consequences of a deed, for it cannot make them undone; nor does it improve the perpetrator, whom it destroys or imprisons; nor the spectators, whom it seeks to bribe through fear or hope. It tortures the repentant wrongdoer just as it does the hardened one, and punishes him not for his own sake, but out of necessary precaution for others. It is evident how imperfect and often cruel such a justice is. But should one think of divine justice in a similar way? Is His goodness not just enough if the transgression of His laws—which are intended to make us happy—punishes itself? And is His justice too kind if He prefers to eradicate the evil rather than the one who has become evil—if He destroys the sickness and not the sick person? If He annihilates all consequences of evil, creates and accepts repentance, and if He improves things so completely that punishments and rewards can cease as soon as everything has become good again?

After everything I have said, I imagine the course of divine Providence as follows: When God devised the material sphere in which He joined good and evil together in conflict, His omniscience had overseen everything that His omnipotence could do. He built
