In this great, wonderful prison we live now, and we view it through the openings of a second dungeon, of which each of us has our own. <note>The author is likely using "second dungeon" as a metaphor for the human body, which houses the soul.</note> These openings are our senses, which are bound to the properties of bodies; the perceptions of these are handed over to our intellect for its sole use; they guide it, and it can produce no other thought from within itself: for the simple essence of things is unknowable to us; it is kept in forms that protect it from all misuse. Through this mysterious veil, the original, direct influence of evil upon our will is held back and diverted; it is weakened by being divided into various passions and acts, as it were, merely figuratively and through images. Only to this extent is it permitted for thinking beings, because of their original freedom, to resist the moral laws of God; since all others, without contradiction, [obey] <note>The word is cut off by the catchword "o-" (obey/gehorchen).</note> their Creator—

The moment of a person's conception determines all his actions within a pre-considered period of time. What we suffer and will suffer is therefore not a sterile revenge that takes pleasure in our torture, but rather the only possible healing means that the Goodness of God employs to show us the consequences of evil in their full extent, to prove the happy intention of divine laws, and to test our will through repentance; It will likely not grant the will its freedom again until it has been secured forever against all abuse of that freedom through a perfect transformation <note>German: *Umwendung*, implying a total turning around or conversion of the soul's orientation</note>.

But since this doctrine is built entirely upon the happy and unhappy consequences of our actions in this and in a future life, it rests, like all moral institutions, on **punishments and rewards**; only these generally compel us toward the good because we are not free; and even assuming we were convinced that none of our actions depend on us, this opinion would still not prevent the irresistible impression that the conviction of good and bad consequences makes on fear and hope; on the contrary, the more we see their utility, the less we will doubt their inevitable outcome.

In all sensitive souls, the thought of punishment must excite a timidity that stands in opposition to love; theologians may always call it a "childlike fear." I never loved my father when he gave me the rod; and when I was extremely afraid of him, my wish was that he did not exist at all. This is the path that the mysterious perfection of human improvement will presumably take.

How much the desired moment, which separates the highest misfortune from the greatest bliss, can be delayed and made more difficult by the presentation of eternal punishment, can therefore be easily understood, because such a presentation increases despair and makes love for God impossible: on the other hand, a more correct insight into the justice and goodness of God presumably belongs to the happier fate of less corrupted beings; in that they will arrive more quickly at the confidence and love necessary for them.

Finally, one will object that, if everything is irrevocably determined, then prayer is useless. An author whose name escapes me says: "certainly God does not need prayer, but humans need it." — — At the very least, it serves as an exercise to develop our trust in God. Yet I do not find it impossible that things could be arranged in such a way that temporal rewards—for example, for oneself and others—[could follow] the heartfelt trust of a prayer.

### Nothing and Everything.

No property seems more appropriate to "Nothing" than the property: not to be. Nothing is the negation of everything that exists—a summation of the non-being of all things; and how can one assert the *being* of non-being?

Negation always presupposes the thing that is being negated: Therefore, the concept of "Everything" <term>Everything: here referring to "The All" (*das All*), the totality of existence or the universe</term> had to be present before the thought of absolute Nothingness could even become possible. Consequently, this thought presupposes what it denies, and is a monster <note>The author uses "monster" (German: *Ungeheuer*) in the sense of a logical absurdity or a conceptual impossibility that violates the laws of reason.</note> composed of two contradictions.

But how could this almost unthinkable and yet so trivial idea have arisen?

It is partial negations that have served to assemble a general but impossible negation. One felt that no concept could be formed of a thing without a beginning; one realized that a beginning must have had a primary cause <term>primary cause: or "first cause" (*Grundursache*), the ultimate origin or principle from which all other things follow</term>, and from this arose the effort to find a thing...

<summary>This page contains the table of contents for the "Cosmological Essays" and begins a philosophical argument asserting that "Nothingness" is a logical impossibility because it presupposes the existence of "Everything" in order to deny it.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, nothingness, negation, primary cause, the All, logic, table of contents</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into "Nothing and Everything." The previous page ended with the observation that humans require a "primary cause" for the beginning of things.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
->Nothing and Everything.<-
<meta>catchword: would,</meta>

to make worthy, to which one could attribute the incomprehensible but necessary property of eternity. It seemed easier, for that reason, to assign it to Nothing rather than to the **All-Being** <term>All-Being: *Allwesen*, a philosophical concept referring to the totality of all existence or a supreme being that encompasses everything</term>, because all things that we know have had a beginning, and we are accustomed to imagining the "All" as a mere accumulation of these things. Therefore, from individual negations of all these things, one assembled the idea of an **absolute Nothing** and said: Before the beginning of all things was Nothing, and this Nothing was eternal.

Just as our eye is calmed by total darkness after it has long and vainly struggled to recognize an object in the twilight; even so did we seek to calm our understanding by covering the thought of eternity—which exceeds human reason—with the irrational idea of a general Nothingness.

But even through the arbitrary acceptance of this idea, we have still found no explanation for the origin of all things, which was, after all, our only motive for making such a claim; for the negation of all things cannot possibly be conceived as a *cause* of their emergence.

Furthermore, it should be noted that, even if Nothing had been eternal before the beginning of all things,

<column-break/>

<page-num>61</page-num>
->Nothing and Everything.<-
<meta>catchword: Everything</meta>

would, then it must have ceased to be through the emergence of all things. Its essence and its eternity would have been destroyed by it, and destruction would surely be the most unbecoming accident <note>The author uses "accident" (*Zufall*) in the classical philosophical sense: a non-essential quality or an event that happens to a substance.</note> that could befall an eternal being.

**Everything** is the original thought which gave rise to the empty word "Nothing." Since "Everything"—this unconstrained whole—contains all possible things and realities within itself, then "Nothing" would also have to be merely a part of that "Everything," and consequently could not have existed before it, if it were to have its own essence or were to be anything at all. But if Nothing is without essence, without reality—not even a *real* nothing—what is it then?

Easier and more comfortable for the human understanding is the sublime and satisfying thought of an **All-Being**, through which we replace everything that is missing due to our ignorance and limitation.

He posits that "Everything" (the All) is the primary reality and that an "All-Being" is a more rational explanation for existence.</summary>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry from pages 60-61. While the previous pages critiqued the irrational idea that "Nothingness" was the eternal precursor to existence, this section begins a positive argument for the eternity of the "All" or the "All-Being."</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

## Everything is Eternal.

**T**he **All** excludes the concept of anything else; consequently, nothing exists outside of it from which it could have arisen—no cause, no impulse, no origin. Still less can we conceive of anything outside of it through which it could cease to be, or be destroyed. Thus, there is one thing of which we recognize that it can impossibly have a beginning or an end.

We err in the mental image we create for ourselves regarding the incomprehensibility of an eternal existence. It is not eternity, but its opposite—beginning and end—that are truly incomprehensible.

Arising without a basis, and ceasing without a cause, are contrary to reason; therefore, the eternal existence of the **All-Being** <term>All-Being: *Allwesen*, a philosophical term for the totality of existence or a supreme being that encompasses all things</term> is a matter of which the opposite cannot be thought at all, and its incomprehensibility lies in its all-too-simple truth: for, once we have understood that the All cannot possibly have a beginning or an end, we have understood its eternity; just as we cannot form a clearer explanation for the beginning or the end of a thing

<meta>catchword: thing</meta>

<column-break/>

<header>Everything is Eternal.</header>
<page-num>63</page-num>

thing than when we have realized that it can have no cause for arising or passing away.

The most fitting image of the All-Being is the number **One**. It has no origin, for before it there is no number; yet it exists, and from it arise numbers and fractions unto infinity.

In this way, universal reality is eternal. Being is without beginning, without end. All modes of Being are fractions of its unity, and the multitude of these modes is infinite like the numbers.

---

<header>God.</header>

### God.

The All-Being <term>All-Being: *Allwesen*, a philosophical term for the totality of existence or a supreme being that encompasses all things</term>, this epitome of everything we know and do not know, must—if it is to be Everything—leave nothing behind over which it is not exalted: eternity and immensity can only be parts and attributes of its essence.

But could one not attribute eternity and immensity to the accumulation or the succession of all things?

If all things had arisen one after another, then one of them would have to be the first—that is, it would have existed before all others without a beginning—and what thing could claim such an immense privilege?

— For every major genus of beings, one would need to seek a particular beginning, or else the thing without a beginning must have possessed the properties of all things; which always results in a diminished conception of a deity.

If all things have existed eternally all at once, I cannot see how things—which individually cannot be conceived of without limits or a beginning—could lead to the thought of eternity and im-

<column-break/>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>God.</header>
<sig>E</sig>

mensity, merely because I accumulate them. It seems impossible to me that parts can constitute a whole whose nature essentially contradicts their own. For an immensity consisting of nothing but divided things is quite absurd, because it would be composed of nothing but boundaries.

Likewise, eternity cannot possibly be attributed to composite beings; for their parts would have had to exist before the composition and constitute a simple being, which again leads back to the idea of an All-Being.

The accumulated arrangement of individual things aligns much more perfectly with the concept of space, and their succession with the concept of time. Only the All-Being, by virtue of its independent, unlimited movement, can be connected to the idea of immensity 1) and by virtue of its All-ness <note>original: *Allheit*</note>, which excludes everything else through which it could have a beginning or an end, 2) can be connected with the idea of eternity.

1) See below in the article: Movement.
2) See in the article: Everything is Eternal.

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>God.</header>

We see, therefore, that the All-Being <term>All-Being: *Allwesen*, the author’s term for a supreme, all-encompassing reality</term> is to be regarded neither as a mere heap nor as a countless series of individual things, but as an extension that is undivided by its very nature, which encompasses all things that have arisen within it, and must be the cause of all beings, all laws, and all forces, because none of these can be conceived of without a beginning or without limits. Why should we not now attribute to a being—which we have recognized as eternal and unlimited—all other perfections that we can possibly imagine, especially since we must seek in it the ground of all possible beings? Such a primal cause must surely contain within itself the totality of wisdom, power, perfection, and goodness, in order to be able to impart all lesser degrees of these qualities—and this being I call **God**.

I consider all proofs of the existence of God derived from reason to be insufficient, but also unnecessary: for the idea of a God is more indispensable to our understanding than a demonstration of His existence. Yet I believe that the latter can best be proven from the certainty we have of our own existence.

It is almost indisputable that inanimate things have no consciousness, and that those beings who enjoy this

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>God.</header>
<sig>E 2</sig>

invaluable privilege are more perfect than the former. Since the All-Being, whose eternal existence we have proven in the preceding article, cannot be more imperfect than we—who are at most only parts of it—and since the source of all selfhood <term>selfhood: *Ichheit*, the quality of being an "I" or a conscious subject</term> must lie within it (because it could hardly give away what it does not itself possess), it is highly probable that its selfhood relates to our own, and all its moral forces to ours, just as immeasurably as the sum of all worlds relates to our bodies. For we cannot conclude anything about God except through analogy from ourselves—we create Him in our image, and err the less if He has created us in His. 3)

## Concerning the First Object of the Idea.

Even the deep-thinking Plato could conceive of nothing more primordial than the Idea of God. In it, he said, lay the foundation of the world, just as the thought of a plan in the soul of an architect was the beginning of the execution of his building. For a long time, later metaphysics maintained this opinion without question. Finally, Spinoza came and proved that no idea can exist without an object, and that consequently an object must have existed before the first idea.

I do not permit myself to investigate with either the divine Plato or the undivine <note>original: *ungöttlichen* — a common 18th-century play on words, as Spinoza was often accused of atheism or "godlessness" due to his pantheist views</note> Spinoza what might have been first in God; for the lowly yet unsolvable question—whether the hen came before the egg or the egg before the hen?—teaches me that it is easier to imagine the existence of both at once than the emergence of one without the other.

It seems necessary that we, in every whole, think of everything belonging to that whole together and at once. Even more so, however, when the All-Being <term>All-Being: *Allwesen*, the author’s term for a supreme, all-encompassing reality</term> is the subject, it would be

---

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>Concerning the First Object of the Idea.</header>
<sig>E 3</sig>

it would be perfectly nonsensical to assume a thing outside of Him that did not belong to everything, or to claim of a thing within Him that it could be more eternal, necessary, and independent than the All-Being.

This necessary quintessence <term>quintessence: *Inbegriff*, the sum total or essential core</term> of all beings and things must contain within itself the substance of all objects and the power of all thoughts, because everything has become and can become out of it; its substance must be object and the power to think at the same time, because its essence is simple <note>simple: in the philosophical sense of *simplex*, meaning indivisible and not made of separate parts</note>; it must have always and collectively been everything it needed to become everything.

One must therefore not consider Him as a chain; He is the great Chain-Maker; He begins the series of causes and consequences where He wills, and can invent various such series.

Yet it remains always true that if a whole were to arise successively, its parts or properties would develop in a certain order and sequence. In a thinking being, memory could impossibly arise before the idea, nor the latter before an object that had produced it; whereas, conversely, an object can exist without an idea, and an idea without a memory of it. Consequently, this analytical procedure shows us nevertheless this much: that the object of the th- <note>The word is completed by the catchword below as "thoughts."</note>

What then is this Object in God that has been able to act upon His thoughts from eternity?

It has often been said: the object of the eternal Idea of God is He Himself; He contemplates Himself—His infinite, perfect attributes and all the possibilities of His creations. Yet possibilities are merely thoughts and require a real object; the possibility to create presupposes the conception of a material from which creation can occur.

God contemplates Himself! But what is God when separated from His thinking and acting? What is He? Attributes are not the self-sufficient essence; they must have a foundation, a source from which they proceed, which produces them and gives them power <term>Power: *Kraft*, often used in this period to denote an active, fundamental energy or force of nature</term>. To be something, one must have a substance; to be Everything, one must have the substance of Everything within oneself.

If God thinks and is conscious, then He must feel; if He feels, He must be a substance that can touch itself and is mobile; movement and feeling must be inherent to this substance, and it must be one and the same

---

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Concerning the First Object of the Idea.</header>
<sig>E 4</sig>

same <note>Continuing the word *einerley* from the previous page</note>, because the divine being is simple <note>In philosophy, "simple" refers to something that is not made of parts; it is unified and indivisible</note>; 1) for this divine being must be so constituted that we can attribute to its nature alone, and all at once, all the properties we assign to God.

I believe, therefore, that the essence of God is a substance from which everything is made and can be made, which is everything and therefore eternal, which must be infinite because it moves by expanding and contracting without ceasing, which is thoroughly sentient and in part perceptible because it thinks and acts. In this way, the essence of God contains within itself the object for thinking and the material for making; His elastic substance, which is pure power and movement, moves in manifold ways and feels its own movement. — — Movement is the object: feeling is the thought.

In us humans, external objects give various movements to the substance of our soul through the senses and teach it new feelings

Just as a musician <term>Musician: *Tonkünstler*, literally a "tone-artist," referring to a composer or skilled performer</term>, through arbitrary experiments on the keyboard <note>original: *Clavier* — a general term for keyboard instruments like the harpsichord or early piano</note>, runs through unintended tones, pairs them, follows them, and thereby discovers a musical thought; so, in a primal being <term>Primal Being: *Urwesen*, a term for the foundational or original essence of God</term> like God, self-determined movements and harmonious feelings of Himself can bring forth thoughts whose objects have developed simultaneously with them.

For the musician, the objects lay within his keyboard; for God, they lie within Him; He is Himself the great musical scale, upon which no dissonance is possible. All tones that it gives forth are harmonic, because no tone is dissonant in itself, but only relatively so; for every dissonance has its chord; it only depends on the scale being extended enough to sustain the division which belongs to the resolution or

## Creation out of Nothing.

God created the world out of nothing! This is a dazzling, authoritative decree composed of purely lofty and obscure concepts; yet, at its core, it is nothing other than the unthinkable assertion—cloaked by a misuse of divine omnipotence—that from nothing, everything has arisen.

Whenever we fetch a thought from the dark storeroom of the "incomprehensibly possible," we should, above all, investigate whether it is above or against reason.

Nothing is more evident than the fact that from nothing, nothing can arise. The opposite of this is impossible; and since I have even proven above the impossibility of the existence of an absolute "nothing," nothing remains for me but to ask: what could move us to assert that God's omnipotence in creation has made the impossible a reality?

I would like to know where our biblical scholars <note>original: *Schriftgelehrten* — refers to theologians or experts in sacred texts</note> have taken this adventurous idea of a creation out of nothing? Perhaps from the arbitrary explanation of the word *barah* <note>original Hebrew: ברא — the verb used in Genesis 1:1, usually translated as "created"</note>?

<column-break/>

<header>Creation out of Nothing.</header>
<page-num>75</page-num>

*barah*, which has been translated as: "He created"? But where does one find that *barah* means "He created out of nothing"? I even doubt whether the general expression "He created!" is correct.

If the Hebrew word *barah* had such a meaning, one would have to translate the passage in Isaiah, chapter 45, verses 6 and 7, as follows: "I am Jehovah and no other, I who make the light and create <note>original: *barah*</note> the darkness: I who give peace, and bring about <note>original: *barah*</note> evil." One sees from this passage, where the word *barah* occurs twice, how nonsensical it sounds for God to declare Himself the "creator" of darkness and evil. One feels that this word cannot even have the meaning of "bringing forth." What, then, does *barah* mean? Whether one derives it from the root *barar* <note>original Hebrew: ברר — meaning to purify, select, or clarify</note> or *barah*, one finds that its true sense expresses a purification or a separation <note>The author is arguing for a "separation" model of creation rather than "bringing into existence."</note>. I would therefore translate the first verse of the First Book of Moses <note>The Book of Genesis</note> as follows: "In the beginning, the legislative powers of God <note>The author likely interprets the Hebrew name for God, *Elohim*, as a plural "legislative powers" or "judges."</note> separated fire, water, and earth."

<header>Empty Space and Fullness.</header>
<meta>catchword: and, The</meta>

## Empty Space and Fullness.

It is common to imagine the "All" — the universe — under the crude image of an all-encompassing sphere. Our sensation of immensity busies itself with seeking out a place for this monstrous clump, and in the darkness, it gives birth to a second monstrosity which we call "empty space"; a worthy half-brother of nothingness, which, even if it could have an essence and exist, would by that very fact only be a part of the <term>All-Being</term> <note>original: *Allwesen* — a term used to describe God as the totality of all that exists</note>. But its essence is nothing: an extension of nothingness, the room for the non-existence of all things.

We allow ourselves to be deceived by an effect of the immensity filled within us, which to some extent misleads us into the imagination of empty space. The infinite space in which our thoughts move their images seems empty to us because we encounter nothing material within it. However, this space is not empty because of this, but rather "thought-full." In a similar way, the infinite space in which everything moves is not empty, but full of forces whose expandable substance fills the smallest gaps,

---

and whose elastic flexibility gives room to all movements occurring within it. The possibility for the movement of the All-Being, however, is not a "space"—because that would have to be greater than God—but rather an infinite capacity to create space.

One might object to me that the place occupied by the All-Being must have existed before Him. However, one must make a distinction between: *Nothing*, which could prevent the All-Being from maintaining its extension, and *Something*, which would assign Him His place. If infinite space is "Something," then it belongs to the sum total of Everything — and thus it has been there eternally, inseparably with Him. If it is "Nothing," then it could never have been there; consequently, one must not say that it had to be there in order to be able to place "Everything" into it.

The concept of the place occupied by a body is always determined by other bodies standing beside it. But the All-Being has no "place," for nothing stands beside Him; He also does not actually "occupy" space like a body, for He has no form, no boundaries, no definition; rather, He forms forms, He sets boundaries, and gives definition. Thus, He creates space within Himself; and outside of Him, there is nothing that could hinder His immensity.

<vocab>Universe, Sphere, Immensity, Empty space, All-Being, Material things, Forces, God, Extension, Body</vocab>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<meta>catchword: barah (a Hebrew verb referring to creation)</meta>

## Creation out of Nothing.

God created the world out of nothing! This is a dazzling, authoritative decree composed of purely lofty and obscure concepts; yet, at its core, it is nothing other than the unthinkable assertion—cloaked by a misuse of divine omnipotence—that from nothing, everything has arisen.

Whenever we fetch a thought from the dark storeroom of the "incomprehensibly possible," we should, above all, investigate whether it is above or against reason.

Nothing is more evident than the fact that from nothing, nothing can arise <note>The author is referring to the philosophical principle *ex nihilo nihil fit*</note>. The opposite of this is impossible; and since I have even proven above the impossibility of the existence of an absolute "nothing," nothing remains for me but to ask: what could move us to assert that God's omnipotence in creation has made the impossible a reality?

I would like to know where our biblical scholars <note>original: *Schriftgelehrten* — refers to theologians or experts in sacred texts</note> have taken this adventurous idea of a creation out of nothing? Perhaps from the arbitrary explanation of the word *barah* <note>original Hebrew: ברא — the verb used in Genesis 1:1, usually translated as "created"</note>?

<column-break/>

<header>Creation out of Nothing.</header>
<page-num>75</page-num>

*barah*, which has been translated as: "He created"? But where does one find that *barah* means "He created out of nothing"? I even doubt whether the general expression "He created!" is correct.

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>Empty Space and Fullness.</header>
<meta>catchword: We</meta>

The <term>All-Being</term> <note>original: *Allwesen* — a philosophical term for God as the totality of all existence</note> can have nothing real outside of itself, and nothing unreal within itself; consequently, it can neither be enclosed by anything, nor can it enclose an empty space. The expansion of its elastic substance is present everywhere, and gives me a concept of the fullness of the <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: *Gottheit*</note>, from which God has separated all beings distinct from Him, divided all forces, and carved out all forms that constitute the sum of all things.

One will accuse me of making everything into God <note>The author is addressing potential charges of "Pantheism"—the controversial belief that God and the universe are one and the same</note>. But is not my force distinct from my person? When I speak, a force that forms and encloses my words goes away from me into the air; it has a duration, it does not return, it is perceived by others through the senses, and it relates to me almost as a spirit relates to God. Furthermore: If I cut my nails and my hair and manufacture something from them, can one say that what I have made from them is my person?

Furthermore, I will be reproached for attempting to attribute a body to God. — And what if I did? Many <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: *Weltweisen* — literally "world-wise ones," an archaic term for philosophers</note> and some <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>early Christian teachers and bishops</note> have been of this opinion. However, if I assume a <term>primal substance</term> <note>original: *Urstof* (modern: *Urstoff*)</note> in God, it is not for that reason a body, but rather force.

<column-break/>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>Empty Space and Fullness.</header>
<meta>catchword: What</meta>

We sense within and outside of ourselves forces that do not think; through forces, our spirit acts upon the body, and forces act upon our senses; for force is presumably the intermediate thing that connects both soul and body, and can make the transition from spirit to matter comprehensible. Everything that can be said of a thing consists of accounts of its forces, and we know nothing that is the opposite of a force. Even "dead" matter—even if we "kill" it as much as possible in our thoughts—acts upon our sight or upon our feeling, and proves to us that it is nonetheless forceful.

Thus, everything is force. — But it does not yet follow from this that force is a material body.

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>What is Matter?</header>
<meta>catchword: "un-"</meta>

## What is Matter?

All the properties of matter that I know are the following: its various effects on our senses; its form, which makes it a specific and limited thing; and its impenetrability.

Now I find that a force can be attributed to each of these properties.

The property of acting upon our senses is obviously powerful. Impenetrability is as well, because it can resist forces, and form must likewise have a force within itself that is destined to preserve it, and which binds its parts together—which would otherwise fall apart—and arranges them for a purpose. If, therefore, I mentally strip away everything from matter that is a force, I am left with nothing but a nameless thing that possesses all the aforementioned forces. Since, however, I will show that forces are elastic substances which can be expanded, and consequently can acquire a specific length, breadth, and depth, I define matter as follows: "that it be a composition of opposing forces, which constitute an

<column-break/>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>What is Matter?</header>
<sig>F</sig>
<meta>catchword: Honor</meta>

impenetrable expansion or form, bounded on all sides," and I believe, therefore, that the various measured divisions and manifold mixtures of compressive and repulsive forces, bound together and purposefully ordered by the simple elastic force, form all bodies.

If I were to express the essence of matter in a hieroglyph <note>The author uses "hieroglyph" in the 18th-century sense of a symbolic emblem or mystical sign</note>, I would represent it by a cross enclosed by a circle:

The cross is an image of <term>adversity</term> <note>original: *Widrigkeit* — here used in the physical sense of "opposition" or "contrariety," where two forces push against one another</note> and the only possible figure that two straight lines can produce. Thus, two lines that intersect each other signify two opposing forces, but the circle that encloses them and which they fill expresses: magnitude, form, purpose, impenetrability, and all the properties of matter. If one now views this ground plan cubically <note>If you imagine this 2D circle and cross as a 3D sphere containing an internal framework</note>, one has a perfect pictorial definition of a body, or of the material world.

With astonishment, I have found this hieroglyph placed beside the Egyptian Sphinx. This mysterious monster seems to guard it as a superior riddle beneath its lion's claw. Should my metaphysical <term>whimsies</term> <note>original: *Grillen* — literally "crickets," a common idiom of the era for idiosyncratic or imaginative thoughts</note> the...

<summary>The author defines matter not as a "dead" substance, but as a dynamic composition of opposing repulsive and compressive forces bound together in a specific form. He illustrates this concept using the symbol of a cross within a circle, which he claims to have discovered associated with the Egyptian Sphinx.</summary>

## Origin of Matter.

We would certainly satisfy a great desire of our understanding if we could show a possibility of how matter might have originated. We wish to attempt to see whether this difficult problem is entirely unsolvable.

In general, it is easiest to think that in God, originally, there was pure Spirit and no matter; consequently, that the <term>primary substance</term> <note>original: *Urstoff* — the fundamental, original element from which all things are made</note> from which all things arose must have been a spiritual force.

I therefore assume a primary substance in God, from which everything that was, is, and will be, consists—as simple in its nature as it is infinite in the variety of its applications—an immeasurable expansion of His elastic force, which moves within itself without separating.

In this [force], God presumably established, before all else and in a manner incomprehensible to us, the individuality of such beings to whom He wished to give consciousness and sensation of His effects.

From this almighty source were also taken the forces which the Father of all spirits,

### Difference between Matter and Spirit.

**W**e call everything that affects our senses "matter," without considering that everything that acts is a force <note>original: *Kraft* — in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to an active principle or energy rather than just physical strength</note>, and that force and matter cannot essentially be the same thing.

Conversely, we call everything "spirit" that is not matter — the opposite of all sensory objects, which we only know through the manifold sensations of their forces. Thus, we associate the word matter with the incorrect concept of a force, and the word spirit with the dark and empty idea of a negation; for we tend to represent almost all thoughts incomprehensible to us through negations of comprehensible things; presumably because negation is the favorite occupation of proud ignorance. Yet, how many things would we not learn to comprehend if we dared to give them intelligible names?

Thus, the elements of matter must also consist of a simple and indivisible substance.

## What may be infinitely divisible, or not.

Two propositions are immensely clear to us; the first: that we have the capacity and the drive to enlarge and diminish a thing infinitely; the second: that matter cannot possibly be the object of such a capacity, because it and its parts cannot be conceived without a form.

The material universe can be imagined as infinitely larger, and its very smallest constituent particle can be imagined as infinitely smaller than it is. The thought of both is unlimited. But the system of all worlds cannot in reality be larger than it is, and the final elements of matter cannot be smaller than they are. In terms of possibility, both could be larger or smaller; yet they are as they are, and have their definite measure.

If we forget that the concept of the limitless cannot be applied to the limited, then we fall into the vain attempt of wanting to think of the material universe as larger than it is, without thereby [attaining] the possibility of a... <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break; the catchword "un-" suggests the next word is "unendlichen" (infinite) or "unmöglichen" (impossible).</note>

However, if we apply this infinite capacity for enlargement or reduction to a **spiritual force** <term>spiritual force: *geistige Kraft*, a term used by 18th-century thinkers to describe an active, non-material energy or "soul-like" power that underlies the physical world</term>, we can, for example, imagine an elastic sphere consisting of air or fire, which we expand from all sides into infinity, or contract into a single point, which can be thought of as a so-called **mathematical point** <note>a point that has position but no volume or physical dimensions</note>.

Here we find no form whose definition would contradict the concept of total indeterminacy: here is the possibility for all forms and an extension beyond the limits of the totality of all forms: here is the possibility for infinite reduction, because there is no cause present that could set limits; for form alone can be the cause of a limit.

The drive we feel within ourselves to enlarge and reduce things into infinity would thus betray the existence of such a spiritual, elastic force, even if we had no other evidence of its effects.

I therefore claim that the components of matter are of the same nature as matter itself; that

## Only Unity is Divisible.

I sometimes find pleasure in attempting to assert the opposite of a generally accepted proposition, and through this method, I have stripped many a **scholastic truth** <note>original: *Schulwahrheit*—referring to the established dogmas or "textbook truths" taught in formal academic settings during the Enlightenment.</note> of its prestige and captured many a new thought. With such an intention, I once wrote on a piece of paper: *Only Unity is divisible.* An orthodox metaphysician found this paper and took such great offense at it that I was compelled to take up the pen and give him philosophical satisfaction. My explanation finds its place here because it can serve to clarify my concepts of spirit and matter.

Here it is:

A metaphysician and a mathematician went forth with the resolution to find something that would be indivisible. They required it for their theoretical systems, and yet they could not find it. What did they do to finish their task quickly? They **supposed** <note>original: *supponirten*—to assume or hypothesize without proof.</note>—contrary to their experience and notwithstanding the impossibility of even thinking it, merely because they needed to ground their theorems—they supposed things which they declared to be indivisible, and

---

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>Only Unity is Divisible.</header>
<meta>catchword: die (the)</meta>

created out of nothing the **monads** <term>monads: *Monaden*, fundamental, indivisible units of reality in the philosophy of Leibniz and his followers, often described as simple substances without physical dimensions.</term> and the **mathematical point** <note>a theoretical point in geometry that has position but no volume, length, or width.</note>.

But let someone name for me a thing that could not be touched from two sides, or whose half could not be imagined? Such a thing cannot even be established in the imagination. And yet, they have accepted an unthinkable thing not only as thinkable but as true, in order to provide unintelligible proofs through an unproven, absurd thought.

But what cause has one had to so greatly loathe a possible **extension** <term>extension: *Ausdehnung*, the quality of occupying physical space; in the philosophy of the time, "extended" things were material, while "unextended" things were spiritual.</term> in the essence of God and spirits? None other than the fear that from the presumed divisibility of their substance, one might infer the possibility of their annihilation.

To honor God and our soul, they invented the monads; they believed one must be at least as small as a mathematical point to escape destruction.

Thus, the following articles of faith have been moved into metaphysics: "There are real beings that take up no space; simple beings have neither extension nor divisibility; they last eternally because God himself cannot annihilate them." Whoever might have demanded too strict a proof for any of these would be seen as a heretic, who

He challenges the dogma that spiritual substances must lack physical extension to be eternal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s unorthodox argument that only "unity" or simple, non-composite substances are truly divisible, whereas composite objects are merely "disassembled." <page-num>96</page-num>
<header>Only Unity is Divisible.</header>
<meta>catchword: Compos-</meta>

...the immortality of our soul has been doubted, they have been persecuted; and thus, prudence and habit have grasped that which the weakness of the understanding could never have comprehended.

But even if these propositions were true, they still would not prove what they are intended to prove.

Whether God has parts or not, there is no one who could divide Him. Whether spirits consist of parts or not, He who made them can destroy them just as easily all at once as He can bit by bit. I cannot see why they could not cease to exist in the same manner in which they arose. On the contrary, if I stick to what I already know and what is intelligible to me, I find that a simple substance—which, because it is extended, occupies a space—is indestructible precisely for the sake of its **divisibility**; for its divisibility is infinite and eternal because this substance is not **composite**. <note>The author uses a paradoxical argument: a "simple" substance cannot be destroyed by breaking it apart because every "piece" of it is still the same substance. Only things made of different parts (composite) can be destroyed by separating those parts.</note> The infinite part of a spirit is and always remains spirit—no division can harm its essence. — It could only cease through **annihilation**, and against this possibility within Omnipotence, reason can offer no proof.

On the contrary, only a composite thing can be not only destroyed but even annihilated through division, because its essence is **compos-**

---

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>Only Unity is Divisible.</header>
<sig>G</sig>

**-ition**. — As soon as this composition ceases, its essence has also vanished.

If I dissect a pear until I find the water, earth, and sugar parts contained within it, then the pear is entirely gone. If chemistry could go further, and resolve not only oils and salts but also earth and water parts, it would reach the **elements of matter**, which only the Creator can divide: If He divided them, their material essence would also cease, and then the **forces** <term>forces: *Kräfte*, in the physics of this period, these were the underlying active principles that produced the appearance of solid matter.</term> from which they were composed would remain; these, divided into infinity, always remain forces, and their simple essence is indestructible.

I believe the knot of our confusion regarding the divisibility of a simple being lies in the incorrect concept we form of "division."

One confuses **dividing** with **disassembling**, and the **divisible** with the **divided**. A composite thing is already divided; it has its specific number of parts, which the divider cannot change without breaking a unity into pieces.

Properly speaking, one can only divide what has no parts and is not already divided by its nature—thus, only a simple thing: but this is regarded as impossible because we have become accustomed to thinking of a **unity** as being as small as nothing.

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>Only Unity is divisible.</header>

think. Therefore, I allow everyone to ask with amazement: how is it possible to divide the number **One**? — I, however, ask in return: how can one divide a **Million**?

A million consists of a thousand times a thousand individual things, not one more and not one less. It cannot be broken down except into the already existing parts of its composition; for otherwise, one would have to attack and mutilate its **units**.

A composite number can be dissolved into no more units than it has itself expressed — — a faithful image of all **material** things, which, because they have a magnitude, are composed of a specific number of elements of their kind, and can only be broken down into their already existing parts. But the number **One** is non-composite, a simple whole that has no parts, and consequently is essentially **divisible**. The latter occurs through **fractions**, whose division and multiplication I can continue to infinity in equal and unequal numbers — — also a faithful image of the whole, the **All-Being** <note>The "All-Being" (German: *Allwesen*) refers to a pantheistic or monotheistic concept of a universal God or singular reality from which all individuals emerge.</note>, whose division into **individual** beings can likewise be followed through the entire **infinity** of numbers.

## Force.

The word **Force** <note>German: *Kraft*. In this context, it encompasses power, energy, and the physical "forces" of nature.</note> is one of the most common and most vague expressions in our metaphysical dictionary.

We call every cause of an effect a "force" before we have examined it more closely, just as we call every object a "thing" until we have found its proper name. In both cases, habit does not err: every active cause is a force, just as every thing about which something can be said is a "thing"; yet in both cases, we take an entirely indefinite expression for granted as known, and allow an empty word to stand in for a concept.

Sometimes we also say: Force is an ability <note>German: *Vermögen*, often translated in philosophical contexts as "faculty" or "capacity."</note> to effect this or that, without considering that neither of these two words explains the other, and that they do not even share the same meaning. For ability relates to force as the future relates to the present,

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for the upcoming sections and begins an inquiry into the definition of "Force," arguing that it is often used as a vague placeholder for unknown causes.</summary>
<keywords>Force, metaphysics, cause and effect, capacity, terminology, philosophy of science, vis viva, Table of Contents</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 101, where the author was distinguishing between "Force" (as an active, present state) and "Ability" or "Faculty" (as a potential or future state). The text completes the sentence comparing the relationship of the possible to the actual.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>->Force.<-</header>
<meta>catchword: We</meta>

ly, <note>original: "-gen", completing "Gegenwärtigen" (presently/immediately) from the previous page.</note> or as the possible relates to the actual: The word **Ability** <note>German: *Vermögen*, referring to a latent capacity or faculty.</note> says nothing more than that everything is present which the force needs in order to act.

### But what is Force?

No force can be conceived without an effect, no effect without change, and no change without motion; conversely, nothing can move without producing a change, and consequently an effect, within or outside of itself; thus motion is the nearest concept that unites with the word force.

I may reflect as much as I will, yet I find no other difference between force and motion than two relative ways of imagining this same thing differently. If force appears to us as a change of space, we call it motion, and every other effect of motion we call force. But which is more forceful: the agent <note>German: *das Wirkende*, the thing doing the acting.</note> or the result? <note>German: *das Gewirkte*, the thing acted upon or produced.</note> 1)

We have become accustomed to viewing motion always as a consequence, and seldom as a cause, and therefore we frequently regard it merely as a property of force; however, there are two types of motions: a passive and an active one. The passive is indeed only the consequence of a force, which, however, can be nothing other than a motion; but the active is always a cause. In the chain of things enclosed in time and space, motion is everywhere the consequence of a preceding one, by which it was effected, and the cause of a new one, which it brings forth. But if we ascend the ladder of motion, we find that it loses itself in infinity; that motion, if it had to have a beginning, could not have arisen otherwise than from a motion, and consequently, that it must have arisen from itself—which is to say: it has its cause, its ground within itself. ### Immobility.

At first glance, motion seems to have taken its beginning in immobility; much like how formed space has its origin in the immense; <note>German: *Unermeßlichen*, referring to the immeasurable or the infinite.</note> for we are inclined to believe that immobility and immensity belong together because we cannot grasp either of them; whereas motion, by contrast, belongs only to space and form, because everything we know is in motion.

However, I suspect that the matter could be exactly the opposite. When I reflect carefully, I find that immensity is by its nature mobile, whereas immobility—if such a thing exists—is perhaps only to be encountered in form and in space.

A thing that is supposed to be immobile cannot change its place; it occupies the same space at all times, for it can become neither larger nor smaller, and nothing would be more contradictory to the nature of immensity than such a restriction.

But we wish to show even more clearly that the immobile cannot be immense.

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>Immobility.</header>
<meta>catchword: un-</meta>

If we wish to form a comprehensible concept of infinity, we must regard every species of it as an eternal progression <note>German: *Progression*, used here to mean a continuous, step-by-step advancement.</note> from possibility to reality: Eternity is and remains at all times in a state of *becoming*;—an inexhaustible future unfolds its unceasing existence: Numbers grow and multiply into infinity; yet the totality of infinite numbers can never be thought of collectively; <note>The author is arguing against the idea of "actual infinity"—the notion that you could ever have a completed set of infinite things.</note> they can never exist as a reality all at once; for they would cease to be infinite if they were simply there without the ability to multiply further. This becomes even more palpable in the representation of an infinite immensity, because it is always connected with the idea of a substance. <note>In this philosophical context, "substance" refers to the fundamental "stuff" or essence that makes up reality.</note> Would a substance be limitless if it could become neither larger nor smaller? And can a real immensity not be doubled to infinity by one that is merely thinkable?

This application of an infinite possibility to an unbounded reality gives us the key to how we can think our way into the incomprehensible infinity, and shows us that the pure immensity of an infinite substance is only thinkable, but that its real immensity lies in the indeterminability <note>German: *Unbestimmbarkeit*, meaning a state that cannot be fixed or measured.</note> of its size, and consists only in the mere capacity

<column-break/>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>Immobility.</header>
<meta>catchword: Can</meta>

to be able to become infinitely larger or smaller. Such a boundless indeterminability can, however, only be maintained through an uninterrupted movement—through a constant change of its size. That is why I have said that motion is essentially inherent to immensity.

Movement or Immobility! one of the two must be eternal; one must have produced the other.

Suppose immobility were eternal; then it would have to remain so always; for that which is eternal can never cease to be, and it would cease if it transformed itself into motion. Therefore, the latter cannot have its origin in immobility.

One will object to me that, for the same reason, motion cannot be eternal either, because it is sometimes made immobile.—

But it is not; it may be held back by a resistance, but it continues again immediately after it; it has never stopped pressing against the resistance, so it has also never stopped being what it was, but has only been **seemingly** immobile.

# Motion.

What then is motion? I am not speaking here of the change of place of bodies, which is brought about as a result of gravity, impact, or some other displacement, but of the living, essential, original force of motion that moves itself and communicates itself to others.

When it moves, it must change a space, and to communicate itself, it must touch other things. In both cases, it must be an extended or extendable substance <note>The author uses "substance" in the philosophical sense: something that exists in itself rather than as a mere property of something else.</note>. 1)

---

1) One will object to me that motion is only an essential property and not the substance of extensibility <term>extensibility: the quality of occupying space</term> itself; but what would this substance be without motion? And what would the force of motion be if it did not have a substantial essence? Accustomed to the ideas of soul and body, we would so like to think of substance as the body and motion as the soul. But what would a soul be that had no substance of its own? In that case, one could also only call it a property of the body.

<column-break/>

Bewegung. 113
H
<meta>catchword: expansion</meta>

When an extended substance moves itself, it thereby becomes either larger or smaller. The substance of motion is thus either expanding or contracting. It must be both; otherwise, it would only grow or only decrease; and it must be both in an infinite degree, for otherwise one would have to assume an immobile force that hindered it from within or without—a wall in both the large and the small, behind which lay nothingness.

It must be eternal, otherwise an eternal immobility must have existed before, from which it would have arisen in an unthinkable way, and we have already shown that this cannot be.

Since we can conceive of no force that exists without motion, it must be not only the most manifold but also the simplest, and because it, in its expansion and contraction

attributed to him by the schools; he could have had only one single thought from eternity, could have done only one single thing; he would be a perfect block <note>German: *Klotz*; a term for a crude, unfeeling, and inanimate lump of wood or stone.</note> without will, without freedom, without choice; he would be the slave of his own perfection; nothing would remain to make him venerable except his incomprehensibility, and this would be of a kind that would contradict the very reason he has given us to recognize him, rather than exceeding it. There is a great difference between *suffering* change and *creating* it; and an even greater difference between change that constitutes a perfection, and a necessary immutability that would be an imperfection. The substance of God is unchanging in motion: if it moved only occasionally and rested occasionally, then it would be changeable!

Finally, one must consider the great truth that the greatest perfection of unity consists in its manifoldness. The divine substance is the greatest unity from which all diversities come, and such a change-creating immutability gives me a true concept of the eternal Creator, who is life and motion.

If life is motion, then immobility in God would be an eternal death. Whoever [considers] the mo— <note>Catchword "we-" in German refers to "Wer die Bewegung" (Whoever considers motion), which continues on the next page.</note>

In motion lies the secret of God’s immensity <note>German: *Unermeßlichkeit*, a term implying both vast scale and the quality of being impossible to measure.</note>, of which we perhaps form a false conception. — Immensity is the infinite expandability of an indeterminate extension; it is, as we have already shown in the previous article, the property of a being whose expandable nature—because it is always in motion—contains all magnitudes and smallnesses within itself without having a specific magnitude or smallness, and which can assume every conceivable degree of these without reaching that unthinkable degree where it could become neither larger nor smaller.

->### Motion.<-
Signature: H 3
<meta>catchword: without</meta>

The Universal Being <note>German: *Allwesen*; a philosophical term used to describe God as the totality of all that exists.</note> of the divine substance appears to me to be boundless and infinite — in regard to its *possible* expansion or contraction — but never in *actuality*. It would cease to have these infinite properties if it were either so large or so small that it could not be imagined larger or smaller. It always has an essential magnitude; however, because of its constant motion, this remains always indeterminate and rises above all other magnitudes by producing them all, separating them from itself, and encompassing them. I see in the eternal motion of God the basis for all creation. Within his infinite expandability all possibilities lie hidden, and his immeasurable extension brings all his diverse works into reality.

But if the divine substance is not actually infinitely extended, it would seem to move in an empty space. We have already shown above that space only exists insofar as it is formed by external forces around a form impenetrable to it, and that it is never empty. But here we speak of the All, outside of which there is nothing. Infinity is its space. Just as reality finds its place in possibility, so the Universal Being moves within infinity.

It is strange that we find it so difficult to break the habit of imagining "Nothingness" as a sea in which everything swims, since it is so contradictory to think that Nothing could be not only Something, but more than Everything.

<page-num>119</page-num>

## Contact.

Motion and contact <term>Berührung: the physical or metaphysical act of two things touching</term> border upon one another, and I believe that neither is thinkable without the other.

That imparted motion <note>The motion transferred from one object to another, like billiard balls hitting each other.</note> is impossible without contact is generally evident and well-known.

But is the essential, independent motion of a simple, undivided substance also grounded in contact? How can a thing touch itself if it has no parts?

We satisfy ourselves with the material conception of contact occurring at specific points, and we regard it as the highest degree of drawing together separate parts: we imagine them so close that nothing remains between them, and our concept of contact rests on this final circumstance.

But precisely this exact connection—that nothing is in between—is very imperfect in the material world. No surface is so smooth, no form so straight, that it could truly touch another except at a few points; and even this is denied by some natural philosophers <note>German: *Naturkündiger*. A term for early scientists or physicists who studied the laws of the material world.</note>.

<sig>H 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>The author argues that space is an extension of God's presence rather than a pre-existing container, and begins a new inquiry into how motion requires "contact," even in indivisible substances.</summary>
<keywords>Universal Being, space, nothingness, motion, contact, substance, natural philosophy, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of "Contact" (Berührung) established on page 119. Having previously questioned how an undivided substance might touch itself, the author now defines true contact as a property of simple, elastic substances rather than just physical bodies hitting each other.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
->### Contact.<-

The closer two bodies strike against each other, the more strongly they touch. The more points that touch one another, the greater is that which we imagine under the term "contact." Now, what is closer together than a cohesive substance that has no parts? What has more points of contact within itself than a being that is infinitely divisible, without any conceivable point that could cease to be touched from all sides? Here there are no boundaries where contact would have to stop; no parts that it would have to leave untouched; no gap that could interrupt it. We see, therefore, that true, perfect, intimate, and self-penetrating contact actually belongs to the simple elastic substance <note>The author is likely referring to a vitalist concept where the fundamental building blocks of reality are not hard "atoms" but flexible, forceful entities.</note>, especially when it contracts; and that this contact, together with motion, constitutes its very essence.

---

<meta>catchword: Feeling.</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>121</page-num>

---

# Feeling.

Bodies move one another in proportion to their contact. But where does it come from that mere contact at a single point can move a body? Often only the outermost edge of its sphere of vapors <note>German: *Dunstkugel*. An early scientific concept describing the atmosphere or field of influence surrounding a physical body.</note> has been touched—so distantly—that one has had to invent the thing called <term>action at a distance <note>original: "actio in distans"</note></term> to explain certain effects of attraction and repulsion. How can the mere approach of one body have acted upon another? Why is the counter-pressure so perfectly matched to the pressure? According to what does the contact of a body effect a specific direction of motion, the line of which can often be calculated? Here one notices that between contact and motion there must be a third thing which distinguishes the former and gives direction to the latter. This thing I call <term>Feeling <note>German: *Fühlung*. In this context, it refers not to human emotion, but to a fundamental physical or metaphysical "sensitivity" or capacity for a substance to react to its environment.</note></term>. I say that it is: an essential force of the elastic substance to direct its motion according to the quality of a contact.

The separated forces, of which the whole of physical nature consists, are antagonistic toward one another

<sig>H 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

<summary>This page explores the metaphysical mechanics of physical contact, arguing that "feeling" or "sensitivity" is the necessary bridge that allows contact to translate into directed motion. The author suggests that even "action at a distance" is mediated by the inherent elastic forces of substances.</summary>

<keywords>contact, feeling, motion, elastic substance, action at a distance, pressure and counter-pressure, vitalism, physics</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how fundamental forces interact. Having established that "Contact" is an essential property of simple elastic substances, the author now transitions to "Feeling" (Fühlung) as the internal mechanism that guides how these substances react to one another, whether by attracting or repelling.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>Feeling.</header>
<meta>catchword: of the</meta>

...or unite. Their laws are: to flee from one another or to unite. These laws are imprinted upon their very essence—as **feelings**. When they are touched, they know exactly whether—and how much—the substance that touches them is of their own essence, or not. They then give the movement of their substance a direction: either to unite with the other by the shortest path, or to flee from it. This shortest path is always a straight line, for all forces act in straight lines. However bumpy or thick the outer shell may be, the force within it is always rectilinear, and the thread of its stream is thinner than can be imagined.

**Contact**, **Feeling**, and **Movement** are three ways of being for a force or elastic substance <note>In the 18th century, "elastic" often referred to the ability of a substance to contain and exert force, much like a spring.</note>, all three of which flow together into one and are present at the same moment. Movement is bound up with contact and feeling, and from the latter, a new movement follows.

Feeling always arises between two forces that touch one another and gives direction to a new movement. In the realm of matter, this new movement is what we call **counter-pressure**. It is always in a certain proportion to the strength, nature, or direction of the initial pressure; and since one cannot rationally conceive how the force

<column-break/>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>Feeling.</header>
<meta>catchword: I</meta>

of the pressure could have left one body to pass into another, the appropriate perfection of the counter-pressure proves a *knowledge* of the strength, direction, and nature of the pressure. This accurate knowledge, drawn from a mere contact, is: **Feeling**.

Simple force can move in two ways: by contracting or by expanding. But it seems that it must have a means—a cause within itself—to determine itself toward one or the other, and this cause is Feeling. Thus, without feeling, movement would lack a cause for itself.

Furthermore, movement would be useless if it could not be felt. Its existence would be no truth, neither for itself nor for anything else. Every individual movement would be lost; it would achieve nothing and would be unable to connect with any new movement.

Feeling binds and manages the artful chain of causes and consequences which **Omniscience** has contemplated and **Omnipotence** has built toward a purpose. Feeling possesses truth and wisdom, for it rarely errs; and why should a substance, which seems originally to have been separated from the **Substance of God**, not be capable of understanding? <note>The author suggests a vitalist view of the universe where even the smallest "forces" possess a spark of divine intelligence or "feeling" that allows them to follow the laws of physics.</note>

Habit, fear, hope, indolence, and courage are transformations that feeling causes within us, and these are words that can also be applied comparatively in the physical realm to the "feeling" of lifeless things.

**Feeling** is the soul of movement. From it spring all distinct diversities, and consequently: order, number, measure, and proportions—everything lofty, thinking, and spiritual ultimately boils down to feeling.

The intimate connection of our thought with feeling—which provides all the material for it—has misled us into denying the power to feel to those things to which we deny the power of thought. Yet, that feeling does not need to think in order to act with wisdom, and that a part can sometimes feel with greater truth than the whole, is most closely and strongly proven to us by the muscles of our body, animated by nerves. <note>The author is suggesting that our bodies possess a "biological wisdom" or instinctive reaction that occurs faster and more accurately than conscious thought.</note>

The perception that life and feeling are almost inseparable has moved us to completely deny feeling to everything we call "lifeless." But what is it that does *not* live? Where in nature could one likely suspect the immense leap between life and death, between movement and immobility, between feeling and perfect insensibility? ## Does God Feel?

The feelings of God alone are without error. He feels the essence of things and their properties; he feels what a thing was, what it is, and what it will be. Would such a way of feeling be unbecoming for a God?

Since all our ideas arose, were composed, or were abstracted from sensory objects, it must be objected to me that God has no senses. Firstly, this is not proven. God can have a single universal sense, or more senses than we do. I do not see why we should not be allowed to say that He has more perfect senses than we, since all perfections that we attribute to Him are only superlatives of our noblest advantages. <note>The author is referencing a traditional philosophical idea that we can only describe God by taking human virtues—like wisdom or power—and projecting them to an infinite, "superlative" degree.</note>

But assuming He had none, He still has concepts of sensory things. He thinks them; and since no other way of thinking is known to us than feeling, we must not unnecessarily assume a way of thinking unthinkable to us, in order,

Another proof that God must feel lies in the only possible concept one can form of bliss <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of supreme, lasting happiness or blessedness</term>. To possess bliss, one must not only feel but also perceive that one is happy. All our pleasures arise from a varied, strong, and frequent feeling of our own or others' powers <note>original: *Kräfte*; in this context, the author refers to the vital or spiritual forces that animate a being</note>, with which we wish to unite; and every reduction of our powers, every separation from a desired union, every hindrance in feeling, is pain and frustration. God’s bliss certainly has higher and more essential objects within and outside Himself than we do; but we cannot imagine how He could enjoy either His happiness or the objects of it without feeling them; and the nature of His feeling is inevitably as perfect as He is Himself. If feeling arises from contact <note>The author is likely referring to the philosophical idea, common in the 18th century, that all senses are ultimately specialized forms of touch or "contact."</note>, then His sensing must surpass everything; for what touches more intimately, more extensively, and more incessantly than a substance—which I have assumed to be infinitely expandable in the Supreme Being <note>original: *Allwesen*</note>—and if the sensing of one's own powers brings happiness, how happy must that being be, who all

<header>Truth.</header>

## Truth.

In humans, feeling is the noblest part of their being, because for them it is the only means to truth. We are acquainted with two kinds of truths. The first are those we do not contradict; the second are those we believe. Both rest on feelings; the former on experiences, which are always abstracted from sensory things—and consequently from feelings; we assume that we or others have felt correctly, and the conclusion drawn from this is true because we cannot contradict it. The latter are simple feelings; these do not need to be proven to us; we believe them to the same degree in which we feel them. One believes a slap in the face <note>original: *Ohrfeige*</note> quite differently than one believes a logical demonstration. The latter is unfeelable; the former is indemonstrable. One seeks to contradict a demonstration, but not a feeling: one holds a blind faith <term>Köhlerglauben: literally "charcoal-burner's faith," an idiom referring to a simple, unquestioning, and absolute belief</term> in feeling; especially when one has need of it, when one awaits it with longing, or when it brings us comfort. Faith is feeling. To be sure, our senses often deceive us in our feeling; yet they have also often deceived us in the premises of a rational conclusion;
<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>Truth.</header>

for every demonstration rests in its distant origin on sensory concepts, which we accepted as true precisely because we had felt them. Only the *form* of a demonstration is free from error, but by no means the *ideas* with which it deals.

Truth is commonly defined as: the affirmation of a thing whose opposite involves a contradiction. This definition certainly contains an essential distinction from all other things, but it does not show how truth *works*; it says only this much: that the false cannot be true. It seems as if, in this definition, more regard was paid to demonstrated truths than to felt truths, because demonstrations make it their business to prove a matter to the point of absurdity <note>original: *usque ad absurdum*</note>.

I would therefore prefer to define truth as: the feelability <note>original: *Fühlbarkeit*; the quality of being perceptible through sensation or internal feeling</note> of a thing’s existence.

It is customary to divide truth into absolute and relative. In my opinion, an absolute truth is that which one feels; a relative truth is that which one demonstrates. The latter always refers back to the word: *If*, with which one can make everything possible and true; for if

Felt truths are indeed relative to the senses—if these do not deceive me, then the matter is so and not otherwise; but that I feel, and feel in the way that I feel, is a positive truth; for to feel that one does not feel, or to feel the opposite of what one feels, is an impossibility. To say as a logical conclusion: a thing *is* because it simultaneously *is not*—would be a formal contradiction. Therefore, logical deduction and the feeling of feeling <note>The author is referring to self-consciousness or the undeniable internal awareness of one's own existence and sensations.</note> are positive truths, the only ones we have.

## Elasticity and Living Force.

A body is called elastic if, after suffering a violent expansion or contraction, it exerts the force to return itself quickly and with violence to its previous state.

One cannot well attribute these powerful effects to the cohesion of a particular physical structure; elastic vapors <note>Referring to gases or steam, which expand to fill their containers.</note> have no cohesion proportionate to their power, and a straight steel spring would have to have a cohesion that allowed itself to be lengthened at one moment and shortened the next; for on whatever side I bend it, the line of its inner arc narrows, and the circumference of its outer arc expands, without the spring becoming thicker. One sees from this that that which constitutes its elasticity <term>Schnellkraft: literally "quick-force"; an 18th-century term for the inherent tension or "spring" in an object.</term> must be a substance that can withdraw into itself and expand its essence out of itself; it is no mere displacement of parts that move in ball-and-socket joints <note>original: *nußartige Gelenke*; the author is arguing against a purely mechanical, "clockwork" explanation of elasticity.</note>; for what organization would bring these parts back to their old position without its own inherent motion? Consequently, elasticity seems to have something living <term>lebendig: used here to describe "living force" (vis viva), an active, self-initiating power rather than mere mechanical resistance.</term> within it.

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header># Elasticity and Living Force.</header>

Nothing in nature shows us a better image of force than elasticity, and proves to us more clearly that there is an active substance <note>The author is likely referring to a "vital principle" or a non-material substance that provides energy to the physical world.</note> whose essence is life and independent movement. The spring-force of steel blades, the power of the air, the violence of fire, and the speed of light are phenomena that seem to border entirely on the **realm of spirits**.

Elasticity is, at times, entirely expansive or merely contractive when it is produced solely by expansive or compressive force. But the undivided, simple substance—the true elastic force—is distinguished from these two separated "half-forces" by the fact that it can both expand and narrow itself and all bodies that it animates. It is originally the force of the **Universal Being** <note>original: "Allwesen." This refers to a pantheistic or deistic concept of a supreme, all-encompassing entity or God acting through nature.</note>, applied within nature to preserve and propagate forms.

When combined with **I-ness** <note>original: "Ichheit." A philosophical term for selfhood or the ego.</note> and self-awareness, it becomes the true **living force** <note>term: "lebendige Kraft" (vis viva). In 18th-century physics and philosophy, this referred to the energy of motion, but here it is used to describe the conscious vital energy of a living soul.</note>, which has the two main directions of movement in its power; it directs them as it ought, and can shift the fulcrums of all the levers of the organized body <note>A term commonly used in this period to refer to a living biological organism.</note> that it governs to wherever it wishes.

<header># Elasticity and Living Force.</header>

When one considers what a burden of air an animal body carries from all sides, and what weights it is able to lift with reversed levers, one must marvel at the magnitude of force it is capable of applying and expending. However, this living force expresses itself in a particularly wondrous way in the **throw** <note>Referring to the act of projectile motion or casting an object.</note>, which it knows how to pre-determine in all directions with the most measured strength and accuracy.
