# On the Opposing Forces.

We have already stated that the undivided elastic substance, when considered as the force of motion, possesses two essential primary orientations within itself: one moves from the center toward the periphery—it is expanding and expelling—while the other is narrowing, withdrawing, and acts toward the center.

The former alone, through its expansion, must have stirred the first resistance, and the latter must have been specifically applied to halt and limit it. In this way, from these two motives of movement, the two **half-forces** have arisen, which are called **repulsive** and **compressive** <note>These terms reflect 18th-century "dynamic" physics, which argued that matter is not made of solid atoms, but is a product of balancing attractive and repulsive forces.</note>. Both are joined together in conflict, and both have three types of objects for their effects: these are their own substance, the constituent parts of the body which they fill, and the force opposed to them.

When they move themselves, or the matter in which they are embedded, they always follow their natural direction; the expansive force expands itself and its coverings, and the compressive force narrows itself and all bodies in which it resides. But both

<column-break/>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>On the Opposing Forces.</header>

**expand** themselves when they press against each other, and contract when they are pressed. In both cases, where they are forced to act contrary to their natural direction, their power is strengthened; in the first case, the force is exerted for the struggle; in the latter, it is gathered and consequently increased.

These two opposing forces are not only the internal and external movers of matter—serving as its **primordial substance** <note>original: "Urstoff"</note>—but these forces also, when they are free, connect bodies at a distance, serve and command the senses, and move the great chain of causes and consequences to which everything in the world is bound. 1)

---

1) I suspect that almost all physical and moral phenomena, when they are based on contrasts, have their ground in these opposing forces and could be explained by them.

Might not the two poles of the magnet, as well as so-called **positive and negative electricity**, arise from a mutual accumulation of compressive and expansive force?

Are the two great **opposites** <note>original: "Opposita"</note> of older medicine, the famous **Acid and Alkali** <note>original: "Acidum und Alkali." This refers to iatrochemistry, a school of medicine that explained health and disease through the chemical balance of acidic and alkaline substances.</note>, anything other than an abundance of narrowing and expanding force?

<summary>This page defines the "repulsive" and "compressive" forces as the two fundamental "half-forces" of nature, explaining how their conflict and balance create matter and govern physical phenomena like magnetism and electricity.</summary>
<keywords>opposing forces, repulsive force, compressive force, expansion, contraction, magnetism, electricity, iatrochemistry, acid and alkali, natural philosophy</keywords>

But is there a real **compressive force** in nature? Could what we call by that name not simply be the overwhelming pressure of an **expansive force** directed against another of its kind?

Indeed, both in nature and in art, an upward-striving force is often employed to create a state of pressure.

But if one assumed that one expansive force always pressed against another, there would ultimately have to be something that ended this pressure and held the whole together—a force that creates boundaries, and which can, by its very nature, exist without boundaries. We thus at least recognize the necessity of a force that surrounds everything, and which contains within itself the basis for the possibility of a circle, a form, and a size.

Moreover, it is highly probable that if there is an activity that is always upward-stri-
<meta>catchword: ving</meta>

If there is an essence that exclusively possesses the movement from the center toward the periphery, it seems that there must also be one to which the opposite movement is characteristic, in order to hold together such a fleeting expansion and to set limits to such an unrestricted widening.

We will endeavor to seek out this not-yet-sufficiently recognized essence in nature.

## Fire.

Three substances deserve, above all others, to be distinguished from the sensory world; these are: fire, light, and air. They seem to depart entirely from the nature of matter, being simpler, possessing an inherent motion of their own, and appearing equal to that essence which we call **force** <note>The author uses *Kraft* (force/energy), suggesting these elements are manifestations of pure activity rather than passive matter.</note>.

Fire is certainly no body. It has no form and occupies no specific space. One can hardly measure its power, and never its extension; it cannot be weighed and possesses no gravity—rather the opposite of it—a **levity** <note>original: *Leichtkraft*. In pre-modern science, "levity" was thought to be a distinct force opposite to gravity, causing substances like fire and air to rise naturally.</note>. 1) For its power, driving upwards in all directions, acts from the center toward the periphery.

---

1) Abbot Fontana <note>Felice Fontana (1730–1805) was an Italian physicist and physiologist known for his experiments on gases and the expansion of air.</note> has found that if one heats a hermetically sealed bottle at the bottom, it decreases in weight, and regains it when it becomes cold; if one warms it only at the neck, it loses less of its weight.

<column-break/>

<header>Fire.</header>
<page-num>141</page-num>

We have not the slightest suspicion that it consists of parts of a different nature. The excellent experiments of Becher and Stahl <note>Johann Joachim Becher and Georg Ernst Stahl were the founders of the "Phlogiston" theory, which dominated chemistry before the discovery of oxygen. They believed a fire-element was stored in materials and released during combustion.</note> have shown that the combustible element in all bodies is the same, and that its "shrouds" <note>Hüllen—referring to the external chemical form the combustible element takes.</note> are watery in spirits of wine, but sticky in sulfur, oils, and resins: thus we may assert that it is a simple substance.

Fire is accompanied by various phenomena which we usually attribute to its essence, but which all have their basis in other beings. The glow is presumably caused by light, the hissing by air, and the flame is produced by light, air, and water working together.

If we now wish to form a pure concept of fire, we must separate everything foreign from it, thinking it away, and then nothing remains but its **upward-driving force**, to which all destructive, melting, fermenting, and growing movements that fire produces in nature—and all sensations of the same, which we call heat or warmth—are to be attributed.

This upward-driving force is so characteristic of fire that it cannot move itself in any other way. When it retreats into itself, it hap-
<meta>catchword: pens (geschieht)</meta>

It references 18th-century chemical theories, including Phlogiston and the experiments of Abbé Fontana, to argue that fire is the primary driver of growth, fermentation, and heat in the natural world.</summary>

Since the expansive force always acts from the center toward the periphery; so it is probable that the main gathering place of fire is in the center of creation, just as a particle of it lies locked within every element of matter: It also appears that there is a large reservoir of fire in the center of our globe, and every seed <note>The author uses *Keim* (germ or seed), suggesting a "vital spark" necessary for life.</note> carries a special spark within itself that develops it; for fire is not employed in nature for destruction alone, but it also serves for the origin and growth of all bodies through its **expansive force** <note>original: *aufblähende Kraft*, literally "inflating" or "swelling force."</note>, the mixing of which with the compressive force constitutes the play of all nature.

---

2) The fable of Prometheus and the representations that most nations have made of the hellish deities agree with the conjecture that the first **rebellion** <note>original: *Empörung*. This likely refers to a primordial or spiritual uprising against the original order of the universe.</note> occurred through the power of fire.

<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>Fire. 143</header>

To such orderly purposes is fire—harmful in itself—set in conflict with a force contrary to it and enclosed in circular laws or forms, the sum of which constitutes the **corporeal world**.

Fire lies deeply locked within most bodies, extremely divided, and so greatly compressed that it has, as it were, lost its energy thereby.

The simplest and most common means by which fire can be produced without the addition of another fire is **friction**: — a species of **stimulus** <note>original: *Reiz*. In the 18th century, "stimulus" was a key concept in both medicine and physics to explain how dormant forces are awakened.</note>; for friction is nothing other than an oft-repeated pressure of two bodies, which gives back their energy to the particles of fire resting in both. When the friction becomes so strong that it attacks the surfaces of the bodies, it destroys the outermost part of the bonds which enclose the stimulated fire particles gathered toward the periphery, and consequently acts even more directly upon their liberation.

However, extremely scattered fire particles would not gather after obtaining freedom, nor act upon the body that was their prison, but would instead fly away through their essentially upward-driving direction, if the compressive power that prevails in the air did not drive them back and hold them together,

Heat, rest, water, and air are the four main requirements that cause fermentation, and without which it cannot exist.

The author refers to the 18th-century concept that heat was composed of physical particles of fire.</note> is developed to such an extent that they begin to exceed the resistance of water and air, we have **spiritual fermentation** <note>original: *geistige Gährung*. This refers to what we now call alcoholic fermentation, which produces "spirits."</note>, which is the first degree of the process.

This process halts if the fermentation is interrupted by motion or cold; in this way, a cooling and entirely non-spiritual substance can appear and maintain itself as spirituous. This happens because the fire-particles, which were previously deeply locked within, now cling to the surface of the fermenting body and affect our senses through their outer shell. # The Air.

**Air** seems to surround everything and, through its exceptional **extensibility** <note>original: *Dehnbarkeit*. This refers to the elastic nature of air—its ability to expand and fill any space.</note>, represents the most beautiful image of immensity. It connects heaven with earth and the souls of men with one another. Within it lie all the secrets of language, of sound, and of the harmonic **art of music**. It gives a body to thoughts, and every thing has a sound that calls out its name to us through it.

All these magnificent properties strike me when my mere imagination occupies itself with air; however, in order to form as correct a concept of a thing as possible, I am accustomed to thinking away from it everything that does not essentially belong to it—all foreign matter that might be contained within it.

Thus, if I separate from the air all particles of earth, fire, and water that float within it, all properties of heat, cold, and moisture, dryness and heaviness—because these are all for the most part worked into it by other beings—nothing remains that is with certainty entirely its own, except for a [motion] toward all direc... <note>The catchword "tungen" suggests the next page begins with "Richtungen" (directions).</note>

[...-tions] <note>This completes the word "directions" (Richtungen) from the previous page's catchword.</note> of elastic pressure going in all directions. The actual nature of tone and speech are still such hidden secrets that we cannot claim with certainty whether air is their acting cause—hardly even whether it is intended to conduct them; for sound is propagated more powerfully through the earth, if one places one's ear upon it, than through the air. There are bodies that ring out more than others; and a deaf person hears through the vibration of his teeth.

The new and beautiful discoveries of our century regarding the various **types of air** should have fairly led us primarily to the investigation of the *essence* of air. For a long time, however, people have only occupied themselves with the nomenclatures of the many kinds of **fixed air** <note>original: *fixer Luft*. In 18th-century chemistry, "fixed air" referred to carbon dioxide (CO2).</note>, which are nothing more than pure air enveloped in parts of those bodies that it permeated.

Following this, other types of air were also noticed, measured, christened, and assigned as the primary types of air, without noticing that only the majority of fire and water makes up these differences, and without specifically tracking the unknown essence of air, which deserves to be discovered above all else. Only **Ingenhousz** <note>Jan Ingenhousz (1730–1799) was a Dutch scientist famous for discovering photosynthesis and his work on the chemistry of gases.</note> has conjectured that all salt is air and only differs according to its base.

## Heat and Cold.

It is denied that cold is something real, and it is asserted that the mere absence of fire causes it. I answer: Since we do not yet know the internal essence of fire, we have the same right to deny heat as we do cold. Indeed, one could also say: the natural state of all things is fluid, and the petrifying, contracting force of cold is that true substance whose absence constitutes all appearances of heat. The equal possibility of the existence of two opposing beings seems to me to give permission to accept or reject either of them.

It is asserted: Solidity <note>original: *Festigkeit*</note> is the natural state of all things, and fire makes them more or less fluid—that water was originally ice—and that <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> was *fixed* <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, "fixing" mercury meant turning the liquid metal into a solid state.</note>. I answer: 1) That a pronouncement about the original state of two equally possible conditions is just as daring as deciding the question: whether sand is crushed glass, or

<header>Heat and Cold.</header>

...whether glass is molten sand; or likewise, whether the chicken species began with an egg or a hen? 2) that, when discussing the origin of such things which essentially intertwine with one another, it is easier for me to conceive of both together than one without the other. Thus, I am inclined to believe that in the beginning, there were both fluid and solid matters, just as there are today. If cold were a mere negation <note>The author is arguing against the "privation theory," which states that cold is not a thing itself, but simply what remains when heat is removed.</note> and not a force, how could it so powerfully drive back flames and all expressions of fire? One can certainly feel the elastic pressure of cold air when it streams through an opening into a warm place. The violence of this pressure is always in proportion to the coldness. Would not the fire stream out into the cold air if the air were without force and merely "empty of fire"?

The flame of a candle standing in the doorway between two rooms of unequal warmth is visibly repelled by the colder atmosphere. Why does the force of fire not strive, as it usually does, toward where there is a lack of fire-particles? Or why would the flame not at least remain standing upright, if the resistance it encountered were not active?

<column-break/>

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>Heat and Cold.</header>

These phenomena are commonly explained by the tendency of fluid bodies to place themselves in equilibrium. 1) But is this "tendency" not a force, and in these cited cases, is the striving force not entirely on the side of the cold?

The following

---

1) Regarding the equilibrium of fluids, I must still mention that I believe it is not the impulse of a special force moving in a horizontal direction, but rather the effect of gravity <note>original: *Schwerkraft*</note> in a matter whose parts are extremely small and of the same measure and weight. The equilibrium in which the air seeks to maintain itself seems to me to result more from the weight of the aqueous vapors within it than from the forces that alternately contract or expand it. For the actual thinning and thickening of the air—that which we call heat and cold—are two forces that indeed mesh together because they struggle against one another; but they never reconcile. We know of no universal measure that determines a degree one could call "temperature," and between the ice point <note>The freezing point of water.</note> and the degree of boiling water, there is a constant decrease or increase; thus, it is always the opposite of an equilibrium. These two opposing forces of nature can never be brought into a quiet state of balance; they remain always in conflict together until one of the two must flee.

# Heat and Cold.

The following experiment has been performed in Switzerland: Ice was placed at the focal point of a <term>concave mirror [Hohlspiegel]</term>, and at a second focal point opposite to it, a <term>thermometer</term> was placed, which immediately dropped. Would this not be clear proof that cold must be a powerful <term>substance [Substanz]</term>, because it casts rays just like fire? <note>This describes a famous 18th-century experiment by Marc-Auguste Pictet. At the time, it seemed to prove that "cold" was a physical fluid that could be reflected and focused, though we now know the thermometer was simply radiating its own heat toward the ice.</note>

Cold interrupts <term>fermentation [Gährung]</term> and <term>putrefaction [Fäulniß]</term>. Would the <term>fire-particles [Feuertheile]</term> <note>The author is likely referring to "caloric" or "phlogiston," hypothetical substances that early scientists believed carried heat.</note> not continue their work of destruction if a power superior to them did not prevent it? One must not believe that they have passed out of the body into a fire-void <term>atmosphere</term> out of a love for equilibrium—they have merely hidden themselves; for after the cold departs, they immediately begin to act again.

Finally, if solidity and cold were without force, then a body expanded by heat would have to remain at the same degree of expansion after the departure of the <term>fire-particles</term>; its parts would have to stand still in the state in which the fire left them. However, I see that this body contracts when it freezes: I see a movement opposite to the expanding force, and from this I conclude that it must be the effect of a <term>compressive</term> force.

---

<page-num>159</page-num>
# Heat and Cold.

Should someone tell me that this receding movement is grounded in the nature of bodies, I answer: Thus, the <term>compressive [compressive]</term> force is indeed contained within bodies by nature.

The objection—that the <term>compressive</term> force does not always cause cold—is quite correct: but the <term>expansive</term> force also does not always cause heat; for both forces manifest these effects, especially in the air, only through the medium of such particles that are analogous to them and act upon our sense of touch.

It seems as if heat and cold are only relative effects of opposing forces—unnatural contractions or expansions of our <term>fluids [Säfte]</term> <note>The original term "Säfte" refers to the "humors" or vital fluids of the body, reflecting the era's medical understanding.</note>—mere sensations. However, there are degrees of heat and frost that are stronger than we are capable of feeling. The intensity of these forces is perhaps infinitely increasable. But whether the cold be felt or not, it is always a contraction, and the latter is an effect of the <term>compressive</term> force, which must be a <term>substance</term> because it can move itself and other bodies. Consequently, the <term>substance</term> of the <term>compressive</term> force is that which produces the cold, and in this sense, it is the <term>principle [Princip]</term> of cold.

## Salt.

All <term>fixed salts [fixe Salze]</term>—and there are no others, for they only become volatile through <term>fire-particles [Feuertheile]</term> that carry them upward—always act compressively. Whether they are harsh and astringent, they constrict; if they produce an artificial cold, they likewise constrict; and if they are caustic, they act from the surface toward the center. Thus, in all these cases, the compressive force seems uniquely inherent to salt.

Air <term>crystallizes [schießt an]</term> in the form of a salt called <term>saltpeter [Salpeter]</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder, which was often thought to contain "condensed" air or spirit.</note>; when this is distilled, it transforms back into the purest air. Salt appears to be a condensed air, and air a diluted salt. 1)

I believe this effect occurs as follows: The saltpeter is [affected] by a surrounding fire...

<header>Salt.</header>

The inner essence of salt is therefore presumably an <term>aerial acid [Luftsäure]</term> <note>At the time of this writing, "aerial acid" was a term often used for carbon dioxide (fixed air), but here the author uses it more broadly to describe a universal acidic principle found in the atmosphere.</note>, which produces all types of salt through the variety of their <term>bases [Basis]</term> <note>In chemistry, a base is the substance that reacts with an acid to form a salt.</note>. Whether the simple aerial acid without a base is the same thing as air, or only a constituent part of it? One could perhaps defend both positions; for until now, we have been unable to discover anything essential in the air that joins with the acid, other than water and fire: now, both of these are things unto themselves and not "air"; thus, we can claim either that the latter is itself this acid, or that it consists of water, fire, and an acid.

The great primary question is therefore the following: whether air is a compound, or whether it is a simple force?

<meta>catchword: At</meta>

<note>Footnote continuing from previous page</note>

...freed from its earthly bonds. The aerial acid immediately pursues the fire in the direction in which it flees, and the more it outweighs the fire, the faster its flight, and the more strongly it acts upon the air and all bodies that come in its way. The power of <term>saltpeter [Salpeter]</term> <note>Potassium nitrate.</note> is thus an effect of fire strengthened by a force inherent to the saltpeter that is opposed to fire. It is, so to speak, the drive of a <term>fire-pump [Feuerpumpe]</term> <note>An early term for a steam engine or a thermal expansion device.</note> which has been reinforced by the drive of an air-pump.

<header>Salt.</header>

At first glance, one might almost believe that air could be the simple substance of <term>elasticity [Elasticität]</term>, because it seems to possess the double movement of force. However, if we examine it closely, we find that it possesses these properties only passively and not actively; it does not expand itself, it is expanded; — it does not contract itself, it is contracted. Heat and cold are accustomed to producing these effects within it. I believe we can view air like a body that has two souls. Water is this body, and the two opposing forces animate it. I would almost like to call the air an image of the human will, which determines itself with roughly the same freedom as the air moves; just as the air, driven about by winds, strives for equilibrium, so the will seeks rest, and finds it only when a superior weight pulls the will toward itself.

Furthermore, if we go through the types of air known to us, we find that they are all composite:

1) The <term>dephlogisticated [dephlogisticirte]</term> <note>This refers to oxygen. In the "phlogiston" theory of the 18th century, substances that burned well were thought to be free of phlogiston (dephlogisticated), allowing them to "soak up" more of it from burning materials.</note> is the purest we know. It contains much aerial acid, little water, and almost no fire. Saltpeter can be almost entirely transformed into it. However, it can also be prepared from many other bodies through the separation of <term>combustible matter [brennbarer Stoff]</term> <note>Also known as phlogiston.</note>.

<sig>L 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: 2) The</meta>

<summary>This page explores the chemical composition of air, debating whether it is a simple element or a compound of "aerial acid," water, and fire. The author uses mechanical and psychological analogies to describe air's elasticity and identifies "dephlogisticated air" (oxygen) as its purest form.</summary>

2) <term>Flammable air [brennbare Luft]</term> <note>Now known as hydrogen.</note> is the opposite of <term>dephlogisticated air [dephlogistisirte]</term> <note>Now known as oxygen; in the phlogiston theory, this was air capable of absorbing the "fire-substance" from burning materials.</note>; for it consists of many fire-parts, some water, and very little <term>aerial acid [Luftsäure]</term> <note>A term often used for carbon dioxide or the acidic principle found in the atmosphere.</note>.

If one puts the two aforementioned types of air together and ignites the flammable air, a conflict arises in which both drop the water contained within them; and this is actually the fundamental cause of the experiment by <term>Lavoisier</term> <note>Antoine Lavoisier (1743–1794), the father of modern chemistry, who demonstrated in 1783 that water was a compound of hydrogen and oxygen, rather than a simple element.</note>.

3) <term>Mephitic air [Mephytische Luft]</term> <note>Nitrogen, or air that no longer supports life or combustion.</note> is the foulest and coarsest. It contains much water and earthy parts, much flammable and some <term>fixed air [fixe Luft]</term> <note>Carbon dioxide.</note>, but very little aerial acid. The more of the latter is added to it, the more harmless and elastic it becomes.

4) Atmospheric air holds the middle ground between the mephitic and the dephlogisticated; it leans now toward one, now toward the other, depending on whether it is corrupted by flammable vapors or overly harsh acids, or refreshed by aerial acid.

5) Fixed air, which should rather be called "that which has been fixed," is actually pure air that was woven into the structure of a body, 2) and which,

2) The fixed air contained within bodies seems to me to be the main cause of their <term>elasticity [Elasticität]</term>,

or at least to contribute infinitely much to it.

Water, when it freezes, mixes with <term>aerial salt [Luftsalz]</term>, which is basically pure air, and the ice becomes elastic because of it. Likewise, when one turns iron into steel, the former is driven apart by fire-parts, and this space is afterwards filled by the air contained in the water. The colder the water, the more powerful is the air within it and the better the steel becomes. It is the common opinion that the hardening of steel is a contraction of the iron; however, I have learned from an iron rod precisely fitted into a brass measure that the hardening has lengthened it. Ice, too, increases its volume, even though it was created by a contracting force. These phenomena cannot be explained otherwise than by the quantity of air that has entered the steel and the water.

Frozen water contains infinitely more air than boiling water. This is yet another proof that the power of cold is connected with the air.

Experiments on the amount of air contained in elastic and non-elastic bodies would perhaps correct my proposition—that air constitutes the <term>resilience [Schnellkraft]</term> of these bodies; but they

# Salt.

All types of <term>fixed air</term> <note>original: "fixer Luft" — At the time, this specifically referred to carbon dioxide, as it was "fixed" within solid minerals like limestone.</note> differ from one another through the variety of these salt-parts, and relate to <term>dephlogisticated air</term> <note>Oxygen; in phlogiston theory, this was air that had been "cleared" of the fire-substance phlogiston.</note> as the acid combined with them relates to <term>aerial acid</term> <note>original: "Luftsäure" — Often used to describe carbon dioxide or the general acidic principle found in the atmosphere.</note>. However, they contain even more fire, water, and earthy parts than dephlogisticated air, and are consequently all composite.
<meta>catchword: We</meta>

---

...could also lead astray, because the internal weaving of the air with the structure of bodies can hinder its <term>elastic force</term> <note>Pressure or tension.</note> in quite different ways. The brittleness of the parts enclosing them and the excessive fragmentation of the air can completely nullify its effect. This latter circumstance is found in water, in which the air is fragmented to the extreme; for this reason, the elasticity in water is so imperceptible that it has been denied to it altogether. If water exerts a greater effect in the form of vapors than air does, this occurs through the <term>repulsive property</term> of fire in the following manner: The water is dissolved into its smallest constituent parts, which are those that come closest to the material elements—the smallest and most impenetrable circles in organized nature, which fire, though it tears apart almost all forms, cannot harm. This cloud, combined with the elastic force of air, possesses a firmness that can be compared with no other body; it is pushed forward by the expelling force of fire and is perhaps the strongest and most appropriate armor that the latter can find in matter to contend physically.

<column-break/>

# Salt.

We have thus seen that all types of air we have been able to identify are composed of fire, water, and a salt. We know of no type of air that is entirely fire or water, or entirely without both; yet here, the salt plays the leading role and appears to be the simple binding agent that forms the air through fire and water. But since I have shown that air is the constricting binding agent of all bodies, and that all salt primarily exhibits the properties of compression, I believe I have found in the pure <term>air-salt</term> <note>A theoretical substance the author believes is the source of all other salts and the essence of physical contraction.</note>, from which all others descend, that substance to which <term>compressive power</term> belongs just as exclusively as <term>expansive power</term> belongs to fire.

However, since this salt does not seem to be entirely simple in regard to its acidity, it would be desirable if we could fathom its inner nature and discover the essential simple substance of compressive force.

## Light.

Light seems to have a close kinship with the air, and to be always more or less connected with it.

The same degree of heat that would set a body in flames in the open air will burn it to powder in sealed vessels without leaving a trace of light to be seen. In this latter case, nothing is missing but the air, which has been entirely driven out of the sealed glasses by the force of the fire; we must therefore seek the cause of the <term>shining</term> <note>original: "Leuchtens" — the quality of emitting light during combustion.</note> within the air itself.

On the other hand, it should be well noted that the light prevailing in the air stands in the most perfect proportion to the <term>aerial acid</term> <note>original: "Luftsäure" — In the late 18th century, this term was often used for carbon dioxide (fixed air), but here the author associates it with the component of air that supports combustion and brilliance, closer to the modern concept of oxygen.</note>. The purer, colder, and more <term>elastic</term> <note>original: "elastischer" — referring to the pressure and expansive force of the gas.</note> the air is, the stronger and whiter is the light it imparts. In a dry cold, lanterns shine at their brightest, while in a very warm room, candles burn dimly.

The coarsest and most impure of all types of air is the <term>mephitic</term> <note>original: "mephytische" — noxious or "deadly" air that does not support life or fire, typically nitrogen or carbon dioxide.</note>; it has the least elasticity and

---

<page-num>169</page-num>
<header>Light.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>
L 5
<meta>catchword: her (part of "ihr")</meta>

contains the least aerial acid within it. If one dips a torch into it, it is extinguished, for such air cannot nourish the light—rather, it swallows it up because it lacks it, and the fire dissipates into smoke because there is no compressing force present that could press the fire against the body of the torch.

Conversely, if one places a glowing metal into <term>dephlogisticated air</term> <note>Oxygen. In the "phlogiston" theory of the time, fire was a material substance called phlogiston. Air that was "dephlogisticated" was thought to be empty of this substance and thus able to absorb it greedily from burning objects.</note>, its sparks spread the most magnificent glow. 1)

It is indeed claimed that the light phenomenon of <term>nitrous air</term> <note>original: "Salpeterluft" — likely referring to nitrogen dioxide or a similar gas produced from saltpeter.</note> is to be attributed to the outflow of fire into the dephlogisticated vapors, which attract it. However, a powerful proof that the fire in dephlogisticated air does not come from the air itself—

---

1) The explanation of the so-called *Argand lamps* <note>original: "Lampes d'Argant" — A major 1780s invention by Aimé Argand. It used a circular wick and a glass chimney to create a draft, significantly increasing the brightness and efficiency of the flame.</note> rests upon this principle; in these lamps, the air—compressed through a glass channel open at the top and bottom—always flows closely around the flame, and with the acid contained within it, nourishes and increases the shining.

In the open and especially in cold air, these lamps burn best and give the whitest light; in rooms, however, they soon lose this quality; their flame becomes darker and redder in a short time because they consume the elasticity of the atmosphere far more than other lights do.

From all this, we see that light is particularly related to **aerial acid** <note>original: "Luftsäure" — a term used by 18th-century chemists like Scheele to describe the component of air that supports combustion, often linked to the modern concept of oxygen or carbon dioxide depending on the context of "compression."</note>, and since I have shown in the previous article how excellently this acid possesses the <term>compressive force</term> <note>original: "compressive Kraft" — the power to pack matter together or exert pressure.</note>, I now wish to demonstrate that this force belongs more originally to no being than to light, in order to justify the suspicion I have: whether aerial acid might not be a light specified for all material compressions?

If fire is not a matter composed of parts, then light certainly deserves the same distinction; even less than fire does it have a measure, a form, or a weight. It does not even have a shell that one could regard as a component of it, and it possesses the most peculiar character of a spiritual force: to act always in a straight line; a formal contradiction to material bodies, which are all formed in purely curved lines; salts alone excepted, whose formation is worked through pure rays of forces.

---

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>Light.</header>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

If the strength of force consists in the speed of movement, then no other is to be compared with light; its course is four hundred times faster than a cannonball, and only the force of the soul, in view of the speed of our thoughts, could compete with its own.

It is also, by its higher descent, of a nobler nature than all substances we know, and the most sublime comparisons of our eloquence are taken from light and its properties.

When I now consider that in the beginning a compressive force was necessary to limit the first resistance, and that afterwards one had to exist to hold the whole of the world together; so I find no other substance to which these advantages could be more justly attributed than to light. It is the only one whose source lies outside our <term>atmosphere</term> <note>original: "Dunstkreis" — literally "vapor-circle," the contemporary term for the layer of air surrounding the Earth.</note>; — the most distant thing we know, and consequently that which deserves the privilege of encompassing everything.

As long, therefore, as our senses do not discover a being whose properties are more fitting for universal compression than the properties of light; so I shall follow the rules of probability, and attribute the primal force of compression rather to the known light, than to [the...]

The **compressive force** <note>A central concept in this text, referring to an external pressure that keeps matter and celestial systems bound together.</note> presumably surrounds the atmospheres <note>original: "Dunstkreise" — literally "vapor circles," the contemporary term for atmospheres.</note> of all planets and suns, encloses our and all world-systems, and borders on the essence of immensity: perhaps it causes the rolling rotation of all planets through its friction against the upward-striving counter-pressure of moving spheres, and perhaps one could explain the entire course of the celestial bodies from the **action and reaction of fire and light** with fewer objections than can be made against the system of **attractive force** <note>The author is challenging the Newtonian theory of gravity (attractive force) by suggesting a model based on the pressure of light and fire.</note>; especially if one wished to view gravity as a relative force of bodies toward the center of the Earth and not as a general property of matter.

It seems to us, indeed, as if light does not surround our world-system but flows out from its center, the sun. However, if **expansive force** is fire and essentially cannot act otherwise than from a center, then it is probable that that center,

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

->Light.<-

which we call the **Sun**, is the primary gathering place of fire. Were this fire held together by nothing, however, it would spread out in all directions and dissipate; thus, it must be hemmed in spherically by the **compressive force** distributed in the vast region of the air, which crowds together around its entire periphery, consequently becoming immensely visible, shooting off in rays from the same, and presenting the luminous image of the sun to our eyes.

A similar phenomenon occurs on a small scale with fire. When it is freed from its bonds and burns, all the light particles scattered in that region of the air gather around it, so that they might fulfill the calling of their destiny: to contest the upward-striving violence of the fire. Through their constriction, they are heaped around the flame, bounce back in the form of rays from the impenetrable resistance of the fire, and become visible. In this way, fire, through the pressure of the compressive force, creates the **illusion** in our eyes as if it were shining.

One has much difficulty tearing oneself away from the preconceived and deep-rooted opinion that light is a property of fire, and especially of **solar fire**. For a short time now, people have begun to doubt the heat of the sun's rays; however, the **Arabs**, who brought the sciences back to Europe, already held the opinion that light is distinct from fire. Just as heat is the most perceptible property of fire, they believed that cold must also have its own essence: they attributed it to light, and they accepted as a known truth of their physics at the time that the sun's rays are cold. 2)

But now I must cite some reasons why I regard light as a substance very different from fire:

How can one simultaneously attribute to fire—which always expels—the contradictory ability to withdraw of its own accord, which cannot be denied to light? For if one allows sunbeams to fall through a small opening into a dark room that is thereby illuminated, it becomes dark instantly as soon as the hole is plugged with a finger. No matter how quick one might be, the rays cannot be cut off; they slip away and withdraw.

The flame of a candle has a kind of **prismatic arrangement of colors**, according to which the blue appears at the bottom of the wick, while the whitish-yellow appears at the outer edge of the flame and at the very top.

In the same order, the **light ray** also falls into the dark, upon an oblique surface; the blue is the end of its tip, and the yellow the beginning of its **dissection** <term>original: "Zergliederung" — referring here to the dispersion or prismatic separation of light into colors</term>. If light emanated from fire, then the innermost part of the flame would have to be white, and the exterior blue; however, it is exactly the opposite, from which one can infer that light takes its direction *toward* the flame, and not *out* of the flame.

Mr. Bailli <note>Jean Sylvain Bailly (1736–1793) was a prominent French astronomer and orator who corresponded with Voltaire. He is best known for his *History of Astronomy* and for his tragic role in the French Revolution as the first Mayor of Paris.</note>, in his letters written to **Voltaire** in a thoroughly Voltairean style, has collected many proofs that the heat attributed to the sun by no means generates the difference of the seasons and the terrestrial temperatures as directly as is commonly believed. This man, famous for his great fate <note>A reference to Bailly's eventual execution by guillotine during the Reign of Terror.</note> and for his excellent *History of Astronomy*, has shown in the most probable manner how the fire circulating in the interior of the earth can produce all local changes of heat and frost through its approach or withdrawal.

But when I combine the system of **compressive force** acting between the Sun and planets with this subterranean fire, I can explain the alternation of the seasons and local temperatures even more clearly:

<column-break/>

<page-num>177</page-num>
->Light.<-

Since the sun is the main gathering place of fire, around which the greatest quantity of light must therefore crowd (as I have shown above), all the light rays which are called **sunbeams** emanate from this circle. These act with their **compressing power** upon the **fire particles** floating in the **atmosphere** <term>original: "Dunstkreise" — literally "vapor-sphere," the common 18th-century term for the atmosphere</term>, and press them more or less strongly down against the surface of the earth, in proportion to their straight or oblique direction; for in the height of summer, these rays fall quite perpendicularly under the **meridian** <term>original: "Mittagslinie" — literally "noon-line," referring here to the equator or the path where the sun is highest</term>, consequently they then most constrict the fire particles of the atmosphere; the latter become more effective through the duration and intensity of their constriction, and for this reason it is warmest under the line <note>The "line" refers to the Equator.</note> in summer, just as it is coldest at the poles in winter for the opposite reason.

Yet the local approach or withdrawal of the subterranean fire, the winds, and the mountains, create great exceptions, or rather variations, in this general proportion; they are the reasons why it can sometimes be warmer in April than in July at the same location, and why one region is colder than...

3) On Lake Como, in August, at the place called *l’Orrido di Bellano* <note>The "Gorge of Bellano," a natural canyon and waterfall in Italy</note>, I felt the cold of winter; and yet, a few thousand paces further at Varena <note>Varenna, a town on Lake Como</note>, I saw an **Aloe** blooming in the open air. Let someone explain these contradictions, placed so close together, through the common doctrine of solar heat alone—without admitting that cold is something real! 

But if I assume that a **subterranean fire** beneath Varena comes to the aid of the sun's effects and serves as a hotbed for this Aloe—and that at Bellano, conversely, a substance of cold, streaming out from the chasm of the *Fiume Latte* <note>A short, cold river near Bellano known for its milky appearance during snowmelt</note> (formed by the melting of glaciers), drives all heat away from this dark depression—then I am no longer surprised by this rare neighborliness of winter and summer.

valleys, and that the aforementioned depths must remain at a certain temperature in all seasons because they are protected both against the fire particles compressed on the surface of the earth, as well as against the winds and the free influence of **air acid** <term>original: "Luftsäure" — an archaic chemical term from the late 18th century, often referring to oxygen or carbon dioxide, then thought to be a component of "vital air"</term>.

Those who must doubt the warmth of the sun's rays the most are our modern **aeronauts** <note>original: "Luftfahrer" — a reference to early hot-air and gas balloonists, a sensation in the late 1700s</note>, who run an ever-greater risk of freezing the closer they come to the sun; or, to speak more correctly, the further they move away from the earth and its fire.

Finally, I must also explain how a system so contrary to the preconceived general conviction regarding the heat of light rays can be reconciled with the experiences of the **burning mirror**. The matter proceeds in the following way:

All the fire particles contained in the column of air resting upon the surface of the **burning glass** are pressed down perpendicularly onto this surface and are there drawn together into an extremely sharp point in a conical shape. This point is called the **focal point** <note>original: "Brennpunkt"</note>. The more vertical the sun and the larger the mirror, the more light rays are collected, and the greater is their effect.

This point can act with infinitely more violence than common fire-power, because of the...

-># Light.<-

...strength originating from the speed of light, and because of the pressure of an extremely sharp cone—which is even more piercing in fire than in a physical, material cone, as we perceive in the astonishing power of a blown flame <note>The author likely refers to a blowpipe used in chemistry or glassblowing, which intensifies a flame's heat</note>, which does not act nearly as quickly, straightly, and steadily as the **cone of fire** driven by light in the **focal point** <note>original: "Brennpunkte" — the point where light rays converge through a lens</note>.

All these probabilities and reasons taken together have moved me, after many doubts, to originally attribute **compressive force** to light. Since I encounter such force preeminently in **hydrochloric acids** <note>original: "Salzsäuren" — in the 18th century, acids were often thought to contain a concentrated "acidifying principle" related to light or heat</note>, and since I have shown how closely light is related to the **purest air** <note>An early term for oxygen</note>, and that glowing is particularly characteristic of the latter: I therefore venture to repeat that the newly-born and well-baptized **Oxygen**, whose inner essence is unknown to us, could be a **light** specified for material compression through its acidic envelope.

Why should we now prefer to call this pure principle "air" rather than "light," since we have already assigned the former name to a substance we know, and which is indisputably not a simple force but a composition? Do we find in nature, besides fire, any other simple substance than light to which we could attribute the essence of the second primary force?

<meta>catchword: How</meta>

---

<page-num>181</page-num>
-># Light.<-

How does it happen, however, that light can dwell in the assigned form of an **atmospheric acid** <note>original: "Luftsäure" — likely referring to a gaseous state of an acid or a specific gas like carbon dioxide or oxygen</note> without shining, that it can be present even at night, and that it can remain invisible in the air?

Recall what I said above in the article on **opposing forces** <note>The author's theory of dualistic forces governing the universe</note>: "that both
»expand when they press, and
»contract when they are pressed."
The sensation we have of light is the effect of its **elastic pressure** on our eyes. No pressure exists without an expansion: therefore, when light is challenged to combat by an exposed fire, it must move by expanding—not only to seek out the fire, but also to gather around it and enclose it.

Then, not only does its image become visible at the perimeter of the fire, but its rays also illuminate the surrounding objects, because our eyes are pressed by the elastic force of the light moving in expansion. Conversely: when it narrows, it ceases to act pressingly upon our sight, and then we say: it becomes dark.

The natural state of compressive force is narrowing; thus light, by its nature, is closed within itself and invisible, only a...

<header>Light.</header>

...an unnatural direction compels it to shine. We should wonder more that we see it than that we do not see it.

No representation expresses the unity of the **primal force** <note>original: "Urkraft" — a central concept in German Romantic science referring to a single, underlying energy from which all others derive</note> and its separation into two primary forces more perfectly or tangibly than my hypothesis regarding the separation of light from fire and their opposing directions. Both are so closely intertwined that they are mistaken for one, thereby providing an image of the original unity of force. Both are the only simple substances known to our senses, and the only ways we can imagine the true nature of a force and a simple, immaterial being. Therefore, if it is true that motion consists of two opposing directions, and that all of nature is driven by the two primary forces of **expansion** and **compression**, we can only attribute these to the two unique substances whose simplicity is known to us—especially since the one [fire] evidently manifests its expansive power, while the other [light] most likely deserves to possess the power of compression.

# On Gravity.

The **attraction** <note>original: "Attraction"</note> so successfully calculated and so ingeniously applied by *Newton* is likely nothing other than gravity; for the cause of **gravitation** <note>original: "Gravitation"</note> seems to me to lie in the body being attracted, rather than in the attracting body.

I cannot understand how one has preferred to attribute the movement of one body primarily to the immobility of another, or why one has sought the cause of its movement far outside of itself rather than within it. If we see someone walking into the city, it is surely more natural to say that he goes there because he has business to attend to, rather than that the city attracts him: Only a poet might be permitted to speak in such a way.

Why should the larger body attract the smaller? How could it do so? Does it have arms or returning effluvia <note>original: "Ausströmungen" — a term often used in older physics to describe invisible flows or "ethers" that supposedly carried forces</note>? How can one suspect force to exist primarily where one perceives immobility? And yet the 

<summary>This page contains a table of contents for a section on material forces and begins a critique of Isaac Newton's theory of attraction. The author argues that gravity is an internal property of the "attracted" object rather than a force exerted by a larger external body.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, Isaac Newton, attraction, gravitation, action at a distance, inertia, stimulus, physics, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the Newtonian concept of external "attraction." On the previous page, the author compared a falling body to a person walking to a city—moving by their own internal will or business rather than being "pulled" by the destination. Here, he proposes an internal "drive for union" as the true cause of gravity.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
<header>On Gravity.</header>

...obscure idea of an unthinkable force of attraction has nested itself in our brain and favored other erroneous secondary concepts.

I will attempt to see if one cannot provide a probable cause for why a smaller body has the tendency to approach a larger one.

This tendency is based, in my opinion, on the original unity of the elastic force, which has been separated by the power of circles and forms, and has retained the urge to restore the unity of its first nature.

The drive for union is the law of all forces. In matter, it is called <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "Schwerkraft." The author views gravity not as an external pull, but as an internal desire of "imprisoned" forces to rejoin a larger whole.</note>. The divided force, which is imprisoned within the elements of matter, as well as all other forces woven into the form of a body, have a sensing feel for the nature and quantity of the external forces that approach them, and the urge to unite with those of their own kind when the quantity of the latter is greater than their own. Every particle of these forces scattered within a body extends in the direction of the straightest line that can be drawn from it to the center of the body it seeks. These forces, gathered as if in a cone and striving toward a goal,

---

<page-num>187</page-num>
<header>On Gravity.</header>

...push against the circumference of the form that encloses them, and since they cannot escape, they pull this form along in the intended direction until it touches the body they strive for, because it contains a larger portion of their essence. This larger part remains immobile, however, because every particle of it cannot leave the greater quantity similar to it to seek out a lesser one; for a complete union would be impossible if the smaller parts did not have to join the main collections.

I would therefore define gravity as: the endeavor of an originally simple force—one which has been violently divided and enclosed within a form—to unite itself by the shortest path with the greatest quantity of its essence.

If one spins around in a circle for a long time and quickly, one loses one's balance and falls over. I believe I have found in this phenomenon a proof that forces, and not parts of the body, constitute what we call weight. The centrifugal force <note>original: "Fliehkraft"</note> from the center (**centrifugal force** <note>original Latin: "vis centrifuga"</note>) indeed has the power to drive the heaviest parts of a body toward the circumference; but only if they are mobile. Now, however,

# On Gravity. #

...do not throw the parts of our body from one side to the other, and yet it seems as if the other half becomes heavier when we turn around in a circle for a long time and quickly; we fall over, and always outside the circle—never into it: thus we can well assume that these are forces that move across into our body and turn the direction of their gravity toward the circumference <note>The author is describing centrifugal force. He suggests that the "force" within the body shifts its orientation toward the outside of the curve during rotation.</note>.

That gravity must increase in proportion to the size and density of a falling body is easy to understand, because these two properties presuppose a greater quantity of divided force. But whence comes the ever-increasing speed or strength of the fall in proportion to the height?

Since the drive of divided forces is union, bodies seek to touch each other in as many points as their figure allows. When one body falls upon another, it first aligns itself on its heaviest side and seeks to make the form of the other as flat and even as its durability permits, in order to unite with it across a greater extent. This endeavor I call <term>pressure</term> <note>original: "Druck"</note>.

When pressure and counter-pressure are equal to each other and no part of these two bodies [changes its] place

<meta>catchword: changes</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>
# On Gravity. #

changes, this is called <term>rest</term> <note>original: "Ruhe." In this context, rest is not a lack of force, but an equilibrium of opposing pressures.</note>. It is the most common state of lifeless matter, and pressure is the common measure of the energy of the fire imprisoned within matter <note>The author likely refers to "fire" in the sense of a fundamental, active element or "caloric" that provides the internal energy for matter to act.</note>. Extremely divided and weakened by long compression, it uses only as much of its force as is necessary to achieve the inner state of rest.

The fall takes its beginning from pressure: As soon as the resistance upon which a body presses is removed, the counter-action of the underlying body ceases, and the force already directed downward becomes freer, consequently stronger; it pushes its enclosing form along this line of direction; and since it no longer feels the accustomed counter-pressure, it gains a greater sensation of freedom at every point of advancement, and this effects a stronger drive toward a swifter union. Every successful doubling of speed reinforces the illusion that it has lost the material bonds that fetter it, and its energy grows until it is stopped once again.

This may well be the main cause why the fall becomes faster in proportion to the height; yet the pressure of the ever-lengthening column of air also contributes something to it.

<page-num>190</page-num>

# On Gravity #

It may be objected that light bodies fall just as swiftly as heavy ones within a vacuum. However, this experiment is performed in a very short glass tube, usually with a feather or some other extremely light body, upon which the air in the open acts very strongly in comparison to heavier bodies. If one could allow two bodies—for which air resistance would be imperceptible—to fall from a considerable height in a vacuum, one would surely perceive a difference in the speed of the fall, just as one does in the open air. The height of the glass on the <term>air pump</term> <note>The air pump was a standard piece of 18th-century laboratory equipment used to create a vacuum in a glass jar or tube.</note> is too slight to detect this difference, especially since in the first moment of the fall, the speed of two bodies of unequal weight differs little; the ratio only increases through the duration of the fall.

If the fall originates from a <term>throw</term> <note>original: "Wurfe" - referring to projectile motion.</note>, my explanation of the cause of ever-increasing speed in proportion to height also applies to this kind of falling: however, how gravity enters into it will first be shown in the article: **Centrifugal Force** <note>original: "Fliehkraft."</note>.

# On Attractive Forces. #

The <term>drive for union</term> <note>original: "Vereinigungstrieb." This is the author’s idiosyncratic term for the internal "desire" of matter to merge or connect.</note> acts either vertically, and this is **gravity**; or it acts in a direction that contradicts the former, and only then should it be called **attractive force**. To this category belong chemical affinities and magnetic effects.

In both cases, the forces locked within the attracted body change the direction that gravity gave to them. They push their "shell" <note>The author distinguishes between the internal "force" and the external material "shell" or "husk" (Hülle) of an object.</note> toward that body which promises them a closer and easier union with a part of their own essence than the center of the Earth can provide.

Magnetic effects seem to occur through <term>unlocked forces</term> <note>original: "aufgeschloßne Kräfte." These are forces that have been released or made active.</note> in the **atmosphere** <note>original: "Dunstkreis." Literally "vapor-circle," referring to the sphere of influence or the air surrounding an object.</note>. I call "unlocked forces" those that have been lured out of a body with which they were interwoven through friction or some other stripping of their shell. Since artificial magnets are prepared by a frequently repeated stroking in the same direction, I suspect that this causes a separation of the compressive and ex-

# On Attractive Forces. #

...passive force arises, of which each is accumulated at an opposite pole. With electricity, the released forces also seem to act against one another; and I believe that lightning and thunder are produced by the friction of a cloud filled with fire against another that is overloaded with <term>nitrous air</term> <note>original: "Salpeter-Luft." In 18th-century chemistry, this term was often used to describe oxygen or nitrogen-rich air thought to be involved in combustion and atmospheric phenomena.</note>; which makes the difference between igniting and merely smashing thunderclaps very clear to me.

Affinity is the <term>drive for union</term> <note>original: "Vereinigungstrieb." A central concept for the author, suggesting that matter has an innate desire to merge.</note> of separated forces, as well as of separated similar parts that come very close to one another. It often acts through the contact of <term>atmospheres</term> <note>original: "Dunstkreise," literally "vapor circles." Here it refers to the sphere of influence or gaseous envelope surrounding a particle.</note>, most easily in fluid matters and during the destruction of bodies. It overcomes the weight of the parts it moves because these are generally very light, while the forces they wish to approach are more released and "touchable" than those which stimulated their gravity.

Affinity never rules *within* the elements of matter, but rather *over* them, and always over their compositions. If it is the effect of a released force or one interwoven in bodily parts, it pushes these parts along with it to wherever a similar released force nearby offers it a secure union.

# On Mixed Forces.

Lifting and carrying are mixtures of <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "Schwerkraft." In this period, this term refers both to the universal force of attraction and the specific weight or heaviness of an object.</note> and a resistance.

Carrying is the counter-pressure of a form, appropriate to a weight, which is impenetrable to the pressing body. That the effectiveness of carrying depends not only on the solidity of the supporting body but also on the force of the counter-pressure is shown by the <term>fulcrum</term> <note>original: "Tragpunkt," literally "carrying point." This refers to the pivot point of a lever.</note> in the lever, which seems to largely constitute its wonderful nature. The closer this fulcrum is to the load, the more it reduces its gravity. This arises because:

All forces bound to bodies act through them in straight lines and are weakened insofar as they must deviate from their straight direction. The closer a load is to the fulcrum, the less its counter-pressure deviates from its natural direction, and the more strongly it overcomes the gravity of the pressure; conversely, the further the load is from the fulcrum, the more the counter-pressure must be divided and from its vertical

<sig>N 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: line</meta>

<summary>The author transitions from discussing attraction to mechanical "mixed forces," specifically analyzing how the counter-pressure of a fulcrum interacts with gravity in a lever. He argues that forces are most effective when acting in straight lines and weaken when forced to deviate.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, mixed forces, gravity, counter-pressure, lever, fulcrum, physics, resistance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the lever's mechanics, specifically how the deviation from a vertical line of force affects the perceived weight of a load. It transitions from mechanical advantage to the concept of "living force."</meta>

line stretch away; it acts more weakly on the distant weight, and for this reason, the weight loses less of its gravity.

From this it follows that the heavy weight on the short arm of a scale becomes lighter, and not—as is commonly believed—that the light weight on the long arm becomes heavier.

Yet counter-pressure does not seem to completely exhaust the secret of the lever: time and space also have a great influence on its wonderful power. A slow, extensive pressure accumulates in the long arm of the lever and produces a rapid effect in a small space; time and space are thus narrowed by the artificial construction of the lever. Since force is nothing other than motion, and the strength of motion consists in its speed, we see how, through time and space, the lever can create an enlargement of force that bears no proportion to the gravity of the weight.

Lifting is either a triumph over gravity by another force of gravity, in which case this mechanical motion is based on the lever; or it is the overcoming of heaviness through a <term>living force</term> <note>original: "lebendige Kraft," or *vis viva*. This was an early scientific term for what we now call kinetic energy or the energy of motion.</note>.

The latter also makes use of a lever here, yet with the difference that the weight which

it hangs upon it is not gravity, but its own force—true <term>elasticity</term>, which has the capacity to powerfully expand and retract itself. This force has its choice among all directions because it knows no specific point of support for itself, and can therefore weigh the vertically descending just as vertically upward.

## On the Centrifugal Force <note>original: "Fliehkraft," literally "flight-force." In modern physics, this refers to the outward force on a rotating body, but the author uses it more broadly as a universal principle of repulsion.</note>

**We** have stated above in the article on Gravity: that the drive for union is the law of all forces:

There is, however, a second law followed by divided forces, which contradicts the first law of union; this is the drive to escape an opposing adversity when it is at its strongest. I call it the <term>centrifugal force</term>. Feeling likewise determines its direction, and moves it—like all forces—in a straight line and through the shortest path toward its goal. It presumably determines the effects of negative <term>electricity</term> and of the second pole in a magnet; in heterogeneous mixtures, it acts as the repulsion of the lightest parts; in organized bodies, it serves <term>instinct</term> in secreting everything harmful; and in the moral realm, it is called antipathy, hatred, fear, disgust, and loathing.

But in raw matter, the centrifugal force expresses its effect particularly in impact and throwing, which can hardly be explained other than through it. Both overcome

---

<page-num>199</page-num>
->On the Centrifugal Force.<-

<term>gravity</term>, and transform it in a wonderful way into a direction entirely contradictory to it.

It appears as if something passes from one body into another, accompanies it, and leaves it again without returning to its source. What then is this continued, communicated motion?

The matter presumably occurs in the following way: Throwing is an impact whose direction contradicts the vertical, and which is strong enough to overcome the resistance of gravity. All forces contained within the body that has received this impact are thus disturbed in their vertical direction; they feel the adversity of a power superior to them, and fear being locked in an even narrower prison. Their already-elevated, conical direction toward the center of the earth turns its point upward and chooses the straightest and most correct path for flight by following the line of the felt pressure; for the shortest path for removal is indisputably the extension of the line that can be drawn from the center of the staying body to the center of the fleeing body. We see, therefore, that centrifugal force is nothing other than gravity changed in its direction. The speed and duration of the

<vocab>centrifugal force, gravity, electricity, magnets, antipathy, instinct, matter, motion</vocab>

<summary>The author defines "centrifugal force" (Fliehkraft) as a fundamental drive to escape repulsion, applying it to physics, magnetism, and human emotions. He argues that the motion of a thrown object is actually gravity redirected upward by the "fear" or "adversity" of the impact.</summary>

<keywords>centrifugal force, gravity, magnetism, electricity, vitalism, mechanics, repulsion, physics of motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of centrifugal force (the "force of flight") as a repulsive drive in matter. It transitions from describing the trajectory of thrown objects to the metaphysical question of whether forces can act across a vacuum or distance without physical contact.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>
->On the Centrifugal Force.<-

The flight of a thrown body is thus determined not only by the strength of the impact, but also by the magnitude of its <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "Schwerkraft."</note>. In this latter lies the true reason why a heavy body can be thrown further than a light one; for it contains more forces that react more strongly, are overcome with more power, flee more vigorously, and require more time to return to their previous direction.

Now, however, opposing forces at a distance do not act upon one another as long or as strongly as forces of the same kind; thus, the sensation of pressure decreases after contact: the first law of union gradually re-emerges, and the peak of the cone-shaped force gradually sinks anew toward the center of the earth: the line of flight is transformed through an ever-increasing curvature into a vertical one, and forms what is called a <term>parabolic line</term> <note>The path followed by a projectile under the influence of gravity.</note>.

## Does Action at a Distance <note>original: "Actio in distans." This is the classical physics problem of how gravity or magnetism can pull on an object without touching it.</note> Exist?

Many physicists have adopted this metaphysical tool to explain such phenomena in which the acting force seems so separated from the effect that one can perceive neither a contact nor any other connection.

If forces occasionally possess such a capacity to "feel" one another in the distance, then this must rather be attributed to their two fundamental drives: to unite 1)

<sig>N 5</sig> <meta>catchword: or</meta>

---

1) The reason why beans and other fast-growing, vine-like plants wind around a stake seems to be their <term>drive for union</term> <note>original: "Vereinigungstrieb."</note> with light: they wish to follow the sun, and therefore turn in a spiral line from morning toward evening, from there again toward morning, and so forth. If one covers flowers with a container painted black on the inside, their calyxes always turn toward the side where the light comes from. If the experiment takes place in a room, they "scent" the position of the windows, and if the box is turned around in the evening, one finds the heads of these flowers turned back toward the window-side on the following day.
