It also introduces the debate over "action at a distance" and provides a botanical example of plants' "drive for union" with light.</summary>
<keywords>centrifugal force, gravity, parabolic trajectory, action at a distance, drive for union, phototropism, plant movement</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into force and contact. The previous page (201) ended with a list of fundamental drives of force: the drive to unite or—as this page begins—the drive to flee one another.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
->Does Action at a Distance Exist?<-

or to flee from one another, rather than being attributed to any other cause.

Here, presumably, the last threads of connection have not been completely torn, because they were the strongest—because they are spun from the essence of unity through an eternal law that can be hindered, but can never cease!

For, if we form the pure concept of a force as we ought, we must imagine it as infinitely expandable; consequently, the connection is never missing from our thoughts, and an actual contact becomes conceivable even at the greatest distance; but *action at a distance* <note>original: "Actio in distans." This refers to the classical physics debate over whether one object can move another without any physical medium or contact between them.</note> dealing with nothingness falls back into the nothingness of all ghostly conceptions which so gladly wish to turn Nothing into Something.

---

<page-num>203</page-num>

# Impenetrability.

Impenetrability is one of the most essential properties of matter, and all forms are more or less protected by it.

Forces and spiritual beings certainly also have their impenetrability, though only in the case where they are adverse to one another and in conflict. Resistance and the circumference of a solid form are the causes that bring about impenetrability.

But might one not suspect that forces which are neither enclosed nor adverse to one another could <term>penetrate</term> <note>original: "penetriren." The author is exploring whether forces can pass through each other without displacing one another.</note> each other?

Hitherto, this extremely subtle property has been denied to the forces of light and sound, because the expression <term>penetrate</term> was associated with the unthinkable meaning: of occupying the same place simultaneously with another thing. This is indeed impossible; yet the infinite divisibility and the unlimited speed, which we must grant to force, suggest other conceivable possibilities to our understanding: namely, whether straight directions, which on the path of infinite divisibility

<summary>The author argues that "action at a distance" is a logical impossibility, positing instead that forces are always connected by an "eternal law" of unity. The text then transitions to the concept of impenetrability, questioning if light and sound might be able to pass through one another due to their infinite speed and divisibility.</summary>
<keywords>action at a distance, physics, metaphysics, impenetrability, matter, force, light, sound, divisibility</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the impenetrability of forces and begins an investigation into cohesion. The previous page (203) ended with the question of whether light and sound can penetrate one another.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>
->Impenetrability.<-

...encounter, and be unable to avoid one another without separating? — whether the finest substance cannot pierce the coarser? And whether, in the realm of infinite velocity, the swiftest impacts could not pass through the slower ones without halting the latter? Certainly, we cannot imagine this non-sensory path through our physical senses; however, we should allow our understanding to accept it, because we recognize not only its possibility but also the necessity of the matter, as all images and sounds would have to become confused if the rays of light and sound could not cross one another without mixing.

All bodies and forces are penetrating and penetrable if one understands these words to mean the effect of a stronger force upon a weaker resistance; but if these expressions are meant to signify that one thing passes through another without any resistance at all, then this can likely only take place among forces of the same kind; for adverse forces and bodies always resist one another.

Thus, true <term>impenetrability</term> <note>The author here uses the term to describe the integrity of a force as it passes through another without being altered.</note> is the passage of one force through another without any change to their mutual directions.

---
<meta>catchword: Cohesion.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>

# Cohesion.

Although cohesion, at first glance, might be attributed to a hooked shape and the interlocking connection of a body’s components <note>This refers to the "hook-and-eye" theory of atomism, popular since the 17th century, which suggested atoms stayed together because they were physically hooked into one another.</note>, it nonetheless deserves a small place among the forces, because it seems to possess something powerful within itself.

Marble, which possesses a great cohesion primarily due to its hardness, is not only dissolved into an incoherent state through the expulsion of the <term>fixed air</term> <note>original: "fixe Luft." This is an 18th-century chemical term for carbon dioxide (CO2). Chemists like Joseph Black discovered that this "air" was "fixed" in solid minerals like limestone and marble.</note> dwelling within it, but if that air is added back to its lime, it is made into stone once more.

One can therefore suspect that fixed air, which contains a compressive force within itself, is the innermost binding agent through which stones are formed.

An airy and salt-like substance seems, in general, to have a special affinity with the internal construction of forms. Every body contains a portion of fixed air and is completely destroyed if that air leaves it through fermentation or combustion.

<meta>catchword: I</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a philosophical argument on how forces like light and sound pass through each other without mixing, and introduces cohesion as a force driven by 'fixed air' (carbon dioxide), which acts as a binding agent in solids.</summary>

<keywords>impenetrability, cohesion, fixed air, marble, light, sound, compressive force, atomic theory, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the causes of cohesion, moving from physical interlocking to chemical and pneumatic forces, and then transitions into a critique of the laws of motion regarding pressure.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
->Cohesion.<-

I believe, therefore, that this air-like and salt-like substance is the second and most prominent cause of cohesion;

However, I suspect a third. In saltpeter <note>Also known as nitre (potassium nitrate). Early modern chemists believed it contained highly compressed "air" or vital spirits that were released during combustion.</note>, the air is compressed to such a degree that a single cubic inch of it could inflate a thousand lungs and burst a very thick metal vessel. How can the thin, earthen crust, which constitutes the base of the aerial salt, have the power to tame and enclose such an astonishing force?

Here I observe a possible circumstance which may play a part in the cohesion of all bodies. Could not elastic forces, through time, habit, and a love of union <note>The author uses "Vereinigungsliebe," a poetic term for chemical affinity or the tendency of substances to stay combined.</note>, achieve a state of rest that would be what we commonly call <term>inertia</term> <note>The tendency of an object to resist changes in its state of motion. The author suggests inertia is not a passive property, but a result of balanced active forces.</note>? Air, which is the great binding medium of all organizations, has in any case the property of not moving itself, but of allowing itself to be expanded and contracted: its body is water — it seeks balance and rest: Thus, rest, habit, and the drive for union could constitute a third cause of cohesion.

<meta>catchword: Pressure</meta>

<page-num>207</page-num>

# Pressure and Counter-pressure.

I believe that the theorem "Counter-pressure is always perfectly equal to pressure" is not quite correctly understood. One imagines that a movement passes from the pressed body into the pressing one, which is just as strong as its pressure was. But how many incomprehensible riddles would one not have to accept as truths if one assumed that a force could leave a body and penetrate into another? How would it be replaced in bodies that were constantly losing it? And where would it go after it had filled others to overflowing? Motion would therefore have to be a fluid essence, compatible with all bodies, which flows through the whole of material nature — a thing such as Mesmer <note>Franz Anton Mesmer (1734–1815), a physician who theorized that a "magnetic fluid" connected all living things, a theory known as animal magnetism.</note> devised in his lectures on animal magnetism. However, even this thing would have to be of two kinds in order to be able to struggle with itself, and even then, all those phenomena would remain unexplained in which we perceive that a movement overcomes resistance.

<meta>catchword: My</meta>

<summary>The author explores the role of compressed "aerial salts" and inertia as causes of cohesion before critiquing the mechanical understanding of Newton's third law, arguing against the idea of motion as a fluid substance.</summary>
<keywords>cohesion, saltpeter, inertia, pressure, counter-pressure, Franz Mesmer, animal magnetism, physics of motion, aerial salt</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Newtonian mechanics, specifically the third law of motion. Having previously discussed the nature of cohesion, the author now argues that "action and reaction" are internal processes within an individual body rather than a simple exchange between two separate objects.</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>Pressure and Counter-pressure.</header>
<meta>catchword: men,</meta>

My opinion on this matter is as follows: Movement is essentially inherent to every powerful substance, because force and movement are one and the same. It does not leave its body to penetrate into another; rather, if it finds resistance, it either knocks it over or it recoils back upon itself and against the form by which it is surrounded. Just as much force as had acted forward now acts backward, and in this way one must understand the theorem that counter-pressure is equal to pressure. We see, therefore, that pressure and counter-pressure do not occur between two bodies striking one another, but in each one individually. The movement of every body reacts upon itself, and not always in equal measure. — For if I push a body along, I do not feel the jolt that I feel when I strike another body with the same force that is immovable to me: and if I apply just as much strength to throw a down feather away from me as I do a stone, I feel an unpleasant tension; in the first case the resistance was too great, in the second too small. <note>The author is describing the physical sensation of "follow-through" or the lack of resistance. He argues that our own body feels the "recoil" of our intent differently depending on the mass of the object we act upon.</note>

Counter-pressure is to be well distinguished from resistance. When speaking of bodies, the latter is based on the solidity of for-

---

<page-num>209</page-num>
<header>Pressure and Counter-pressure.</header>

-ms <note>original: "formen," continuing from the previous page.</note>, which is the most essential property of matter, and does not allow one body to displace or damage another unhindered.

But when forces resist each other, their directions contend with one another—horizontal pressure cancels out vertical pressure, and thus the throw puts gravity to flight when it is the weakest. <note>The "throw" refers to the impetus given to a projectile, which temporarily overcomes the downward pull of gravity.</note>

Resistance never has a perfect equality with pressure; otherwise, a standstill in movement, an immobility, would arise, which I have shown above resembles a non-entity. <note>The author views "absolute rest" or "immobility" as a physical impossibility or a logical absurdity ("Unding"), as everything in nature is composed of active forces.</note>

Sometimes a very slight resistance can excite the most violent movement: A cannonball, rolled slowly by hand, comes to a stop when it finds a small stone that halts it; yet rolling just as weakly and near its standstill, it leaps away from that same small stone with renewed force and can still spread death and destruction if it had been shot from a cannon.

Whence this difference? Does it not prove that every body [possesses] the force of its own move-

<meta>catchword: ment</meta>

<summary>The author argues that pressure and counter-pressure are internal reactions of a body's inherent force rather than a simple transfer between objects. He distinguishes between passive resistance (solidity) and active counter-pressure, suggesting that absolute immobility is a physical impossibility.</summary>

<page-num>210</page-num>

-ment <note>original: "Bewegung," continuing from the previous page's catchword.</note> has within it the fact that resistance bears no proportion of strength to the movement, and that the slightest stimulus <term>Reiz: a sensory or physical provocation</term> can act just as powerfully as the strongest pressure? For here one sees a fleeing body, which, as it enters a state of rest, is so violently disturbed by a small circumstance that it takes flight again almost as strongly: it believes that the same force which had canceled its natural direction were present again, and fears for a second time the strength of its superiority. <note>The author uses personifying language—"believes" and "fears"—to describe how a physical object reacts to external stimuli, suggesting that matter possesses a rudimentary form of "feeling" or "perception."</note>

How greatly does this strange revival of an almost expired force prove the power of feeling <note>original: "Fühlung," referring here to the physical sensation of contact or touch</note>, its ability to command movement, and that forces can be deceived, as I asserted above in my explanation of the increasing strength of falling?

When one force presses another, and this pressed force acts against it, the expression *resistance* seems to me more fitting and proper in this case than the word *counter-pressure*.

Pressure and resistance always occur between two different bodies. However, counter-pressure arises within each pressing and each pressed body individually: in the pressing [body],

<meta>catchword: because,</meta>

<column-break/>

<header>Pressure and Counter-pressure.</header>
<page-num>211</page-num>

because a resistance drives its pressing force back; in the pressed [body], because its resisting force is compressed by a pressure.

In truth, counter-pressure is nothing other than a movement of pressure altered by the feeling of resistance. It is an effect of resistance and a consequence of pressure. — Pressure and resistance are **active** — counter-pressure is **passive**. <note>original: "leidend," which in this philosophical context means "undergoing" or "receptive," as opposed to "active."</note>

<header>The Stimulus.</header>

## The Stimulus.

A stimulus <term>Reiz: here meaning a provocation or excitation that triggers a response</term> arises from a persistent, rapidly multiplied, gentle pressing. The forces that lie interwoven within bodies, enclosed in such diverse husks, have acquired through assimilation and habit such inertia <term>Inertie: the tendency of an object to resist changes in its state of motion</term> that they require a strong or persistent shock to be awakened from their sleep, to collect themselves, and to regain their energy.

The stimulus acts most effectively in undivided force and reveals itself, therefore, primarily in elastic bodies.

An elastic resilience <term>Schnellkraft: literally "fast-force," referring to the internal power of an object to return to its original shape, such as a spring</term> that has been weakened by a strong, tedious pressure is strengthened by a lesser weight, and increased and enlivened by a gentle, often repeated pressure.

It seems as if the reacting force becomes weary in the long run and withdraws when it feels that it cannot resist at all. But if one removes the overwhelming weight and presses upon it anew, but more weakly, the force experiences, at the first attempt at resistance, that its power is once again capable of something. If there then follows immediately

<meta>catchword: a</meta>

---

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>The Stimulus.</header>

a second pressure, it gathers itself even more for resistance and feels doubly the excess of its strength. The more often a reacting force repeats this victorious feeling, the more courageous it becomes within its bonds.

This is observed not only in steel springs, but also in the nerves, muscles, and elastic parts of our body, which are strengthened by the stimulus.

Furthermore, if one applies this teaching to the moral resilience <note>original: "moralische Schnellkraft," referring to the psychological or spiritual capacity to recover from adversity</note>, one will find that insurmountable resistance destroys it, while frequently overcome difficulty strengthens it. For this reason, "iron necessity" is the most powerful teacher of patience; and for this reason, effort gives strength—to the soul as well as to the body. For this reason, one should present children—except in the most necessary cases—with no other prohibition than one as firm as a rock, which they could never hope to shake; one should never provoke them to stubbornness through insignificant refusals and then giving in when begged.

Finally, for this reason, I wish that one would impose upon nations few but clearly necessary duties, and none of the kind that can be exaggerated through fanaticism <term>Schwärmerey: a state of wild, irrational enthusiasm or religious ecstasy</term>, neglected due to their sheer number, or even bought off with money.

<sig>O 3</sig> <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early modern printers to keep the folded pages of a book in the correct order.</note>
<meta>catchword: I</meta>

<summary>The author explains how gentle, repeated stimulation (stimulus) restores the energy of both physical objects and biological bodies. He then applies this principle to human nature, arguing that moral character and good governance are built on surmountable challenges and firm, clear boundaries.</summary>
<keywords>stimulus, resilience, elasticity, moral strength, child-rearing, political duty, inertia, physics of the soul</keywords>

I wish that it were made easy for people to be good Christians and perfect citizens. I wish that legislation would place more good weights than evil ones in the scales of her justice; for resistance always aligns itself with the strength and nature of the pressure. The greater the punishments, the greater the crimes become: cruel laws make people cruel. <note>The author is applying the physical principle of "action and reaction" from the previous pages to social engineering and criminology.</note>

Could one not command the good, instead of forbidding the evil?

Our laws present to the imagination <term>Vorstellungskraft: the faculty of forming mental images; in Enlightenment philosophy, this was seen as the primary engine of human desire</term>, which is the driving wheel of our will, a complete registry of all the shameful deeds and wickednesses that a human can commit; they stimulate the imagination through the image as much as through the resistance. Would it not be better to give the imagination a perfect list of virtues and beautiful deeds, and to make it more attractive through rewards? I cannot imagine that in a country where the highest prize was set upon saving the life of a fellow citizen, where everyone, from childhood on, had the desire to earn this prize, anyone could become a murderer.

## Substance of the Soul.

A spirit or the soul is, in my opinion, a part of the substance of the Universal Being, which has received an individual essence through the center of selfhood. <note>The author suggests a panentheistic view where individual souls are specialized "portions" of a greater divine or universal substance.</note>

Motion, touch, and feeling constitute this substance; for I believe that everything we call <term>powers of the soul: the various faculties of the mind, such as memory, will, and reason</term> can also be traced back to motion, touch, and feeling.

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for a treatise on psychology and metaphysics, followed by a definition of the soul as a fragment of a "Universal Being" defined by motion and sensation.</summary>
<keywords>substance of the soul, selfhood, universal being, mental faculties, psychology, metaphysics, consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "Substance of the Soul," specifically detailing how individual sensations are processed into complete mental concepts.</meta>

[Concepts] consist of sensations of the properties of a thing. The more properties we have felt and the more senses have conveyed them to us, the more complete and distinct our concept of this thing becomes. A single sensation gives no concept, just as a single line cannot form a figure—one knows nothing more than that one has felt something.

But how many various sensations and movements does even the simplest concept presuppose! When an object touches a sense, directly or indirectly, an artificial movement—consisting of many elastic pressures and counter-pressures and shaped by the harmonious structure of that sense—passes into the substance of the soul. The latter must compare the sensation arising from this with the sensation of itself in order to distinguish the object from itself. All of this is recorded in the memory, and yet the soul then has only the distinct idea of a single sensation. It must perform several such operations through the same or other senses upon the same object,

<meta>catchword: in order to</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>221</page-num>
# Concepts.

in order to obtain feelings of several of its properties. It must compare these with one another, with itself, and with the object, to be certain that they all belong to this thing and no other; and only a collection of such manifold movements, feelings, and memories constitutes a <term>concept: a mental grasp of an object's various qualities combined into a single idea</term>. 1)

Every concept is, on a small scale, what one calls a work of genius, in which our spirit encompasses many results at once and makes them into a whole: both actions of the soul are effected by the **drive for union**, which is just as active in the spiritual realm as in the material, and everywhere seeks to increase unity.

<meta>catchword: Ob-</meta>

---

> 1) The operation of the soul by which this collection occurs is called **representation** <note>original: "Vorstellung"</note> by **Kant**. I would rather call it **imagination** <note>original: "Einbildung"</note>; for it forms a unity. <note>The author is making a play on the German word "Einbildung" (imagination), suggesting it literally means "forming into one" (Eins-Bildung).</note>

<summary>This page explains how the soul forms "concepts" by collecting and comparing multiple sensory impressions. The author compares the formation of a single concept to a work of genius, driven by a natural "drive for union" that seeks to create unity from diversity.</summary>
<keywords>concept formation, sensory perception, memory, drive for union, Immanuel Kant, representation vs. imagination</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into how the human mind constructs reality from sensory input. While the previous page explained how the soul combines multiple sensations into a "concept," the author now asks what actually exists behind those sensations—the "object" itself.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>

# Objects.

What, however, is that thing which we call an **object**, and of which we have been able to feel nothing else but **properties**—which themselves are nothing more than the feelings of our senses? What kind of mental image can we form of this thing if we strip it of its properties? Does it even exist? To be sure, its **essence** <note>The German word "Wesen" refers to the inner nature or "being" of a thing that remains unchanged regardless of how it appears.</note> is invisible and unknown to us. We cannot even imagine an object through our senses without taking the specific property that affects us most strongly and turning it into its "image," using this as a foundation to investigate all other sensations of its properties. All of these are relative effects upon our senses—presumably illusions; but the fact that a thing is *there*, resisting the pressure of our senses, seems to me to be an **absolute truth**. Because it resists different senses in such a manifold variety of ways, I have reason to believe that the surfaces of things truly exist and are distinct from one another. As far as colors and tones are concerned, I admit they might belong more to our senses than to the object; yet I suspect that they also have a fundamental basis in light and in air.

---

<page-num>223</page-num>

## Of the Essence of Things and Their Properties.

All properties of a thing exist more *outside* of it than *within* it. They are **effects**. The cause of these effects is the thing itself, or at least lies within it; but the effects occur where they produce a change. Properties are changes, feelings, or mental images produced by an acting force outside of themselves. Therefore, something is present that is more independent and more "true" than what we call its properties—a basis upon which these properties cling, and this is the <term>Essence: the underlying substance or "thing-in-itself" that exists even if no one is there to perceive its qualities</term>.

When we take away everything that we have been able to feel and recognize of an object through our senses, the object still remains. It is just as certainly there as the fact that we have felt it; however, through its resistance, it sends back all our questions regarding its essence, answering them only with unsatisfying riddles.

We seek to unite ourselves with external objects, doing so either indirectly or directly

<vocab>Objects, Essence, Properties, Senses, Effects, Force, Absolute Truth, Illusions</vocab>

<summary>The author explores the philosophical distinction between an object's essence and its perceived properties, arguing that while our senses only perceive "effects" (like color or sound), the physical resistance an object offers proves its independent existence.</summary>

<keywords>epistemology, perception, essence vs. appearance, sensory resistance, theory of objects, German Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the ontological nature of objects. Having previously established that properties are merely sensory effects, the author now proposes that a singular "elastic force" or "substance" underlies all physical reality, manifesting differently depending on its "covering" and the sense organ it encounters.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

<header>Of the Essence of Things</header>

to touch, and they resist or unite with us; what we feel thereby is ourselves. — We perceive changes that these objects produce within us; but what they themselves are — their **essence**? — that we do not feel. — Only this much remains certain: that we have felt something real — something forceful outside of ourselves.

If we had other senses, the same force would have produced other feelings in us. Thus, we can easily assume that a single force must act differently upon different senses. But since this force is varied through infinite **modifications** <note>In 18th-century philosophy, a "modification" is a specific form or state that a substance takes on, much like how water can be ice, liquid, or steam while remaining the same substance.</note> and each modification of the same can act differently on different senses, I can grasp the infinite variety of effects that can arise from a single force, and I arrive at the suspicion that the same force produces all sensory effects.

Now I know nothing more active than **force**, no other force than **movement**, and no independent movement other than **elastic substance**. I am therefore of the opinion that the essence of things is of one and the same nature and is the simple **elastic force** <note>The author uses "elastic" here not just to mean "stretchy," but in the historical sense of a fundamental, self-moving energy or "ether" that fills the universe and reacts to pressure.</note> which constitutes the **Universal Being**; <note>original: "Allwesen"</note> but separated from it, acting now in forms, now

<meta>catchword: without</meta>

---

<page-num>225</page-num>

<header>and their Properties.</header>

without forms, and through the variety of its directions, its coverings, and its determinations, it increases the variety of its effects on countlessly different objects beyond measure; consequently, I say: the essence of things is the **modification of the elastic substance**, the variety of which constitutes the essential difference between things.

**Substance** is essence in general: **modification** of the same determines the essence of each thing in particular. Its connection with various objects produces its **properties**.

The main modification which the substance of force suffers in the material world is the **covering** <note>original: "Hülle"</note> which encloses it. The **impenetrability** of this covering is the cause of the indirect or direct counter-pressure upon our senses, and the variety of the impenetrable surfaces of these coverings determines the manifold variety of vibrations of which the senses are capable.

Nothing is more real than essence. **Reality is truth**, and truth reveals itself only to feeling. This is a new proof that essence must be an extended substance, capable of being touched by the senses.

<meta>catchword: Das</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>

<header>Of the Essence of Things, etc.</header>

**Essence**, therefore, occupies a space. If it is the **Universal Being** <note>original: "Allwesen." This refers to the totality of existence or the "Absolute Whole," often interpreted in this period as a pantheistic or monistic concept of God or Nature.</note>, the absolute Whole, then it constitutes absolute space. If it is a part of the Whole, it must be able to stand in connection with other parts of the same. The more it is "something"—the more reality and effect it possesses—the more points of contact it must have.

Properties always have a reference to a foreign thing outside themselves. **Essence** alone is independent. It needs no other thing outside itself in order to be, because it fulfills the concept of Being. It must possess everything within itself that is necessary for it to be what it is.

## Abstract Ideas.

Just as a sensory concept is composed from a collection of various feelings <note>In 18th-century psychology, "feeling" (Gefühl) often refers broadly to any sensory perception or "impression" received through the body, not just emotional states.</note> of the self-same thing, we conversely create an **abstract** one from the union of such feelings which have been taken from *different* objects.

Abstract ideas are transpositions of sensory ones; they are produced by applying sensory ideas to an object that does not fall within the reach of the senses.

Here I must mention a particular discovery: namely, that every sensory concept is likewise abstract. For we feel nothing of any sensory object but its properties; if we think these away, nothing remains that we could feel. However, we abstract that property from it which is the most striking to us, establish it as the foundation, let it stand for the **essence** (which we do not know), call it the "object," and attribute all our other feelings of this thing to it as

<sig>P 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: proper-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that essence is the only independent reality and occupies space, while also proposing that all sensory perceptions are fundamentally "abstract" because we only perceive properties rather than the thing-in-itself.</summary>
<keywords>ontology, Universal Being, space, essence, abstract ideas, sensory perception, properties, cognition</keywords>

# Recollections

are repeated concepts—repetitions of inner feelings <note>As noted previously, "feelings" (Gefühle) in this period refers to sensory impressions or perceptions.</note> linked through analogies. Their objects are the **memory-images**, the nature of which is a mystery difficult to fathom. However, these must also have motion and touch as their foundation, because they have arisen through concepts, and because they are capable of exciting the same sensations within us that real objects are accustomed to producing.

# Memory-images.

If we could explain the secret of memory-images, perhaps the entire riddle of the soul <note>original: "Seele." In this context, the term refers to the mind or the seat of consciousness rather than a purely theological entity.</note> would be solved for us: for all our thoughts, conclusions, experiences, and even consciousness itself are composed of nothing but memories. However, one can hardly even devise hypotheses about these magical images. Yet, I venture to put forward a few, and leave them as "dreams" to my metaphysical successors:

## First Hypothesis.

Could memory-images not be creations of the soul—and the worlds—the memory-images of God? Just as the Divinity separates and encloses forms out of His infinite substance, so perhaps the soul—whose drive for imitation cannot be ignored—could form images out of its own substance: copies of the originals that surround it. These might perhaps maintain themselves for a time like physical bodies; and like bodies, through the drive for union and re-

<column-break/>

<header>Memory-images.</header>
<sig>P 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: these</meta>

pulsion <note>original: "Vereinigungs- und Fliehungstrieb." A reference to the Newtonian or psychological forces of attraction and repulsion applied to thoughts.</note> they might arrange and attract one another, or displace and destroy one another; like bodies, they might grow and diminish, arise and disappear. They would be the wealth of the soul, a little world in which it reigns, and a treasure of the manifold sensations of itself! 1)

One must not be concerned that matter and space could be lacking for such an enormous quantity of forms: for the substance of the soul is infinitely divisible; and there, where our power of imagination finds no limits when it tires of measuring immensity—in the unfathomable point of **I-ness** <term>I-ness: (Ichheit) A philosophical term for the core of self-consciousness or the "Ego."</term>—there, memory too cannot lack for space.

# Memory-images.

These images are nothing other than vibrations, impacts, and movements of their elastic substance. I imagine them like rectilinear movements, which clothe feeling with forms—almost like the basic lines of a drawing, which determine only the length, width, and turns of a figure and are afterwards covered with the contour.

According to this conjecture, memory-images exist in the soul <note>original: "Seele." As previously noted, the author uses this to mean the seat of consciousness or the mind.</note> not as feelings, but as movements; not as forms, but as lines and angles. If I follow this thought, I imagine the substance of the soul as a labyrinth crossed by infinitely many passages, which the senses have laid out—a path which movement knows through habit, but in which it also frequently loses its way.

### Third Hypothesis.

If one wished to think of the soul's feeling as a large musical instrument, consisting of as many smaller ones as we have senses, then the secret connection of memories and their origins could be explained as an effect of harmonic accord. One could say: Just as

<column-break/>

<header>Memory-images.</header>
<sig>P 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

every tone carries with it the echo of its third and fifth <note>original: "Terz und Quinte." These are musical intervals that naturally resonate with a root note.</note>, seeks to transition into them, and sets in motion all the strings tuned to its chord, which in turn have the same property of striking other thirds and fifths and continuing a musical scale to infinity; in just the same way, every sense can have its harmonic nerves, which stand in a music-like connection not only among themselves but also with the primary feeling. Thus, when a feeling echoes like a tone into the great instrument of the soul, it seeks out its consonant partners; these pass into theirs, and continue this harmonic course until the vibrations lose their power or are disturbed by dissonances.

### Fourth Hypothesis.

Pythagoreans and Jewish Kabbalists <note>The author refers to ancient traditions that linked mathematics and mysticism, suggesting the mind operates on hidden numerical laws.</note> have regarded the play of thoughts as a kind of calculation, the art of which the soul practices mechanically. Presumably, the order, the proportion, and especially the speed that reigns in thought gave rise to this ingenious opinion.

If one wanted to apply this to memory, one would have to say: All ideas are in

<page-num>234</page-num>

<meta>catchword: sich [himself/itself]</meta>

of the soul is preserved through numbers or rather through algebraic signs, which record the manifold movements that the senses have taught to the self-feeling <note>original: "Selbstgefühl." In 18th-century psychology, this refers to the internal awareness of one's own existence and bodily state.</note>. The faculty of comparison orders, sums up, or divides these signs, and increases them through the results drawn from them. This calculation is not perceptible, but merely mechanical; it only runs through the signs of the movements: the latter remain quiet, and thus produce no feelings.

But when the soul interrupts its rapid calculations and lingers on one of these signs, the latter excites the movement associated with it and will maintain it until it can connect to the inner end of that sense through which it was first brought into the soul; thus, a pictorial memory arises. Namely, the inner end of the sense is shaken in unison by the sustained movement and imparts back to the substance of the soul, through an artificial deception <note>The author suggests that memory is a "deception" because the mind reacts to a stored vibration as if the external object were actually present.</note>, the very same vibrations which the presence of the actual sensory image had previously given it.

When I notice the analogy that words have with hieroglyphs, and that a child

<column-break/>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>Memory-images.</header>
<meta>catchword: diesen [these]</meta>

invents for itself a small language before it learns to speak; when I further consider that the mathematician needs algebraic signs to facilitate for his memory the correct comparison of immense quantities and magnitudes, I am inclined to consider the hypothesis of hieroglyphs as the most probable.

Visible or audible signs are essentially the same. Words are to the ears what writing is to the eyes—both are hieroglyphs <note>The author uses "hieroglyphs" here in a broad sense to mean any symbolic representation or shorthand for a complex idea.</note>. When a child points to two objects and designates the most prominent of them with a sound, it has invented a hieroglyph; it has, as it were, created such a thing and taken it from itself; for no sensory object, no reflection could have given it such an abstract thought. Furthermore: the child cannot have the intention of making itself understood to others through these hieroglyphs, for it does not yet have the quantity of concepts necessary to compose such an intention; thus it has invented this audible sign for its own use—presumably for its memory; for as often as it sees this object again, it gives the same sound, and repeats it as often as it desires the object. Only when others [offer] it more than once...

given these sounds, it senses the true use of language.

Even more so, I find that hieroglyphs <note>In the 18th century, "hieroglyphs" was often used to describe any system of symbolic signs that represent ideas directly, rather than just the specific Egyptian script.</note> have a great affinity with memory when I consider what ease algebraic signs provide to it, and how easily every person—without knowing algebra—invents their own counting systems, abbreviations, and ordering symbols to aid their memory. They do this to calculate without the formal art of arithmetic, to separate confused heaps into compartments, and to distinguish these with primary titles. All these untaught actions seem to me to be expressions of the algebraic order that lies within human nature—proofs of the **mechanical erudition** <term>mechanical erudition: the mind's automatic, almost machine-like ability to process and organize information without conscious effort</term> of one's memory!

Another reason to suspect that all memory-signs might be hieroglyphs rather than actual images is as follows:

We can only create a pictorial memory of visible things, but not of those things felt through the other senses; even less so of abstract concepts, and least of all of accumulated comparisons and all the great results of experiences, which we store as decided certainties so simply in our

---

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>### Memory-images. ###</header>
<meta>catchword: Was [What]</meta>

memory that we can survey them with a single glance. These results must therefore have been written with a sign of meaning <note>original: "Bedeutungszeichen" — a symbolic representation rather than a literal "picture" of an object.</note>, because they have no figure of which the soul could form an image, and yet they are recognized by the soul clearly, convincingly, and instantaneously. Now, as soon as these accumulated conclusions and results cannot be expressed other than through hieroglyphs, it would be superfluous to create a special picture gallery for visible objects and to paint them differently than the invisible ones.

## Representations.

or mental images are lingering memories, which we adapt to the specific sense related to them, thereby moving that sense backward <note>The author suggests that while external perception moves from the organ to the mind, a "representation" moves from the mind back to the sensory organ</note>. They are repetitions of a sensory impression from the inside out; they stir the inner end of a sensory organ; for this reason, they are more vivid and pictorial than mere memories. 1)

---

1) Professor Kant calls this action of the soul: intuition <note>original: *Anschauung*</note>. I have retained the old expression because it is understandable to everyone and describes the action so literally and beautifully; for it implies that one places the object before oneself <note>The German word *Vorstellung* literally means "placing before."</note>. To those who do not know the Kantian dictionary, "intuition" gives the idea of a physical, sensory viewing.

<column-break/>

<page-num>239</page-num>

## Imagination.

This word has a double meaning. To distinguish between them, it would be desirable if we had two separate words. Strictly speaking, imagination is the faculty of dwelling upon a memory until it becomes sensory through contact with the inner side of a physical organ.

Then the soul also has the faculty to unite various ideas from the realm of possibilities and to create from them a whole that not only does not exist, but often cannot exist. This art we also call the power of imagination <term>power of imagination: *Einbildungskraft*, often referring in this period to the creative or "productive" imagination</term>.

In my opinion, it relates to the former sense of the word as a sensory concept relates to an abstract one. One might well call it the "abstracting imagination."

It is related to wit, the spirit of invention, and genius, and it gathers for them—from distant regions of the soul—the material from which they can make useful possibilities into reality.

---

<summary>This page defines "representations" as internal sensory echoes and explores the dual nature of imagination: as both a tool for vivid recall and a creative faculty for synthesizing new ideas. The author also justifies his terminology in contrast to the philosophical vocabulary of Immanuel Kant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the mechanics of the mind, transitioning from the "Imagination" (the ability to visualize or create mental images) to the cognitive process of "Comparison." It explores how the mind organizes sensory data into knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>240</page-num>

# Comparison

To **compare** is to feel: do two objects give us similar feelings or not? — **Distinction** is merely a negative comparison.

The **drive for unification** <note>original: *Vereinigungstrieb*. This refers to a perceived natural impulse to find harmony or patterns in nature.</note>, this general law of all things, is the motive for comparison: what cannot be made similar, however, is set aside, denied, and thus distinction arises.

The first operation of the human soul is therefore inevitably a distinction; for it seeks to unite the first feeling that the senses bring to it with the self-feeling of its **I-ness** <term>I-ness: *Ichheit*, a term used in 18th-century philosophy to describe the core sense of self or the "Ego"</term>, which lies at the foundation; and since this is not possible, it distinguishes the feeling from itself.

The closer two objects are together, the easier the eye can compare and distinguish their properties. Presumably, memory images are also moved closer together when we compare in thought.

<column-break/>

<page-num>241</page-num>
# Comparison

<sig>Q</sig>
<meta>catchword: Com-</meta>

We see from all this that comparisons presuppose a movement of approach and consist of nothing but feeling. They are, as it were, *feelings of feelings*. When the primary feeling of I-ness is moved by partial feelings that radiate toward its center from different sides, it feels not only their form—whether each has the same one or another—but also where they come from; thus, it perceives the difference in their number, their place, and their origin.

Just as words are composed of letters, thoughts from words, and entire books from thoughts, so all our wisdom is nothing other than a manifold repositioning and collection of compared feelings. To repeat and compare feelings is the unceasing occupation of our soul—which is what we call **thinking**—and all our knowledge consists of nomenclatures and measurements. The latter are comparisons of a measure with a thing—means to compare more accurately.

Unity and multiplicity are the purposes of the Creator and all his works. We, too, seek to produce unities and multiplicities after our own fashion. This happens through...

<page-num>242</page-num>
# 242 Comparison.

**Assimilation** (generalizing) and **dissection** (analyzing); these are the noblest and most refined activities of our understanding, but fundamentally they are nothing other than more artful applications of the faculty of comparison.

I believe that the operation of comparison contributes infinitely to ordering the memory. It gathers similar objects and removes the dissimilar ones, brings the former and all their particular properties under headings, thereby determines main species and sub-species, discovers and dissects all the differences belonging to each, connects them through logical conclusions, turns the latter into experiences, and finally assembles them into a scientific order.

Perhaps the forms that seem to lie within the soul—the existence of which we only discover through their useful and necessary application to sensory concepts—are nothing other than the order in which the **drive for unification** <note>original: *Vereinigungstrieb*; the natural urge to find patterns or connections between separate things.</note> places the markers of memory. The collections, the series, and sequences of these, or their distances from one another in the memory, are perceptible to the faculty of representation. Their orders and differences are noticed by the soul, and must therefore be distinguished by names; and thus arise the strange: abstract, non-sensory, collective, and dis-

<column-break/>

<page-num>243</page-num>
# Comparison. 243

<sig>Q 2</sig>

junctive concepts, which Aristotle called **Categories** <note>Aristotle's list of the most fundamental ways in which things can be described, such as substance, quantity, and quality.</note>, and Kant called **pure forms** <note>Immanuel Kant argued that space and time are not things we find in the world, but "forms" or structures built into our minds to organize sensory data.</note>. In this way, we have obtained the words and ideas of quantity, size, distance, time, and space. 1)

---

1) I have shown above in the first section that every event, because it has a duration, has its own time—and every body, because it is extended, has its own space for itself, without a necessary reference to our power of representation. However, the ordered sequence of events in the memory forms Kantian time, and the side-by-side positioning of physical memory-markers forms Kantian space. Through the perceptibility of this order, inscribed in the memory, we develop the abstract concepts of these two modes of representation: however, we attain the correction of both by comparing the indeterminate duration of events and the indeterminate size and distance of various spaces with the determination of a measurement. These are the primal sources of geometry and chronology.

<summary>The author explains how the mental act of comparison allows us to organize memories into categories, eventually leading to abstract concepts like space, time, and quantity. He reconciles these mental "forms" with the philosophies of Aristotle and Kant, suggesting they arise from the way memory is structured.</summary>

## Proportion

is the comparison of the magnitudes, the strength, the duration, or the movement of two or more things—the comparison of such properties that allow themselves to be measured: measurement, however, is the comparison of a smaller proportion with a larger one of the same kind.

---

<meta>catchword: Ex</meta> <note>The original catchword "Er" refers to "Erfahrung" (Experience) on the following page.</note>

<page-num>245</page-num>

## Experience

is the repeated comparison of effects <note>original: *Folgen*; literally "consequences" or "sequences."</note> with their causes—the expectation of similar cases. This is that insufficiently valued lot which Leibniz <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a German philosopher who famously argued that animals operate by "empiricism" or memory of past sequences, whereas humans possess "reason" and understand universal truths.</note> cast to the animals in order to deny them reason, and which nevertheless constitutes the truest part of our wisdom.

---

## Understanding

is the collection of many experiences ordered through logical deductions <note>original: *Vernunftschlüße*; the formal process of reaching conclusions through the faculty of reason.</note>.

---

<sig>Q 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Repr</meta> <note>The original catchword "Vors" likely points to the next entry, "Vorstellung" (Representation) or "Vorsicht" (Prudence).</note>

<summary>This page defines Proportion, Experience, and Understanding as a hierarchy of mental operations derived from the act of comparison. It specifically defends the value of Experience—the ability to predict outcomes based on past causes—against Leibniz's view that it is a lower, animalistic trait.</summary>

<page-num>246</page-num>

## Prudence.

From experiences and possibilities, the soul prepares for itself conjectures about the future; this comparison is called foresight.

## Wisdom

is the arrangement of various experiences and acts of prudence toward a single purpose.

All of this occurs through logical deductions <note>original: *Vernunftschlüsse*; the formal process of reasoning toward a conclusion</note>, which also simultaneously constitute the internal fabric of experience.

---

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>

## The Logical Deduction.

All logical deductions reduce down to **Yes** and **No**. These two great primal feelings are, as it were, the skeleton of the famous principle of contradiction <note>original: *principium contradictionis*; a core rule of logic stating that something cannot be both true and false at the same time</note>, which is the cornerstone of all demonstrations.

**Yes** and **No** have their origin in the most certain of all truths—in the certainty of our existence—in the self-feeling <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*; a sense of self-awareness or intuition of one’s own existence</note> which confirms the truth of all our feelings. The child first feels: *I am*—an affirmation. When a foreign sensation arrives, the child feels something other than itself—a negation. This is the first experience of a human being, their first comparison, and the foundation of all syllogisms <note>original: *Syllogismen*; logical arguments where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</note>.

The logical deduction is the comparison of an object with an experience—the total unification of an individual concept with a universal one, whereby the former is put in the place of the latter.

If we assume that concepts [exist] in a specific order and as hieroglyphs

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>248</page-num>
<header>The Logical Deduction.</header>

...be entered into memory, then the progression and form of an affirmative logical deduction <note>original: *Vernunftschluß*; a formal argument consisting of a major and minor premise and a conclusion</note> is roughly as follows:

A proposition based on experience and consisting of two general concepts **A** and **B** is established as a proven truth. These two general concepts are placed in memory as cause and effect, one above the other: 

-> A / B <-

Then an individual, foreign concept **a** is compared and united with the general concept **A** in such a way that both count as one, and both are written down next to each other as the same cause above the effect **B**: 

-> a = A / B <-

Thus, the conclusion <note>original: *Conclusion*</note> is: **a**; just like **A**, produces the effect **B**. This seems to me to be the order according to which memory-signs form a logical deduction. The soul can read nothing other than what is written in the memory; it becomes physically necessary for it to think according to this order.

If the mechanism of memory were better known to us, we would clearly see the physical infallibility of the logical consequence.

The logical consequence is a formula through which one makes a third truth out of two;
<meta>catchword: but</meta>

---

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>249</page-num>
<header>The Logical Deduction.</header>
<sig>Q 5</sig>

but this formula is more certain than any of these three truths; for it alone is absolutely true: the others are only relatively so—namely, if the experiences on which the two premises <note>original: *Vordersätze*; the initial statements from which a conclusion is drawn</note> are based are correct, then the conclusion <note>original: *Nachsatz*; the final statement that follows from the premises</note> is also true.

The weight of a logical consequence is so captivating that one can resist neither its movement nor its truth.

All demonstrations are composed of logical deductions, but we cannot demonstrate the nature—the necessary truth—of the formula of a logical deduction; it seems to belong among the perceptible truths <note>truths that are felt or sensed intuitively rather than proven through further logic</note>. A child feels the certainty of a logical consequence if they understand the premises: no one, not even a fool, makes a false conclusion; he can only fail in the premises <note>original: *Prämißen*</note>.

In conclusion, I want to mention the strange similarity that a logical deduction has with a triangle—one which seems remarkable to me because it is only through logical deductions and triangles that we discover unknown truths.

The syllogism <note>original: *Syllogism*; a form of reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed propositions</note> constitutes a whole through three concepts, each of which could represent an angle

## Desire.

The representation <note>original: *Vorstellung*; a mental image or concept</note> of a feeling that is in harmony with our own sense of self <note>original: *Selbstgefühle*; the internal awareness of one's own existence and state</note> produces desire. I call "harmonious" that which can be united with a need of the soul, the passions, or the body. Desire is a reasoned <note>original: *raisonnirte*; an effect processed through the mind's logic</note> effect of the drive for union.

is the object of desire—the actuality <note>original: *Würklichkeit*</note> of a desired possibility.

# The Resolution

is a <term>syllogism|A logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</term> on a large scale; it is a comparison and union of all the consequences of an action with a purpose established in the **imagination** <note>original: *Einbildung*</note>. Guided by experience, it seeks its means within the field of causes and distinguishes the obstacles within the sequence of consequences. It contains the entire set of instructions for all movements and feelings that the sustained power of the **will** is meant to execute, in order to reach the goal that the **mental representation** <note>original: *Vorstellung*; the internal image or concept of the goal</note> had established.

---

<column-break/>

# The Choice.

When two different feelings relate to one's **sense of self** <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*; the internal awareness of one's own state</note>, the latter inclines toward the most pleasant, withdraws from the most disagreeable, or is moved by the strongest.

Our entire **selfhood** <note>original: *Ichheit*; the essence of the "I" or the ego</note> acts in the same way when it compares the consequences of two actions with itself or with a purpose: it unites with the representation of the one that pleases it and flees from the one that is disagreeable to it. These opposing impulses constitute what is called **choice**. One could compare it to a scale. The fulcrum would be the selfhood, the scale beams would be the actions, and the considered consequences of the latter would be the weights.

Choice is a resolution between two objects.

Sometimes we choose without being able to find a **motive** <note>original: *Bewegungsgrund*; literally a "ground for movement"</note>, and in such cases, the objects of our choice have no relation to our selfhood: One should actually [call] such an action...

<summary>This page defines 'Resolution' as a large-scale logical process and 'Choice' as a mechanical balancing of feelings against the self, using the metaphor of a physical scale.</summary>
<keywords>resolution, choice, selfhood, sense of self, will, syllogism, imagination, motives</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on "The Choice" from pages 252-253. # The Choice.

...cannot actually be called a choice, for our **imagination** <note>original: *Vorstellungskraft*</note> finds no consequences that could move our **sense of self** <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*</note>. However, we may surmise from general experience—that everything occurs in a chain of causes and effects—that even in the most indifferent resolution, there must be a motive <note>original: *Bewegungsgrund*; literally a "ground for movement"</note>, whether so distant or so near that we cannot notice it. Moreover, this motive does not need to be strong at all; for the necessity of doing one thing or the other removes all resistance, and the slightest twitch of a muscle, or the most fleeting memory of something that once bordered on one of the two options, is sufficient to bring about a decision that, so to speak, hung by a hair.

The choice among objects indifferent to us is thus infallibly based on a preceding, hidden sensory perception which we have hardly noticed, and which is nonetheless connected to our choice by a thread whose physical effect is a mystery. Thus Comus <note>Nicolas-Philippe Ledru (1731–1807), a famous French illusionist known as "Comus" who performed feats of "rational recreation" and mentalism.</note>, who often guessed the resolutions of his spectators before he had even presented the objects they were to choose, once said to me: “Perhaps this gentleman would have chosen another number if he had arrived in a red-

<column-break/>

# The Choice.

lined carriage and not in a green one.”

Our decision without a visible motive resembles an event whose cause is unknown to us: we attribute it to chance <note>original: *Ohngefähr*</note> because we do not know how it was brought about, and so we believe we choose freely because we do not feel what moves us. But nothing is coincidence <note>original: *Zufall*</note>; and were we the creators of coincidence, then causes and effects would be useless, because we could incessantly interrupt and destroy them.

***

<summary>The author argues that "free choice" is often an illusion caused by our inability to perceive the minute, hidden motives or sensory triggers that influence our decisions. Using the example of an illusionist's trick, he suggests that every action is part of an unbreakable chain of cause and effect.</summary>

# The Will.

He, the monarch, who seems to initiate, order, and govern everything, is yet only a slave crowned by prejudice—a slave to his minister, **Feeling**, which dictates all his movements. Guided by various artful maneuvers, the Will commands its subjects, the limbs of the body; and because they grant him blind obedience, he believes, along with the spectators who are unfamiliar with his court, that he alone rules.

For us, there is no such thing as free will. One must always know what one wants; at least one thought must precede any action, and only **free thoughts**—which we do not possess—would produce that which we might then, to some extent, be permitted to call a "free will." <note>The author argues that because our thoughts are triggered by external stimuli or internal mechanics we don't control, the "will" resulting from those thoughts cannot be truly "free."</note>

Thus, the source of a **Primal Will** <note>original: *Urwille*</note> can only lie within eternal motion, which contains its own cause within itself. Such a will requires no thoughts to move, but rather creates thoughts by moving in an arbitrary and harmonious manner.

<column-break/>

# The Will.

...cision <note>original: *Entschließung*. This word completes the catchword "Ents-" from the previous page.</note> and purpose animate our will. One of these two must have passed into the **sense of self** <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*</note> to provide the entire substance of the soul with that specifically directed movement which constitutes the will.

The Will is the mover of all appetitive, rational, or "pre-felt" <note>original: *vorgefühlten*; referring to actions prompted by intuition or instinct before they are consciously processed.</note> actions of the soul and the body. Like any movement, it lies between two feelings: between a **representation** <note>original: *Vorstellung*; a mental image or idea</note> and a **decision**. However, one must not mistake it for a partial or simple movement; it is as distinct from a simple movement as the twitch of a muscle is from the immensely artful and complex application of force in a master-shot on a billiard table. The Will is the movement of the entire substance [of the soul], and it differs from movement itself in that it can at times halt and at times accelerate it; it changes and directs it; it presupposes a decision and pursues a purpose.

This decision is suspended within the **memory** and contains all the experiences, means, and precautions belonging to its purpose. All these sensations, ordered through reflection, effect a series of movements that correspond to them, and whose direction, velocity, or... <note>The text breaks off here at the catchword "Vers-," likely leading to "Verschiedenheit" (diversity/variation) on the next page.</note>

<summary>The author presents a deterministic view of the Will, comparing it to a monarch who is secretly ruled by "Feeling." He defines the Will not as a simple impulse, but as a complex movement of the soul's entire substance guided by memory and purpose.</summary>
<keywords>will, free will, feeling, soul, movement, decision, memory, determinism, Primal Will, billiards metaphor</keywords>

# The Will.

...lingering, being determined by these feelings. <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding how sensations guide the soul's movement.</note> The sum total of all these movements is the **Will**, and in this way it acts, supported by feelings, until it reaches the **final purpose** <note>original: *Endzweck*</note> that it desires.

This final purpose is the actual **driving wheel** <note>original: *Triebrad*; a mechanical metaphor common in Enlightenment philosophy to describe the primary motivator of a system, similar to a watch's mainspring.</note> of the will. A continuous, comfortable feeling of the image established in the **imagination** <note>original: *Einbildung*</note> maintains the attentive, appetitive drive within the **selfhood** <note>original: *Ichheit*; the internal sense of "I."</note>, guiding its primary movement through all the paths prescribed in the resolution, in order to achieve the desired action and to make the mental representation of it a reality. This entire interplay of feeling and movement toward a single purpose is the Will.

Can one act without willing? We perform bodily movements that occur without consciousness, and often even against our will; yet **convulsions**, **instinctive** movements, and those produced by a muscular sensation—imperceptible to the soul—are actions of the body or its individual parts, and not our own. — We do not act in these cases, so we do not need to "will" them.

But what happens when we act without a specific cause? — in an insignific-

<column-break/>

-ant action, when we say to ourselves: stretch out your hand!

To effect this, we establish in our imagination the image of such an extension, along with the series of all feelings and movements belonging to this action. We repeat the entire course of feelings that we received during the first and all subsequent hand-extensions, and these repeated feelings produce a movement from within that follows the same path taken by the one that first taught us to stretch out our hand.

Such an action occurs without a preceding desire, and even without a deliberate will, but nonetheless through the repetition of the will that had acted for the first time. — It is a **memory-will** <note>original: *Gedächtnißwille*</note>, as it were, which takes the place of the true rational will in unthinking actions; and for this reason, I stated above that the Will is also a mover of "pre-felt" actions.

The strongest **sophistical proof** <note>original: *sophistische Beweis*; referring to a deceptive or fallacious argument that appears plausible.</note> generally cited for the **freedom of the will** <note>original: *Willensfreyheit*</note> is the following: It is within our power to contradict all moral and physical motives that guide us, and to prove that we [can]...

<summary>The author defines the Will as a mechanical interplay between internal feelings and the imagination, driven by a "final purpose." He distinguishes between conscious actions, physical convulsions, and "memory-will," which allows us to perform habitual actions without active deliberation.</summary>
<keywords>Will, Final Purpose, Selfhood, Imagination, Instinct, Memory-will, Free Will, Determinism</keywords>

<page-num>260</page-num>
# The Will.

...freedom, even to be able to act irrationally and unnaturally. 

But what kind of force is this, which man can attach to the willing **lever** <note>original: *Hebel*</note> of his **resolution** <note>original: *Entschluß*</note>—through the mere abstract thought: *I will prove it!*? What is this pressure that outweighs all feelings which, without it, would direct our movement elsewhere? Does it not seem to be a special, free, living force that transcends all laws of motion and, just as a human hand lifts a weight, can overcome **moral gravity** <note>original: *moralische Schwerkraft*; an Enlightenment-era metaphor suggesting that our choices are "pulled" by our strongest desires or inclinations, much like objects are pulled by physical gravity.</note>?

The matter proceeds as follows. When I say to myself: *I will prove it!*, the same thing happens as we found in the previous action when we said: *I want to stretch out my hand!* Upon the command *I will prove it!*, there follows a **mental representation** <note>original: *Vorstellung*; a term used to describe an internal mental image or idea that triggers a physical response.</note> of that action which is intended to prove my freedom, and this works from the inside out to produce all the feelings and movements necessary to mechanically force the entire action. Between the presentation of the image and the action itself, no further will takes place; only the resolution remains, which compelled our will to choose this representation—to act as if it were free—to appear as if it wanted what, at its core, it does not want. This commanding

<page-num>261</page-num>
# The Will.

resolution, which sought to compare consequences with purpose through no choice of its own, was not free at all: for the circumstances under which we formed it did not depend on us. The more unusual it was, the more we had to be guided to depart from the natural course; likewise, with every forced choice, we will feel an unpleasant sensation similar to that which accompanies **indecision** <note>original: *Unentschlüßigkeit*</note>.

<page-num>262</page-num>
## Self-Awareness.

Mere sensation <note>original: *Fühlung*; refers to the basic physical capacity to sense or touch.</note> feels nothing but the pressure of a thing different from itself. **Self-awareness** <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*; in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to the internal "feeling of oneself" or the immediate consciousness of one's own existence.</note> feels its own substance.

To feel requires two forces: a pressing force and a pressed one. Both feel one another. However, one must have both together; one must be able to press oneself in order to feel oneself.

If two things are separate, they can press against each other; if both are united into one, they press themselves. But what can press itself? And where do two different forces constitute a single one?

The **elastic force** <note>The author uses "elasticity" as a vitalistic metaphor for the tension between expanding and contracting forces that creates life.</note> consists of two different movements, of which one is expanding and the other is contracting. These move, touch, and feel against each other. All of this takes place within the same substance, and in such a way, the simple, undivided force possesses a self-feeling.

---

<header>Self-Awareness.</header>
<page-num>263</page-num>
<sig>R 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: feel,</meta>

Since we have stated above that **organized bodies** <note>original: *organisirte Körper*; a term for living organisms such as plants and animals, seen as systems of "organized" matter.</note>—through an undivided force that connects their parts—contain the laws of their formation, preservation, and reproduction within themselves, the question arises: could organized bodies, muscles, and plants be endowed with self-awareness?

We cannot entirely deny it to them; they possess half of it, for they feel their own movements, but not their sensations. They feel external pressure and their own movements occurring within them; but they do not feel *that* they have felt. This requires a counter-pressure within the sensation, an **impenetrable foundation** within it, which they do not possess. This resistance within one's own sensation is **I-ness** <note>original: *Ichheit*; the quality of being a self-conscious "I" or ego.</note>.

The **instinct** that animates all organized bodies is indeed a summation of all their sensations, and its "cleverness" seems to constitute a whole that resembles an I-ness; however, I do not believe that this primary sensation can perceive, distinguish, and compare its substantial sensations. For instinct must always act in the same way in the same case, and lacks the **sad privilege** of thinking beings to be able to err between two different resolutions: it can [experience] nothing painful <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<header>Self-Awareness.</header>
<page-num>264</page-num>
<sig>R 5</sig>

...feel if its body is destroyed, because it never itself violates the laws given to it for its preservation; it also cannot recognize pleasure because it lacks a comparison with the opposite of displeasure. It feels neither well nor woe.

No one can imagine <term>self-awareness</term> <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*; here referring to the immediate, non-reflective consciousness of one's own existence.</note>. Imagination is a pictorial memory—the repetition of a past feeling. But the feeling of self is always present to us; indeed, it is the only "presence" we are capable of enjoying. Every moment of a physical sensation is already past by the time the moment arrives in which it touches our soul; and even that moment has vanished by the time we learn in our memory that we have felt something outside of ourselves. But self-awareness resonates alongside it perpetually; it is a fundamental truth that can be neither grasped nor imagined, but which we feel at all times, even unconsciously. Self-awareness is the essence of the soul: if it could leave the soul for a single moment, the latter would cease to be. It is said in the <term>philosophical school</term> <note>original: *philosophische Schule*; likely referring to the Rationalist tradition (such as Descartes or Wolff) which held that the soul’s essence is "cogitatio" or constant thought.</note> that the soul must constantly be thinking; this, I believe, is false:—it must **feel** without ceasing.

<column-break/>

<page-num>265</page-num>

Self-awareness gives us the feeling of a being—the only being we can feel without its properties—and it is also the only being we feel without the use of the senses.

All other feelings are produced within us by separate forces; for this reason, our pleasant sensations are interrupted by so many adverse ones and are so imperfect. Often our pleasure is merely the absence of pain; however, our happiness is always just a lesser degree of unhappiness. For we are never truly happy; we are only happier than this or that person with whom we compare ourselves. Even the greatest possible satisfaction of our drives grants neither lasting peace nor satisfying well-being. It is more a matter of hope than of enjoyment.

Only the harmonious feeling of the undivided <term>substance</term> <note>original: *Substanz*; in this context, the fundamental, unchanging essence of the individual soul.</note> of a being can promise us true contentment. We feel, however, no other undivided, simple substance than our own; for this reason, the joys we can find within ourselves are always the best. Health, love, and cheerfulness without cause are our greatest <term>blessings</term> <note>original: *Glückseligkeiten*; a term implying supreme happiness or a state of grace.</note>. But if we could feel other beings besides our own, we would surely taste a pleasure of which...

<summary>The author argues that self-awareness is the constant, underlying essence of the soul, distinct from fleeting sensory perceptions or mental imaginations. He challenges the traditional philosophical view that the soul is defined by constant thought, proposing instead that it is defined by constant feeling.</summary>
