<page-num>266</page-num>
<header>Self-Awareness.</header>
<meta>catchword: vers</meta>

of which we can form no concept. How infinitely happy, however, would we be if we could have feeling and union with the **Universal Being**—with our source, with the whole of the undivided force, after which all of nature strives and with which everything seeks to unite?

Thus we can rightly sense that a perfect happiness, a true peace, awaits us, because union seems to be the <term>destination</term> <note>original: *Bestimmung*; can also mean "vocation" or "purpose."</note> of all things.

There lies within us a strange contradiction between the impatience with which we wish to leap from one desired point in time to another, and the natural reluctance to approach the end of our days. The former is the irresistible **urge for union** <note>original: *Vereinigungstrieb*</note>, the urge to fulfill a happier destiny, which lashes us, as it were, through time into eternity. The latter, however, arises from the fear, clouded with dark uncertainty, of failing our destiny for eternity; for all concepts are relative to our senses; we see everything around us arise and pass away, and this gives us the doubt whether we might not, through a separation from our senses, [lose] ourselves

---

<page-num>267</page-num>
<header>Self-Awareness.</header>
<meta>catchword: Be-</meta>

lose ourselves. This thought, which contradicts the essence of our soul, gives birth to the fear of death. But precisely this revolting horror of annihilation, and primarily the ever-present nature of <term>self-awareness</term> <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*; the immediate internal awareness of existence.</note>, are sensations which should convince us of the eternity of this inner being.

We know what similarity time and space have with one another: Just as the parts of the originally simple force, which have retained the urge for union, relate to space—moving their coverings toward where the greatest mass of their own kind resides together, tearing their bonds and seeking their lost, immeasurable unity—in the same way, spirits seem to relate to time. Eternity is their essence and their destination. They have lost it since they have been enclosed in time; eternity is the **present**, and here they know only that which is past or what may be future. The constant effort of these spirits is therefore to overtake the presence of the future, and to imagine their past sensations as present through the power of memory. Connected with the interrupted existence of self-awareness, these

## Selfhood.

<term>Selfhood</term> <note>original: *Ichheit*; a philosophical term referring to "I-ness" or the quality of being an "I." It represents the core identity of the individual.</note> is, among all ideas, the most incomprehensible and at the same time the most certain, because our <term>existence</term> <note>original: *Daseyn*.</note> is the only truth that we cannot doubt at all. It cannot be demonstrated, for it is the simplest of all feelings, just as it is the most powerful; we also believe in it to a higher degree than all other truths: and it convinces our entire being more forcefully than the **principle of contradiction** <note>original: *principium contradictionis*. This is a fundamental law of logic stating that something cannot be both true and false at the same time. The author argues that the "feeling" of being alive is even more certain than this logical rule.</note> convicts our understanding; for the feeling of our existence is the yardstick of all our certainty; and we have only this single true certainty, because we feel nothing immediately but ourselves, and outside of us no other beings, but only the properties of beings, or perhaps even only the properties of our senses. 1)

1) Although every important demonstration requires that it be traceable back to an undeniable principle or a definition, yet all arithmetic truths, which we [consider] to be infallible...

->Selfhood has two main characteristics: to feel that it feels itself, and to know that it feels itself and not something else.<-

...stablest hold, upon a thing for which we can give no sufficient explanation, and—this thing is called: **One!**

&emsp;But what is an explanation? Is it anything more than a distinguishing observation of properties that we have received through the senses? Its certainty, therefore, rests only upon the reliability that we can attribute to our sensory perceptions. **Unity** <note>original: *Einheit*; also translated as "Oneness."</note> is not in this case; it cannot be explained through any sensory concepts, because it is not composed of them like other things. It has no properties; it is <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*.</note>, and its image is the true, meaningful <term>hieroglyph of essence</term> <note>The author uses "hieroglyph" here in a 18th-century philosophical sense, meaning a sacred or hidden symbol that represents a fundamental truth of reality.</note>; it can only be felt, and for that very reason, it is actual truth. We comprehend it just as little, and believe in it just as much, as our own self-being. Our existence, and the existence of all things, constitutes its reality; its idea is innate to us; it is inseparable from the inner, independent feeling of our **Selfhood**, and for that reason is just as undoubtedly true as our existence.

&emsp;When the soul distinguishes all foreign feelings from its self-feeling—and this is its first action—then each of these ne-

<column-break/>

-gations is presumably recorded in the memory, and certainly with the same sign; for these negations are all equal to one another; of each, the soul feels and says nothing further than: *this is something other than I!*—thus they are nothing but identical negations.

&emsp;In this way, the reality of an intuitive **plurality** <note>original: *Mehrheit*.</note> arises in the memory, composed of nothing but identities, and consequently the essence of **numbers**, which consists of units.

&emsp;In the following stages, this intuitive plurality is gathered and divided by the "unifying drive" <note>original: *Vereinigungstrieb*; the natural mental impulse to group individual things into a whole.</note>, and every variety is distinguished by a sign; and this is the **invention of numbers**. The unification of these we call **Adding** or **Multiplying**; the separation of them, **Subtracting** and **Dividing**. In this manner, the <term>art of reckoning</term> <note>original: *Rechenkunst*; an older term for basic arithmetic.</note> arose; it is a work of comparison and distinction, and has no other material than **Unity**, from whose repetition all numbers are composed. But for this very reason, mathematical demonstrations are more evidently certain than all others built upon a definition.

An organized body feels its own movement through the undivided force which constitutes its structure and its instinct—but not its **feeling** <note>original: *Fühlung*. In this context, the author distinguishes between the physical "instinct" of a body and the higher, internal capacity for sentience or "feeling" itself.</note>.

The **sense of self** <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*.</note> associated with **Selfhood** <term>Ichheit</term> feels its entire substance—its movement and its feeling simultaneously. It is as if a foundation exists there, which lies behind the feeling and appears to be more than the feeling itself—a "Something" that is capable of having the sensation of feeling.

Just as one cannot see oneself without a mirror-like resistance, we cannot feel our feelings in any other way than through a resistance in the **primary feeling**.

This is the impenetrable point that I have attributed to Selfhood; it is, as it were, the mirror of feelings into which they all fall, from which all movements originate, and in whose immeasurable depth the multitude of **miniatures** <note>The author refers to "miniatures" as the small, internal images or representations that constitute our memory.</note> find their place, filling the memory.

Feeling is always a reaction, which generally has its direction against movement; but here, it is directed against itself. In the center of Selfhood, the otherwise interrupted series of pressure and counter-pressure ceases, and from this center, it begins again to go backward—

This happens in the following way: It compares its partial feelings with the feeling of itself, and distinguishes the former from the latter. <page-num>274</page-num>
# Selfhood, Consciousness.

S 2

...the great difference between the physical **sense of self** <note>original: *Selbstgefühl*. This refers to a raw, bodily awareness of existence common to all living things.</note> and **Selfhood** <term>Ichheit</term>. Organized bodies have a feeling of self without knowing it—without **memory**, which would present the enjoyment of their existence to them. Their merely physical sense of self gives them a being that they cannot enjoy. But thinking beings have a feeling of themselves that is connected with memory. They can distinguish such a feeling from other feelings, and this operation of the soul is the first of all those undertaken by a human being as a child.

Memory is therefore that which reveals and gives birth to the **Selfhood** generated by the sense of self; without it, we would know nothing of ourselves. Memory is the innermost secret of the soul, and it lies unerringly buried in that point of Selfhood which is impenetrable to our powers and to all others.

Selfhood is therefore a sense of self connected with memory and distinguished from other feelings.

<column-break/>

<page-num>275</page-num>
**Consciousness** <note>original: *Bewußtseyn*.</note> is the representation of **Selfhood**. It relates to the latter as a recollection does to a feeling. With every feeling, we perceive our Selfhood; and, since all our recollections are marked with the stamp of the sense of self, every one of them is a consciousness. The latter seems to us more and greater than Selfhood because memory quickly runs through a long series of recollections and encompasses entire years of Selfhood, but fundamentally, consciousness is only the memory-sign of Selfhood—a connection of the **concept** we have of it with a series of repeated sensations.

> “Would our consciousness cease in the event that we had no memory?”

Without memory, we would be nothing at all: we would have only individual, successive feelings which we could never collect into the completion of a **concept** <note>original: *Begriff*.</note>; we would never be able to make a comparison or attain an **experience**.

If the chain of our memories were completely torn apart, we would cease to be the same person we are, and would have to start with a new series of sensations to become a different person. The sense of self would likely continue uninterrupted, but we would know nothing of it; we would remain the same person—for others, but not for ourselves.

However, we can forget a part of our lives and yet always find ourselves again in the undeniable certainty of a continuous sense of self: for every glance we cast back into memory shows us that we are at home, because both—the sense of self and memory—lie in the same substance. Memory, however, remains forever the ultimate secret of the soul, its most precious possession, the treasure chamber of all its knowledge, and the bond of its entire being, without which it would lose itself.

To provide more clarity, I must repeat myself somewhat and ask my readers to consider carefully: that although I have described matter as a combination of forces;—yet, from what kind of forces? —from nothing but separate forces, which indeed feel and move one another mutually, yet have no **sense of self**, no **will**, and no **consciousness**. These are properties that I have attributed solely to the **undivided, elastic substance** <note>In 18th-century physiology, an "elastic substance" was often proposed as the medium through which the soul communicated with the nerves.</note>, and only when it possesses the rare privilege of **Selfhood** <term>Ichheit; the philosophical concept of a distinct, individual "I"</term>. This constitutes the divine essence of our soul, and a simple elastic substance endowed with Selfhood, knowledge, and memory seems to me to represent a being just as respectable and just as immaterial as a **Monad** <term>Monad; according to Leibniz, an indivisible, simple starting point of all reality</term> or a thing of which we say that it is of a nature unknown to us.

However, since I have assigned to the body and to every muscle a portion of undivided substance and a rational-like instinct, which unites and guards their organization, one might perhaps conclude from this that an artificial composition of these "guards" of the human body could constitute that which we call the soul—especially if one does not

precisely notice and think through what I have said about instinct and the body. However, I have shown that instinct must obey a certain law, and consequently does not need to *think*; that it is nothing other than a mechanical order of forces for a single purpose; that the body is composed of muscles and organs, each of which has its own particular instinct and performs purposeful tasks without the knowledge or will of the whole. The body constitutes a whole for itself that can perform all its actions mechanically without the necessity of **Reason**, which is particularly observed in **fools** and **sleepwalkers** <note>The author refers to "fools" (the mentally ill) and sleepwalkers as evidence that the physical body can act on its own "autopilot" without the conscious "I" being involved.</note>. In contrast, **catalepsy** <note>A medical condition where a person becomes unresponsive and rigid but may remain conscious of their surroundings.</note> proves the existence of a particular being that thinks within the body and is no longer connected with it and its actions, because in this state we are conscious and perform all operations of the soul without being able to move the body.

If, therefore, one even encounters proofs in my system that a being distinct from the body dwells within it, then I do not see why the existence of the soul should become doubtful just because I make its essence more comprehensible...

# Harmony.

If our body had only nerves and no senses, then through all contact we would feel nothing other than that something exists outside of us; and if two contacts arrived from opposite sides at the same time, we would merely distinguish their directions. At most, we might obtain an obscure idea of the varying size, duration, or strength of such contacts through comparisons; but even then, that artful interaction of surfaces upon the organized nerves—which constitutes the nature of a sense and forms that general, fundamental sense called feeling <note>The author uses 'Fühlen' here to refer to the sense of touch, which was often considered the primary or "base" sense from which others were derived.</note>—would already begin.

The senses, especially hearing and sight, are ingenious gateways into the soul, which

through their structure, give a specific form to a certain movement and convey it to the self-feeling <note>The term 'Selbstgefühl' (self-feeling) in 18th-century psychology refers to an internal sense of one's own bodily existence and state of being.</note> as a harmonious perception; they are mechanical tools that the soul uses to multiply the movements of its substance, to break down the main movement into partial ones, to learn new movements, and thereby to provide itself with new and diverse feelings. To create diversity out of unity is the great task—the drive pursued by our desire to be happy: self-feeling is the unity; the senses are the means to multiply it, and harmony is the rule that divides this unity and orders that diversity. Harmony awakens in us a sensory pleasure for which we cannot give a clear account, because it is a feeling <note>The original 'Fühlung' implies an immediate, tactile-like internal sensation rather than a reasoned thought.</note>.

Sound was the first object that developed the concept of harmony within us. Through analogy, we later perceived it in colors as well. The three dimensions of length, breadth, and thickness, whose proportions form the beauty of a body, teach us that there is also a harmony of forms; and perhaps a theory will yet be discovered through which one can determine the harmony of

Here a great and difficult question arises: does harmony reside in the object or in the senses? This shall serve to develop my thoughts on the exaggerated opinions regarding sensory illusions.

Truly, no important novelty has been discovered when it is found that an eye is necessary for the visible and an ear for the audible; but how is it possible to doubt the reality of the visible and audible when one has eyes and ears?

One cannot deny that the properties of bodies are not exactly what they appear to be to the senses for their own sake; however, I am firmly convinced that they must contain the causes within themselves as to why they are always felt in the same way when they act upon the same senses. A surface is not red or yellow in itself, but it must be structured in a specific way to always give these sensations to healthy eyes. A

Sound must be a real thing, because it acts upon our ear; it must be distinct and different from others in a specific way, because we feel its particular distinction: but whether the division of the light ray into red, yellow, and blue, and of the tone into the prime, third, and fifth <term>the 1st, 3rd, and 5th notes of a musical scale, forming a major chord</term> is an essential, unchangeable, true division of these unities? That is a question that can hardly be fully resolved. It would therefore be more useful to investigate why harmonic proportions give us pleasure?

Every harmonic system seems to me to be a unity that dissolves into three parts, which are entirely different from it and from one another, but, when taken together again, restore the same unity. ...enter purely, and if it could be specified with equal strength, it would certainly sound exactly like its <term>prime|The fundamental or root note of a musical scale.</term>.

Every figure has three diameters that represent all its proportions simultaneously, and perhaps there are also only three main categories of salt: bitter, sweet, and sour. <note>In the 18th century, "salts" were often used as a chemical shorthand for the fundamental building blocks of matter and taste.</note>

Thus, the harmonic scale seems to proceed by threes, whereas we otherwise tend to create all our scales by halves and divide them into equal numbers. But these measures created by us are all arbitrarily adopted, and we know no measure of our own for "nothingness": we have no reason why we should cut a thing into exactly so many parts, and an accurate equality of division is impossible in the physical world. # and Harmony.

Might we not then suspect that the pleasure produced in us by the proportions of harmony [arises from this]? And that a primary color and its secondary color, such as blue and green, do not look as well together as two primary or two secondary colors?

Allow me to represent harmonic unity as a triangle, whose angles are called red, yellow, and blue, or Prime, Third, and Fifth; each side of these angles, however, shall represent the middle tone or the secondary color. We know that we cannot recognize a triangle from a single angle alone; however, if we know two angles, or one angle and the base opposite to it—likewise, if two sides are fully known to us—then the entire triangle is revealed to us. If one sketches this image, one will find that within it, all harmonic tones and colors constitute either two angles, or two sides, or one angle with its base; conversely, the adjacent or otherwise dissonant tones and colors indicate only one angle and the length of the side bordering it, and consequently provide no knowledge of the whole. Since, however, knowledge of the whole is better than the isolated feeling of a part of it, we can explain from all this the pleasure or displeasure caused by regularly or falsely paired tones and colors.

->T<-

<note>original: "Harmonie, Triangel, Prime, Terze, Quinte, Farben, Töne, Dreizahl, Durchmesser, Maaßstab, Salz"</note>

# On the Senses

...never cause [discord], stems from the fact that we recognize in them the truth of an essential measure? For the harmonic feeling reveals to us two of the rarest and noblest things: **Essence** and **Truth**; two sisters who usually show themselves more to feeling than to the intellect. 3)

---

3) We make such comparisons through the rules of harmony in colors as well as in forms, but especially in music, all of which are taken from the essence of the matter itself. We compare parts of the very same whole, and this must provide us with more pleasure than when we compare foreign things together; for the true proportions are those which a thing has with itself; through them, we learn something of the essence of the whole; we feel the multiplicities of its unity, for all harmonic multiplicities of a unity are nothing other than proportions of itself. The more sensually <note>Meaning "through the senses."</note> we recognize such things, the truer they are for us, and the less effort we have to enjoy them: we feel them; however, as often as we compare foreign things with one another, we have the trouble of passing a judgment, and receive in return only a relative truth: whereas, when we compare essential parts of unity, we feel a double truth without effort: the essential truth of the thing, and the relative truth that we have felt it.

<column-break/>

# and Harmony.

The strange, hitherto unexplained phenomenon of unison (<term>Einklang|The phenomenon of two notes sounding at the exact same pitch, or sympathetic resonance.</term> <note>original: "Unisons"</note>) gives us strong proof that at least the harmony of tone has its basis in the object, and that sound is a real thing. How could a string, tuned identically to another, not only sound the same tone but also be seen trembling before our eyes if the sound of the first string were not grounded in the string itself, but only in our ear—if it were only an illusion and not a reality? Does there not seem to be a harmonic proportion at work here—a law of <term>assimilation|The author uses the term "Verähnlichung," suggesting a natural law where one movement excites or attracts another of its kind.</term>, according to which a shaped movement excites and attracts another of its kind?

The connection of certain properties of the same body among themselves can give us proof that what we call "properties" are also not mere appearances of our senses. The physician concludes the nature of the illness from the complexion and the voice—he knows that a herb, because it is bitter, must at the same time be <term>hot|In traditional humoral medicine, "hot" refers to a property that stimulates or warms the body's internal state.</term> or stomach-strengthening.

There are thus certain correspondences of various properties in relation to the body to which they are attributed; consequently there must also

In general, I do not see why we strive to increase the possible, and even sometimes actual, deceptions of our senses through imagination and sophistry <note>Sophistry refers here to clever but fallacious reasoning intended to deceive.</note>, rather than seeking to diminish them through attention and experience. He who sees a rare phenomenon for the first time—one that contradicts his principles—certainly does better if he steps closer and investigates it precisely, than if he walks away and asserts that his eye or someone else intended to deceive him. We simply have no other truth than that which we feel; and it has not been proven at all whether there are truths that cannot be felt. The senses, to be sure, are not pure feeling; they heighten and enchant it through their artificial <note>In this context, "artificial" (künstlich) refers to the complex, mechanical, or technical processes of the organ, rather than something "fake."</note> movements; yet one only needs to subtract the artificiality from it, and truth remains.

## Of Sight and Hearing.

### Seeing.

We are taught in school-level optics <note>The author is referring to the standard scientific textbooks of the 18th century, likely based on Newtonian principles.</note> that the process of seeing happens through light rays which fall from the surfaces of bodies into our eye and paint their shapes and colors upon its retina.

In this teaching, I find something incomprehensible, namely: how light rays—which, bouncing off the curves of bodies, diverge in such different directions—can reunite in the small space of the eye to represent an entire figure or even a whole landscape. Individual parts of objects, and indeed only those directly opposite the eye, can certainly be represented to it by straight-moving light rays; but how is it possible that they show the eye those sides of the bodies,

from which they cannot originate, because these sides turn their light rays away from the eye?

When I compare the **diameter** <note>The straight-line distance through the center of the eyeball.</note> of the eye with the size of the space it encompasses, and when I consider that all forms located within this space—with their reciprocal curves—are imaged in the eye; I cannot explain the problem of seeing, supposedly produced by incoming light rays, in any other way than by assuming: that all light rays bouncing off so many objects must aim at the **point of sight** <note>The central point of the retina or the focus of vision.</note> like a **target**, in order to be able to crowd into it.

Would it not be easier to say: that a particular **visual power** <note>A "living force" or active energy belonging to the observer, rather than the eye being a passive receiver.</note> lies hidden within the human being, which goes out from him and seeks out external objects?

I believe, therefore, that the functions of sight could occur in the following manner:

Above all, one must imagine light as a continuous and, as it were, *fluid* substance, but by no means as a heap of rays; for rays are expressions of force from an elastic substance—they are shocks and

strengthened movements of the same according to specific directions.

The **light matter** <note>The author uses the pre-modern concept of "light matter" (Lichtmaterie), suggesting light is a physical fluid or ether that fills space.</note> is the medium that connects our visual organs with visible objects—an **elastic contiguity** <term>Contiguity: a state of being in direct contact; a continuous connection without gaps.</term>, through which surfaces that stand opposite one another are linked and act upon each other.

This light matter presses upon the eye constantly—more or less, according to whether it is moved more densely or thinly, strongly or weakly. But when the eye lingers particularly on an object, or is stimulated to look at it, the mass of light resting on the eyeball breaks through its **crystalline envelopes** <note>The cornea and the lens of the eye.</note>, gathers itself in a cone shape toward the point of sight, and through it touches an **inner being** that possesses a **living force**. This being reacts to the light-cone that has penetrated; its counter-pressure travels the very same path that the pressure had taken. Consequently, it takes on the inverted cone-shape of the pressure, flows out from the point of sight like rays in the form of a conical bundle, and encompasses the vast space which we call the **visual field**.

This powerful bundle of rays now falls upon all objects which are located in the arc

According to this system of mine, seeing takes exactly the opposite path from what we are taught: **not the objects shoot their rays into our eye, but the latter shoots its own onto them.** For the rest, however, all other optical laws remain as they are generally accepted.

One might object that light rays, once they have touched a surface impenetrable to them, immediately continue their path, more or less refracted; that they therefore cannot return to the eye to form the above-mentioned effects therein.

other luminous bodies. The visual rays are rectilinear effects of a purposefully acting force in the substance of light: they are shocks or pressures that proceed until they find a counter-pressure. This hypothesis of mine—that the living force of man primarily performs the **business of seeing** <note>The active process of visual perception.</note>—is particularly consistent with the tangible idea we have: that we perceive external objects at a certain distance, and not within ourselves upon our eye.

Finally, it also solves the difficult question: How is it possible that such a vast multitude of light rays can cross each other incessantly without becoming confused? One no longer needs to assume that they must bend or penetrate each other; for the visual ray is a

<meta>catchword: We</meta>

How is it that various bodies, during a strong vibration, tremble to such an extent that one sees and feels their quivering, yet they emit no sound?

Why is the crack of a whip heard much more loudly than the passing of a cannonball, even though the air is divided infinitely more violently by the latter and pressed against the ear?

Why is the report of a rifle loaded with powder far louder than that of an air gun <note>original: "Windbüchse"; a pneumatic rifle that uses compressed air instead of gunpowder.</note>, even if the force of each were equal in terms of propulsion?

How is it that one can recognize by sound alone whether glass or wood is breaking? That one distinguishes not only the various cries of animals, but also the noises of countless inanimate bodies from one another? What kind of language is this?

From all these questions and many others that could still be asked, it can be inferred that hearing has much greater mysteries than sight. Furthermore, we have progressed much further in optics <term>optics: the scientific study of sight and the behavior of light</term> than in acoustics <term>acoustics: the branch of physics concerned with the properties of sound</term>. How poor is the artificial ear in comparison with the artificial eye! What is a speaking trumpet <term>speaking trumpet: a cone-shaped device used to amplify a person's voice</term> compared to the microscope, or an ear trumpet <term>ear trumpet: a tubular or funnel-shaped device used to assist the hearing-impaired</term> compared to a Herschelian telescope <note>original: "Hirschlischen Tubus"; referring to the powerful reflecting telescopes designed by William Herschel.</note>?

<page-num>302</page-num>
<header>Hearing.</header>

While we possess a multitude of optical truths, we have almost not a single acoustic one that proves truly useful. Theaters have been built in so many countries for a long time, yet we still have no definite fundamental rules <note>original: "bestimmte Grundregeln"</note> on how they should be constructed in order to be able to hear within them with certainty.

The nature of sound and the sense of hearing have piqued my scientific curiosity and occupied me longer than all other physical subjects, yet without my finding anything satisfying. Nevertheless, I will venture to leave my thoughts on the matter to more fortunate thinkers.

Air indisputably plays the greatest role in the business of hearing. It is the domain of the ear and seems to contain the components from which sounds, which fly back and forth within it, are composed. However, no sound can be produced by air alone; a solid body is always required to resist it or to communicate its vibrations to it. There are bodies whose tremblings are visible and yet are not heard. On the other hand, there are palpable movements in the air—gusts of wind that can knock one over without being heard; for the

<meta>catchword: wind</meta>

---

<page-num>303</page-num>
<header>Hearing.</header>

wind is only audible when it rubs against solid bodies. Every audible object, therefore, takes its origin in certain specific movements of a solid body, which are communicated to the air and seem to take on a formal shape within it.

Such an object always consists of two sounds: a musical one and an articulated one—a self-sounding <note>original: "selbstlautenden"; the German word for vowel is "Selbstlauter."</note> and a co-sounding <note>original: "mitlautenden"; the German word for consonant is "Mitlauter."</note> tone. The latter seems to be the shell which encloses the former;—as it were, a body of which the musical tone is the soul. For even the most impure sound, e.g., the fall of a wool sack <note>original: "Fall eines Wollsacks"; a common historical reference for a dull, muffled thud.</note>, contains a tone that is admittedly not musically perceptible because its vibrations are too slow for our ear; and this tone is accompanied by a dull noise which, as indistinct as it is, we nevertheless know how to distinguish from all others.

Every letter has a form that encloses a breath <note>original: "Hauch"</note>, which always constitutes a musical tone. These forms emerge from our mouths and can maintain themselves in the air for a time without dissolving, which the effects of the echo primarily prove.

From all of this, I suspect that the substance of musical sound is connected with the expansive force <note>term: "expansive Kraft"; a contemporary physical concept referring to the outward pressure or elasticity of gases like air.</note>,

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

and the articulated form, which encloses it, has a community with the compressive one.

The rising of sound, especially of words in the air, brought me to this thought. I find therein a particular similarity to hot air balloons <note>original: "Luftballonen"; the author is likely referencing the then-recent invention of the Montgolfier brothers in the 1780s</note>. Our breath <note>original: "Odem"</note> seems to me to be the substance of the vowels in speech: it is a warm haze—for one does not speak by blowing—mixed with flammable air, and consequently lighter than atmospheric air. If one now assumes that the mechanical pressure of the organs of speech can briefly give a form to the breath, or that a compressive force emanates from the living force to surround it, then the aerostatic word is complete; and should words form in this manner, then other sounds could likely be shaped in a similar way.

All bodies contain within themselves the substances of compressive and expansive forces, but air is preeminently composed of them; it contains unbound particles of light and fire, and is alternately contracted by cold or expanded by heat. Now, when a solid body disturbs the air through its movements, vibrations form within it that enclose a shaped space 

<meta>catchword: enclose</meta>

---

<page-num>305</page-num>
# Hearing

enclose. This airy form consists without fail of a denser air that surrounds a thinner one; for its entire substance is nothing but air. Through the back-and-forth striking of the vibrations, the inner space is naturally thinned and its circumference thickened. Thus, it is mostly fire-particles that fill this inner space, and light-particles—if indeed light is the true substance of compressive force—that form the circumference or the shape.

In this way, sounds might roughly arise in the air; they float in it like soap bubbles, and the buoyant force that fills them expands them until they become thin enough that they can no longer offer counter-pressure and they dissolve.

When such disturbances in the air originate from a body that contains many expansive and compressive forces, I believe that through a harmonic affinity of these forces with those in the air, it produces a purer and stronger sound; and that herein lies the sounding quality of bodies—what is usually called **sonorous bodies** <note>original: "les corps sonores"</note>. Metal and wood emit a brighter and more perceptible tone than straw or wool.

# Hearing

One more daring word about harmony or unison <note>original: "Einklang oder unison"</note>!

The ear is receptive to all sounds, and the air is tuned to all tones, because both possess an indefinite amount of compressive and expansive forces; whereas a solid body knows only a single, specifically musical tone that moves it. Could it not be that air lies distributed within bodies according to harmonic proportions? Could one not dream with Plato <note>The author refers to Plato's *Timaeus*, where the soul of the world and the elements are structured according to musical and mathematical ratios</note> and say: that these measured quantities stand in a musical connection with one another, and that one tone calls out to another?

If a vibration of the air must have a specific form to become audible, this explains the question: Why one hears the crack of a whip more loudly than the passing of a cannonball; for the latter causes no vibration in the air, and the two parallel lines of its path form no figure: the whip, however, describes a circle; it only cracks when its tip loops around in such a way that the cord is touched by it.

After I have endeavored to examine the objects of hearing, I come to the mechanism of this sense itself, which however

<column-break/>

<page-num>307</page-num>

# Hearing

is almost unfathomable. The way in which it is commonly imagined is very unsatisfactory to me. It is claimed that various specific vibrations fall into our ear and produce on the eardrum <term>Trommelhaut: the tympanic membrane</term> the countless variety of sensations necessary to distinguish all possible sounds from one another. But these sounds would all have to pass directly in front of the ear's opening to be able to act through their pressure: yet we know that we hear very clearly what happens behind us and before us. If we want to assume that the vibrations or the form of an audible object are so large that they fill the entire listening area, then I cannot understand how they can crowd into the small opening of the ear: but if the forms of sounds are small, how can each reach so many ears that are sometimes gathered in one place? How do these sounds not mix together, or how do they avoid one another?

When I consider that among various noises or conversations, one can listen to a single one preferentially without being confused by the others; then I am convinced that what I said in the previous article about our own innate visual power <note>The author is likely referring to the "active" theory of perception, where the eye and ear are not just passive receivers but actively "reach out" to process information</note> applies even more urgently to

hearing should be applied, and that we possess a special **hearing power** <term>Hörkraft: the innate capacity or vital energy of the ear to perceive sound</term>, which seeks out its objects.

Moreover, it seems to me that the tool of **hypothesis** is never more permissible than when a matter is in itself almost inexplicable.

I thus seek to make the operation of hearing comprehensible to myself in the following way:

Hearing reigns in the air much as sight does in the light. Both substances press incessantly upon the sensory organ that is so akin to them: but when the ear is stimulated by an unusual agitation of the air, **hearing rays** <note>The author is proposing a theory of hearing analogous to "extramission" theories of vision, where the sense organ sends out energy to "meet" the incoming stimulus</note> proceed from it, which have a similarity in their nature to visual rays, but receive a completely different turn through the structure of the ear, which differs from that of the eye; for the counter-pressure of the **living force** <term>lebendige Kraft: a historical term (vis viva) for what we now call kinetic energy, often used by 18th-century thinkers to describe vital or active forces in nature</term> returns through the snail-shaped canal of the ear <note>original: "schneckenförmigen Kanal"—referring to the cochlea</note>, and spreads out in whirling circles. In this way, the hearing power can traverse a very wide space and instantly seek out those sounds whose partial pressure had summoned it. It sends out the whirling cone of its hearing rays:

# Hearing

Every point of the audible object returns the pressure of the ray that strikes it, and the diversity of these reactions gives to this ray of our living force all the manifold **vibrations** which constitute the **sensations** of hearing.

# Nerves

A volatile spirit is the finest thing we know of in matter, and **nerve-fluid** <term>Nervensaft: a theoretical 18th-century substance thought to flow through nerves, similar to the classical "animal spirits"</term> is the very finest thing we assume to exist in the bodies of the animal kingdom. However, all volatile spirits appear in a moist form when their scattered parts unite to become visible. Therefore, I consider the opinion that views **nerve-force** as a spiritual fluid to be the most probable; especially when I consider that tight binding interrupts sensation, and when I notice how slow, unresponsive, and sluggish **phlegmatic bodies** <note>In the theory of the four humors, a phlegmatic person was thought to have an excess of phlegm, making them calm or stolid</note> are, I am inclined to believe that even a coarse moisture can mix with the nerve-spirit.

From the natural immobility and apparent lack of feeling in a nerve itself, one can conclude that it is not the nerve, but something powerful acting within it—that the nerve is in itself a dead shell, a mere canal in which an invisible essence circulates. But to what could one more readily attribute impact, feeling, movement, analogy with spirit and matter, extreme fineness, and

<column-break/>

<page-num>311</page-num>

# Nerves

and speed, than to the **elastic substance**? I therefore believe that the power of the nerves consists of an **elastic vapor** <note>original: "elastischen Dunst"—the author is using "vapor" to describe a state of matter even more refined than fluid</note>, which is the medium that connects soul and body through feeling and movement. This vapor-like spirit seems to me to play the same role between the two as light and air represent between external objects and our senses. Upon it act the rays of light and the vibrations of air, when they entrust to it the counter-actions of forms, which it brings back to the soul as sensations.

I find it highly probable that for every mechanical movement, a specific nerve is designated, which the soul pulls like a thread. At the very least, there is no better way to explain how it happens that we can move every joint of our fingers at will without moving the arm or the hand.

If every movement has its nerve, then every kind of feeling could well have its own, and I am very much inclined toward the ingenious recent opinion of a **philosophical physician** <note>Likely a reference to contemporary medical theorists like Johann August Unzer or Albrecht von Haller, who were beginning to distinguish between motor and sensory functions</note> most esteemed by me: that one could appropriately distinguish nerves into "moving" and "feeling" and assume that they, through a...

<summary>The author proposes that nerves are hollow canals for a "nerve-fluid" or "elastic vapor" that mediates between the physical body and the soul. He also suggests the modern-sounding idea that specific nerves are dedicated to specific movements and sensations.</summary>

<keywords>nerves, nerve-fluid, vitalism, soul and body, elastic vapor, physiology, motor nerves, sensory nerves, 18th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the nervous system, specifically moving from the nature of the "nerve-fluid" to the functional separation between sensation and movement, and then critiquing contemporary theories of muscle irritability.</meta>

<page-num>312</page-num>

...be governed by a **primary sensation**, which guards and rules the entire structure of the machine <note>The "machine" refers to the human body, a common metaphor in Enlightenment-era physiology</note>.

The cases of **catalepsy** <term>Cataleptische Zufälle: a condition characterized by muscle rigidity and a lack of response to external stimuli, though the person may remain conscious</term> show us a state in which one can feel everything and yet cannot move at all. Does such a complete separation between **sensation** and **movement** not become more understandable if we assume that the tools for both are different?

Another strong proof of this opinion is the consideration that if sensation and movement resided in the same nerve at the same time, one would hinder and confuse the other. How great would the confusion be in two such different tasks if sensations and movements could encounter one another in the same channel?

<page-num>313</page-num>

## Muscles.

I fear that the **Hallerian discovery** <note>Refers to Albrecht von Haller (1708–1777), a Swiss physician who famously distinguished between "sensibility" (nerves) and "irritability" (muscles)</note> of the **irritability** <term>Reizbarkeit: the inherent ability of muscle fibers to contract when touched or stimulated</term> of our muscles—which we do not feel—will give rise to many a physiological error. From the aforementioned phenomenon, it was immediately concluded that the muscles have no feeling, just as it was concluded from the immobility of a nerve when pricked that nerves are not irritable.

But can one form a satisfactory concept of movement and sensation if one completely separates these two interlocking essences of force through an **unproven postulate** <term>Heische-Satz: a philosophical term for a demand or a premise taken for granted without proof</term>? How and why could an irritable body move powerfully and correctly if it had not felt the stimulus beforehand? And what purpose would sensations serve if they were not most precisely connected with movements? How should both act upon each other if they did not intimately touch one another?

In the case of the apparent non-irritability of the nerves, their assistance has been confused with the powerful substance residing within them. However, the wisdom of the Creator has [given] us this assistance...

<meta>Catchword: irritable</meta>

<summary>The author argues for a functional distinction between sensory and motor nerves, citing catalepsy as evidence, while critiquing Haller's theory for over-separating muscle irritability from sensation.</summary>
<keywords>nervous system, sensation vs. movement, catalepsy, Albrecht von Haller, irritability, physiology, Enlightenment medicine</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument against Albrecht von Haller’s theory of irritability. The author is attempting to prove that muscles must possess their own unique form of "sensation" (Fühlung) even if we are not consciously aware of it, leading into a new theoretical exploration of the nature of sleep.</meta>

<page-num>514</page-num>
<header>Muscles.</header>

...made irritable, presumably so that the important business of **sensation** <term>Fühlung: an 18th-century term for internal perception or the "sense" of a muscle, distinct from conscious touch</term> within them, protected from all external contact, can circulate all the more undisturbed. The sheaths of the nerves are non-irritable because they are insensitive and have an immobile structure; but the potent substance within them is irritable because it consists of feeling and movement. Regarding the muscles, I believe that each has its own nerves that are not connected with the main nervous system; for we often feel nothing at all of the most important, wisest, and most beneficial contractions of our noblest parts <note>The "noblest parts" likely refers to vital organs like the heart or diaphragm, which move without our conscious will</note>. However, the proper sense of the muscles—to shake off what might be harmful to them or to the structure of their body—proves that they possess a special physical power to distinguish between manifold touches—and this is sensation. The stimulus of the muscles is thus, in my opinion: the effect of its own sensory nerves upon the nerves of its movement.

# Sleep.

## A Hypothesis.

Sleep tends to set in most readily and strongly after fatigue. It seems as if the volatile **nerve-spirit** <note>Nervengeist: a theoretical "vital fluid" or gas that Enlightenment scientists believed filled the nerves to transmit signals and energy</note>, which cheers and strengthens the body, escapes from it and can collect itself again through rest; roughly like the fluid in the capsules of the joints, which is called **synovia** <term>Sinovie: the lubricating fluid found in the joints</term>, which disappears through heavy movement but soon replenishes itself when one rests. The nerve-fluid also seems to me to have much in common with **synovia**.

When a fluid fills a channel and is so thinned within it that it is close to losing its continuity, one can suspect that it will separate from itself in order to unite more precisely into two halves, because the laws of **cohesion** <term>Cohäsion: the physical force that pulls molecules of a substance together</term> in liquid matter seem to favor such a split that strives for a stronger union.

<meta>catchword: Upon</meta>

<page-num>316</page-num>
<header>Sleep.</header>

Based on these previously stated **postulates** <term>Heischesätze: an older German term for premises or requirements used as a starting point for an argument</term>, I now take the liberty of building the following hypothesis:

The spirit-like mass of the **nerve-juice** <term>Nervensaft: in 18th-century physiology, this was the fluid thought to flow through hollow nerves to transmit sensations</term> can be so diminished by physical movements that it no longer fills its channels, from the ends of the sensory organs to the entrance into the soul. It divides into two halves in order to stay together as much as possible: one of these halves attaches itself to the soul, and this separation seems to isolate sensory imagination from the fleeting memory. — A gap is created. The larger this gap is—the more nerve-juice has evaporated through fatiguing movement—the deeper the sleep is, and the longer it must last until what was lost can be replaced. This is also why the first sleep is so firm and the last is so light.

It seems as if the soul, when it is stimulated neither by the senses nor by the imagination (which is related to it), can fall into an inactive rest and, as it were, sleep with the body; for in the deepest sleep, one does not dream at all. Unplagued by sensory perceptions, the soul rests from its work of **comparison**—

<meta>catchword: work</meta>

---

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header>Sleep.</header>

—and remembers nothing, because it has done nothing. 1)

---

1) This way of being could also explain the state of the soul during **fainting spells** <note>original: "Ohnmachten"</note>, in which all memory ceases; and since fainting seems to be caused by a total withdrawal of the nerve-juice, the soul is untouched from without and pressed from within; it is freed from feelings and hindered from moving its **memory-signs** <note>Gedächtnißzeichen: the physical traces or "tokens" in the brain that 18th-century thinkers believed represented memories</note>. Presumably, in this state so close to death, it is too occupied with itself, with its self-feeling, and with the fear of losing itself, to read and compare memory-signs.

There are three unnatural states which interrupt the connection between the body and the soul in just as many different ways: 

*   **Fainting**, in which feeling and movement cease due to the presumptive causes we have just described; 
*   **Catalepsy**, where movement ceases but feeling is present because, as we showed in the previous article, only the power of the motor nerves has withdrawn toward the body; 
*   And finally, **sleepwalking**, where the power of the sensory nerves is divided, as in ordinary sleep, but more strongly, while the motor nerves have retained their full play and are guarded all the more powerfully by the **instinct** that cares for the preservation of the body.

<header># Sleep.</header>

As long as the **nerve-juice** <term>Nervensaft: a theoretical fluid in 18th-century medicine thought to transmit signals through the nerves</term>, through its division, cuts off the senses from the soul from all sides, one dreams little; but as soon as a sensory vibration occurs—caused by an external touch or a movement in the **imagination** <term>Einbildung: the faculty of the mind that produces mental images</term>—which is sufficient to lengthen the outer half of the nerve-juice through a swaying motion until it touches the inner half at even a single point, the soul feels something that it can remember and compare. This marks the beginning of a dream, which the soul can continue, partly through a series of associations and comparisons of **memory-signs** <term>Gedächtnißzeichen: mental markers or symbols stored in the memory</term>, and partly through new vibrations from within that produce contact on the side of the senses.

This explains the dreams that are awakened by an uncomfortable position in bed, by smells, noise, and other physical sensations that affect a sleeper. These dreams are brought to us through the body; they penetrate from the outside in.

However, there are also dreams that are produced from within. Thoughts that have occupied us greatly, or strong impressions, are the most common material for dreaming; but often they keep the soul in a restless motion that tosses the memory-images around. These thoughts can

can then also generate dreams that have no further relation to them, or are completely incoherent.

When we go to bed with impressions, worries, or expectations that are very intense, the nerve-juice remains incessantly agitated; this prevents its division, and one cannot sleep.

In general, the efforts of the soul and mental labor prevent sleep almost as much as physical movements encourage it. It seems as if the nerve-juice intended for sensation is better protected and more expandable than the juice intended for movement, because it performs more noble tasks than moving arms and legs. It evaporates less and expands more finely and cohesively than the latter. Consequently, the soul can feel through the body during sleep more easily than it can move it, which one notices during heavy dreams and especially in that laborious state called **nightmare** <term>Alpdrücken: literally "elf-pressing," a historical term for sleep paralysis or the sensation of a weight on the chest during sleep</term>.

Awakening is either natural or forced. The former happens through the gradual replacement of the nerve-juice, which little by little regains its natural cohesion and restores the complete connection between the soul and the senses. Forced awakening arises either through a noticeable vibra- <note>The word cuts off: Erschütterung (vibration/shock)</note>

# What is Instinct?

We are so eager to deny animals **reason** <term>Vernunft: the cognitive capacity to draw conclusions and reflect on experience</term> in order to keep it for ourselves alone, and in exchange, we give them **instinct**—an exchange in which we would gain little if it were actually the case: for conclusions of reason and experiences often deceive us, but pure instinct never errs; it acts without experience, without reflection, and never fails its benevolent purpose. Therefore, that which we so arrogantly place below our reason might well challenge her for the preference.

Instinct is presumably the **reason of the Creator**, one of the main laws that He has bound to natural forces for the formation and preservation of the physical world, as well as for the restriction and guidance of the moral world.

I define instinct for myself as follows: a wise and artful arrangement of the **drives for union and separation** <note>These "drives" (Triebe) were viewed by 18th-century thinkers as fundamental biological forces of attraction and repulsion.</note> within organized parts for the preservation of the whole: it is, so to speak, an organized feeling, a mechanical construction of the

<summary>This page explains that waking up occurs when the "nerve-juice" is jolted back into a continuous state and begins a philosophical defense of instinct, arguing it is a divine form of reason that is more reliable than human intellect.</summary>
<keywords>physiology of waking, nerve-juice, shock of terror, instinct, reason, animal intelligence, divine law, biological drives</keywords>

forces, and that which for so long has been called the **animal soul**.

Instinct often seems to have **action at a distance** <note>original: "actionem in distans." This refers to the philosophical problem of how objects can affect each other without touching.</note>, because it acts through forces that, as we have seen above, stand in a secret and distant connection with all forces of their kind, and can also be touched from afar by opposing forces through the very extensive **spheres of vapors** <note>The author refers to the "atmospheres" or "effluvia" that 18th-century scientists believed surrounded all living bodies, allowing them to sense things at a distance.</note>.

Instinct is more the property of a **non-thinking** than a thinking being, and one notices it particularly in animal bodies. In these, it seems to be inherent to the muscles and vessels, which accept certain things and repel others with a wonderful accuracy: Their choice always serves for the preservation of that part with which they are most closely connected, and the power of the **will** has no influence on the most important of their effects: The digestive vessels separate the necessary from the superfluous, the useful from the harmful; the **noble parts** <note>historical term for vital organs like the heart, lungs, and brain</note> of our body make convulsive movements to remove what can injure them; and all of this happens without our knowledge or will.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

---

<page-num>323</page-num>
<header># What is Instinct?</header>

The reason for distinguishing so many varieties is uncommonly simple. It rests on the first two laws of forces: on the **drive for union**, assimilation, and sympathy, and on the law of aversion, to avoid the stronger—on the **centrifugal force** <term>Fliehkraft: here used in a biological sense as a repulsive force or "flight force"</term> or **antipathy**; but the complexity, variety, and wisdom at which we marvel lies in the application of these simple laws for the preservation of the whole.

The most sensitive muscles guard the entrance of the most delicate vessels. The diversity of their drives toward assimilation or the rejection of useful or harmful substances determines the post of each one; only those parts pass through which are appropriate to the force prevailing in these muscles, or to the shape and width of the channels, and these parts go exactly where they are needed; just as all those are separated which are **heterogeneous** <note>foreign or incompatible</note> to the entrance of the channels, because they would be so to the place which this entrance protects.

This is only a brief attempt to describe the **mechanism of nutrition** and the preservation of organized bodies, and to show how that which we are accustomed to call instinct is grounded in the simplest laws of forces.

<page-num>324</page-num>
<header># What is Instinct?</header>
<meta>catchword: Adversities</meta>

I turn now from the physical to the **moral** or **passionate instinct**, which is essentially that which we are accustomed to call **sympathy** and **antipathy**.

The great sages of antiquity believed that bodily effluvia <note>original: "Ausflüsse der Körper." In early modern science, these were invisible particles or "vapors" emitted by all objects that were thought to influence other people.</note> caused love and hate.

The King of Achem <note>Likely referring to Aceh (Sumatra), though the author places it in Africa. 18th-century texts often conflated distant locales or relied on legendary travelogues.</note> in Africa chooses his beloved in the following manner: races are held among all the beautiful girls of his country; their shirts are bundled together and sent to the monarch, who smells them; thus, the nose is the path to his heart. There is, after all, some truth in this adventurous custom. The evaporation <note>original: "Ausdünstung," referring to a person's scent or "aura."</note> of an object one loves can be just as pleasant as it is disgusting when it originates from a person one cannot stand; and the sense of smell, by capturing the most volatile parts of the most extensive **sphere of vapors**, can contribute in both a perceptible and imperceptible way to sympathy and antipathy.

Yet, the passionate instinct is not always stirred through the nose, but more often through all the senses; everyone finds similarity or

---

<page-num>325</page-num>
<header># What is Instinct?</header>
<sig>X 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: They</meta>

adversities <note>original: "Widrigkeiten," here meaning repulsions or things that conflict with one's nature.</note> in the realm of its effect, and it incites the forces of his whole body toward union or avoidance.

Up to this point, physical instinct acts correctly and necessarily because it follows the law of union; but when it becomes **moral**—through its mixing and entanglement with influences upon the soul and the soul's counter-reactions—it can err. It can produce both evil and good consequences, and may contradict now physical, now moral laws. This passionate instinct is one of the heaviest weights that the Creator has placed in the scale of our decisions. It is also one of the greatest mysteries if, as I suspect, it is the physical means for the union of material forces and contains the elementary laws of the **procreation** and **reproduction** of all species.

We must take care not to confuse **habit** with **instinct** simply because habit, like the latter, seems to act according to its purpose without reflection. For reflection was present before the habit was formed, and after frequent experience, it was declared unnecessary by a conclusion of the soul. Habit is founded upon this process of the soul.

<page-num>326, 327</page-num>
<header># What is Instinct?</header>

It is a final conclusion for reason, which the latter, after frequent examination of all premises <note>original: "Vordersätze." In logic, these are the starting statements from which a conclusion is drawn.</note>, regards as true once and for all. Habit can increase and expand instinct, but instinct itself is and remains a physical law without experience or reflection; and when we attribute it to the soul, we either mistake habit for it, or the soul assembles instinct-like memories so artfully and swiftly that we believe it has achieved its purpose through an instinct inherent to its nature, without experience or reflection.

The latter occurs during quick and uncommonly correct resolutions in great dangers, when we take the only course of action <note>original: "Parthey," meaning a side, path, or choice.</note> possible for our rescue without reflection, and therefore attribute it to an instinct belonging to our soul. However, were it instinct, it would have to be a law that could never fail; but we very often take the worst course of action for our preservation. Often, a wonderfully expedient resolution is mere chance; and when it is not, it certainly had a reflection at its basis, which its speed or the impression of danger prevented from remaining in the memory.

<meta>catchword: Gives</meta>

<column-break/>

But is there not an entirely **moral instinct**, which one could call good or evil, if it were a natural drive toward virtue or toward evil, without the precondition of rational motives?

Passions that resist one another, or none at all; correct or exaggerated ideas of the laws, and frequent opportunity to do good, can through habit assemble that which we call the **instinct for good**; likewise, violent passions and forgetfulness of the laws can also form through habit that which we are pleased to call the **instinct for evil**.

Thus, habit will be both, if we examine it correctly; — practiced resolutions — in such instances where the soul has once and for all made the resolution no longer to reflect upon what it had previously weighed so often with fear and hope, pleasure or displeasure.

If there lies in man a true moral instinct, it is the drive toward benevolence <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit," meaning charity or the active desire to do good for others.</note> — a precious branch of the tree of love — a drive that encompasses all the drives of union. When it overflows, or is not limited to specific objects, it extends

# Of the Passions.

If one can call the sustained anticipation of bliss — the joyful drive to increase said bliss through union, without the admixture of a physical desire — a passion; then in eternity there was only one — love.

Spirits harmonized among themselves and with their Creator <note>original: "Urheber," literally "author" or "originator."</note>, through the mutual communication of their movements and feelings: union granted them an inexhaustible variety and an infinite doubling of their blissful sensations. The two movements of their elastic substance — expansion and contraction <note>original: "Ausdehnung und Verengerung." These terms reflect an 18th-century view of the soul or "substance" as having quasi-physical properties.</note> — were entirely dedicated to the service of unity and were permeable by the power of the Universal Being: The freedom of their will, which held these two directions in its power, used them only to invent new movements and feelings for themselves and for all; for loving union was the sole purpose of their will, and the manifold creation of unthought feelings was the work of its freedom.

<page-num>330</page-num>
<header>Of the Passions.</header>

Thus, the unity in the spirit world presumably existed before time and space—before beings were restricted within circles and separated from the Universal Being <note>original: "Allwesen." This refers to God or the primordial source of all existence.</note>.

Yet, in the beginning of time, a resistance occurred, which we have shown could not have been caused by anything other than an abuse of the expanding force <note>original: "ausdehnende Kraft." The author uses physical metaphors of expansion and contraction to describe spiritual and psychological states.</note>. Through this arose the second passion,—the opposite of union and communication:— — a discord; — egoism and selfishness.

Lust for power <term>Herrschsucht: an obsessive drive to dominate others</term> and arbitrary power <term>Eigenmacht: the exercise of power based on one's own will rather than law or reason</term> became its rebellious expansion, yet it was itself born through a contraction. Selfishness pulled its strength together to strengthen the resistance by a gathering of itself.

Therefore, we observe that the lust for power and all its passionate sub-types still act expansively within us; whereas self-love, on the contrary, contracts our substance in order to be closer to itself.

Lust for power is always expansive, in order to push back, to resist, to remove, to harm, and to enlarge its boundaries to the detriment of others. Selfishness contracts,

<meta>catchword: in order to</meta>

<page-num>331</page-num>
<header>Of the Passions.</header>

in order to avoid other beings, to touch itself more intimately, and to feel its own I-ness <note>original: "Ichheit." A philosophical term for selfhood or the essence of the ego.</note>.

But when love is expansive, it follows its original drive; it shares itself and seeks out feeling beings to unite with them; if it sometimes contracts its substance, it happens in order to lovingly evade the lust for power, or to enclose the images of beloved objects within itself and unite them with its I-ness. 1)

There are originally only two passions, and these are: selfishness and love. — The former

<meta>catchword: seems</meta>

---

1) One must distinguish the natural, essential disposition <note>original: "Stimmung," meaning the tuning or inner mood of the soul.</note> of the soul toward a certain kind of passion from the exercise of the same.

In the first case, the soul waits with sustained, eager receptivity for the indefinite objects of its passion; in the second, it has found a specific one that makes these active.

The type of passion toward which the soul's inner inclination is primarily decided determines the essentially moral state of its will; however, the exercise of this passion depends on the person's fate; for the objects are not within his power—opportunity leads them toward his wishes.

[Selfishness] seems to be the mother of all evil passions: hatred, envy, pride, anger, and greed are her sons. Love, on the other hand, is almost always good, at least in its intentions: mildness, humility, compassion, benevolence, and gentleness are her daughters. All these affects are sub-types of the two main passions mentioned above and are formed through the mixing of temperaments and social relationships.

Just as the body has a great influence on the soul, so temperaments have a similar influence on the passions; they are, so to speak, the bodies of the passions.

What one calls temperament is nothing other than the specific measure of expansive or compressive forces <note>The author uses a "hydraulic" or mechanical model of the human psyche common in the 18th century, where emotions are seen as physical forces that push outward or pull inward.</note> which has the upper hand in a human body, ²⁾ and we can easily see that a body in which the expansive force <note>original: "auftreibende Kraft"</note> rules must strengthen all the passions of its soul, especially if the lat-

2) The so-called sanguine and choleric temperaments are excesses of the body’s expansive force, which are modified by the soul's two main passions. The melancholic temperament is a predominant compressive force—the phlegmatic, however, is a mere lack of force.

ter is tuned toward expansive passions; whereas a narrowing force in the body tames them and only increases those which likewise have a narrowing effect.

Instinct, however, also has a noticeable influence on our passions. Just as the difference in temperaments makes passions more volatile <note>original: "aufbrausender," literally "effervescent" or "fizzing up."</note> or more introverted through their expansive or narrowing force, so physical instinct strengthens the effects of passionate objects when such things are physically agreeable or adverse to the body.

Instinct heightens passionate objects through attraction or through centrifugal force <note>original: "Fliehkraft."</note>; and temperaments act upon the passionate mood of the soul through their expansive or narrowing movement. A choleric person <term>Choleriker: traditionally someone with an ambitious, energetic, or easily angered temperament</term> is prouder, and a melancholic person <term>Melancholiker: someone prone to deep thought and sadness, but here associated with "sympathy" or participation</term> more sympathetic than a phlegmatic person <term>Phlegmatiker: someone characterized by calm, apathy, or a lack of emotional force</term>.

When our passions continuously move the substance and inner essence of the soul, it is quite natural that they then frequently outweigh the moral concepts of our reason; for that which attacks the whole produces a greater effect than that which only influences a part. Hence the clouding and confusion of ideas which always accompany the passions.

-># Of the Passions.<-

Here an explanation develops regarding the harmful consequences with which unhappy passions often wound our soul. When the hope that feeds a passion ceases; when the object of our desires, which is constantly held up in our imagination, is destroyed; then the forces that were accustomed to and directed toward that object find a void in which they would lose themselves if they did not return. Instead of acting upon their lost goal, they recoil back upon the <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: "Ichheit." This refers to the individual’s core sense of "I," which becomes the target of its own redirected energy.</note> and, through a persistent pressure, weaken the elasticity of their own source. <note>The author uses "elasticity" here as a mechanical metaphor, suggesting the soul is like a spring that loses its bounce or resilience when over-compressed by negative emotions.</note>

Fear and hope are also counted among the passions of man and seem to have a great similarity to them.

Hope expresses itself through an impulse for union; it acts forward and moves the substance of the soul to expand itself. Fear, on the other hand, seems to be a repulsive force; it acts backward and drives our soul, as it were, into a narrow space. Both, however, occupy themselves only with possibilities, and consequently with ideas that have no limits. For this reason, they are not subject to any correct comparison of reason; they outweigh all counter-arguments, and are deceptive, false, and surrounded by obscure concepts. Fear and hope

<column-break/>

<page-num>335</page-num>

-># Of the Passions.<-

seem to be more the companions of the passions than their sisters; but they are inseparable from the representation of everything that we consider to be happiness or unhappiness. In this regard, they could well be counted among the passionate impulses; and I would be inclined, since they deal more with ideal than with real objects, to call them the passions of the imagination.

## Of the Original Source of Morality.

Selfishness and love dwell in all souls, and are the true sources of all good and evil actions.

They alone are the foundations of natural morality and remnants of our original free will; for they are free and independent of all coercion of the laws, resist punishments, and reward themselves.

But in every soul, one has the upper hand over the other, and this preponderance decides whether we are essentially good or evil. If a spirit is less corrupted, the feeling of love prevails within it. — But the more power-hungry it is — the worse it is.

It has been accepted as a known truth that self-love is the only driving force of all our inclinations. If this were correct, we could be called perfectly evil. However, to our good fortune, we still have a second moral driving force, which unfortunately is the smallest, which moves us to do good without self-interest: This is love; it
<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<column-break/>

<header>Of the Original Source of Morality.</header>
<page-num>337</page-num>

is the only antidote to selfishness, and alone can heal the original corruption of our will.

---

->Self-love is self-interest!<-

Only in such regions where there is room for everyone, where no personal boundaries or property exist, could self-love effect something good; <term>e.g.</term> <note>original: "z. E." (zum Exempel)</note> in the realm of so-called truths, in the sciences, and in the zeal to surpass others in virtue, scholarship, and bravery.

But even with such a good and rare direction, self-love never appears without pride and contempt — without hatred or envy.

No matter how well-intended it may be, and for this reason—as the saying goes—seeks its benefit in the general welfare, it will nevertheless always calculate for its own benefit and strive to contribute little to the treasury of society in order to take out much.

Selfishness is the inventor of property, of the social classes <note>The original term "Stände" refers to the historical "estates" or social ranks of the time.</note> and their rights: just as sharing <note>original: "Mittheilung." In this context, it implies the act of communal sharing or imparting oneself to others.</note>, this daughter of love, [is the mother of] the community of goods, the equality of classes, and that which, by the
