<meta>This page continues the argument that self-love, even when aimed at the general welfare, fails to achieve true moral equity. It transitions into defining love as the true foundation of morality, contrasting this with Kantian ethics.</meta>

<page-num>338, 339</page-num>
<header>Of the Original Source of Morality.</header>
<sig>Y 2</sig>

rigor of the law has devised such various forms of equity, without ever being able to introduce them.

In the feeling of love, we must seek the pure <term>principle</term> <note>original: "Principium"</note> of morality—within it, we can find that which Professor Kant, in his *Critique of Practical Reason*, calls the pure, imperative motive for the law: the practical ground of the freedom of the will.

The **drive for union** is the first law of all things, and from it flows a second for all of nature: the **drive for identification**, <note>original: "Trieb zur Verähnlichung," literally the "drive to become similar" or the impulse toward resemblance.</note> which endeavors to facilitate union through this means. Both drives are the innermost motives of love and of pure morality.

The highest and most universal moral maxim, which can serve as a principle for general legislation and simultaneously indicates its motive, is the following simple law: "What you do not want people to do to you, that you shall also not do to them." This is engraved in all hearts. But by what means? Through the **drive for identification** that lies within our nature. I rejoice when I do someone good because I involuntarily

<column-break/>

place myself in their position—I feel as though I have done good to myself; and when I see someone suffer, I suffer because I involuntarily put myself in their place. If I, however, am the author of their suffering, I believe I have hurt myself by identifying with them—if I kill them, I commit a kind of suicide.

Only the preponderance of a violent passion can move us to perform an action that is entirely contrary to the love of humanity. In the moment of the deed, the drive for identification is admittedly suppressed; however, the stronger it was, the more powerfully it awakens in memory once the satisfied passion has passed. It constantly shows us the image of the sufferer and always puts us in their place. It incessantly compels us to repeat the unfortunate deed in our memory and, by virtue of its illusion, to commit it upon ourselves. This **self-torment** is what constitutes the essence of a <term>guilty conscience</term>. <note>original: "böses Gewissen," literally "evil conscience."</note> The greater this torment is, the greater is the moral goodness of the person; for this torment proves the strength of their drive. But the more their conscience remains silent, the more their moral foundation is corrupted.

<summary>This page argues that morality is rooted in the "drive for identification"—an innate impulse to empathize by placing oneself in another's position—rather than just abstract duty. It explains a "guilty conscience" as the self-inflicted psychological pain of reliving the suffering one has caused others.</summary>

<page-num>340</page-num>
->### Of the Original Source of Morality.<-

From all of this, we see that the **drive for identification** <note>original: "Trieb zur Verähnlichung," the natural impulse to make oneself similar to others or to see oneself in them.</note> is the true source of conscience, love, and human goodness.

These moral forms, which lie within all souls, are admittedly everywhere flawed, dull, or even entirely broken; nevertheless, they can still be conceived of as perfect. The highest ideal degree of moral goodness, combined with perfect wisdom, would always act in such a way that the **maxim** <term>maxim: a subjective principle of action or a personal rule of conduct.</term> of its will—as **Kant** expresses it—could serve as a principle for **universal legislation**. <note>This refers to Immanuel Kant’s Categorical Imperative, the idea that one should only act according to rules that could be turned into universal laws for everyone.</note> It would possess all those rare and difficult-to-grasp qualities of the practical ground of **Kantian morality**: it would act freely, without regard for divine or human laws, without regard for punishments or rewards, without reasoned self-love, and even without an object; for the powerful drive for identification that animates it knows no law other than its own, punishes and rewards itself, has no other object than itself, and acts in the moral realm just as powerfully, just as necessarily, and with just as much attraction as **gravity** does in the physical realm; for both seek to fulfill the law of union.

**Unity** is the essence of love—its fractures are mutilations: the more universally it spreads itself, the more overflowingly it pours out upon all of nature—the better it is. It can only fail in the choice of its objects, in an exclusive narrowing, and in the means it employs.

## Of Physical Love.

Physical love contains within itself particular contradictions that are remarkable enough to be considered. As necessary as it is for the preservation of the human race, just as ruinous it seems to be for its individual members. It frequently harms the body it governs; since it gives up a part of its strength, it contributes something to the destruction of its form. One should recall what I have said above regarding both stimulus and friction; consider how preeminently both are connected, in the truest sense, with physical love, and one will easily be convinced that not only nourishing but also essential parts of the body depart through a literal expansion of its circle <note>This refers to the "animal economy" or the circulation of vital fluids, a common 18th-century medical belief that the body had a finite amount of "vital spirits."</note> when they appear as **seed**. <term>seed: original "Saame," referring to semen but also to the concentrated "vital spark" believed to be a distilled essence of the body's strength.</term>

**Kabbalists**, **magicians**, and **mystics** have dreamed that the highest perfection of the spirit and the indestructibility of the body could be achieved through perfect chastity, and the final remnants of this opinion are monks and nuns. Just as well as one can prove,

<meta>catchword: can,</meta>

---

<page-num>343</page-num>
->### Of Physical Love.<-

that involuntary chastity in a voluptuous temperament shatters the health, just as well can one imagine that a body would certainly last much longer and remain healthier if it could remain unweakened and chaste—without the disorders that the formed **seed**, by accumulating excessively, is accustomed to cause. This would occur if a lustful imagination never gathered the forces through an internal stimulus, developed them from their subtle bonds, <note>In the vitalist biology of the time, "subtle bonds" referred to the way life-forces were bound within the body's fluids.</note> enveloped them in viscous juices, and in this manner prepared the germinating and fermenting **seed**. However, this spiritual chastity is impossible, and without it, physical chastity is fruitless and harmful.

Yet it is always remarkable that the reproduction of the animal <note>The author uses "animal" here in the sense of the biological human being.</note> is precisely linked with its destruction; and even more remarkable that the moral organization and limitation of love in human society will remain an insoluble problem for all actual and **Platonic legislators**; <note>Refers to idealist political thinkers who attempt to design perfect societies, following the model of Plato’s *Republic*.</note> for communal wives, seraglios, concubines, mistresses, and separable or inseparable marriages each, in their own way, present insurmountable concerns and harmful consequences. ## Seed-Force.

We have shown above under the title "Creation out of Nothing" that the sacredly obscure expression "He created!" can likely have no other meaning than "He separated." <note>original: "sonderte ab." The author is arguing against creation from literal nothingness, suggesting instead that God "separated" parts of His own essence to form the universe.</note> Procreation and reproduction are also a form of separation, and seem to me to be a kind of creation.

The first beings whom God separated from His substance were presumably those called spirits. They are the children of the Universal Being <term>Allwesen: a philosophical term for God as the totality of all existence</term>; offspring from Him, His instruments, and the first external objects of His thoughts. Perhaps they were the means He first required to multiply His sensations and to develop the infinite diversity of His unity.

If these spirits were to be instruments of their Author, then it is believable that they also could have received the power to continue a kind of creation and to increase the diversity of the unity. But at the same time, it is probable that between His creation and theirs, a...

One need not worry that this simple, elastic substance, which seeds everything, might finally be weakened and destroyed through such manifold division and separation; for it is a part of the substance of the Universal Being, which combines infinite divisibility with the power to expand immeasurably; the smallest particle of it would be sufficient

# The Ladder of the Drive
## for Union.

->**From gravity to love, everything is union.**<-

Gravity is the lowest degree on the ladder of this universal need of divided unity. Gravity is a law of union for coarse matter, and it occurs between half-forces <note>original: "Halbkräften"; the author suggests that forces in nature are incomplete or "halved" and naturally seek their other half to find balance.</note> of the same kind, which can feel each other, but not themselves.

Attractive force or sympathy is the second degree of the aforementioned ladder. It arises between organized beings that have no consciousness, and half-forces that are useful to them. Preservation or reproduction is its purpose, and instinct is the feeling that directs its course. This attractive force is chemical affinity in the mineral kingdom; in the vegetable and animal kingdoms, however, it is an aspiring union of plants and muscles with forces that can heal or nourish them; however, its noblest busi-

<meta>catchword: busi-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>349</page-num>
<header>The Ladder of the Drive for Union. 349</header>

-ness <note>The word "geschäfte" (business/activity) continues from the previous page's catchword "Ges".</note> in the plant kingdom consists in pollination <term>Besaamung: literally "seeding"; the reproductive process by which plants ensure their continuation</term>.

Appetite, stimulus, curiosity, and the love of arts and sciences, is the third degree of our great ladder. It exists between the senses or the imagination of thinking beings and their various objects; it unites the body or the soul with objects that give them pleasant sensations. Pleasure is its purpose; self-awareness <term>Selbstgefühl: a term used here to describe an internal sense of one's own existence and state</term> is its source and guide.

The fourth degree is sensual love. It occurs between animal bodies, has the most pleasant of all sensory feelings as its basis, and is grounded in the powerful law of reproduction. Sensual love is the drive for union of one feeling with another—a deceptive feeling of such a union; but it is an actual gathering of forces from all parts of the body to mix with a similar collection from another body.

Passionate love is the fifth degree of the drive for union, and it only occurs between thinking beings; it has sensual love as a secondary purpose and strives for the union of souls. It seeks a...

<summary>This page outlines a philosophical hierarchy or "ladder" of cosmic forces, arguing that all natural phenomena—from gravity and chemical affinity to biological reproduction and human love—are different expressions of a single universal drive for "union."</summary>

...a doubling of our selfhood <note>original: "Ichheit"; a philosophical term referring to the essence of the "I" or the individual's sense of self.</note>—but it receives only a stronger sensation of sensual love and a sweeter delusion, through the enjoyment of a certain harmonic correspondence, which it takes for a real union.

If thinking beings were free of bodies and could unite unhindered, then their senses of self would feel one another just as they feel themselves. This highest degree of union is that for which love strives, but which it never reaches, which is why it can never be satisfied.

Could we, or were we permitted to ever attain such a union, we would nonetheless not enjoy the most perfect degree of contentment thereby; we would remain constantly in danger of a separation through mutual desire for variety, and the possible connection to a higher and more powerful being would always promise us an even stronger enjoyment.

The supreme degree of bliss <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to a state of perfect, divine happiness or beatitude.</note> would thus probably be the union with the Universal Being <note>original: "Allwesen"; the author uses this to refer to a pantheistic or supreme divine totality.</note>, in which we could connect ourselves not only with the self-feeling of all spirits, but perhaps even with His own. Supplemented by all our ev-

Ever-present enjoyment and unceasing feeling of love is the conception I have of the eternal union which awaits us.

<page-num>352</page-num>

## Conclusion.

Our soul always seems to be seeking something. — The riddle of itself, its origin and its destiny occupies it, more or less, so urgently and so in vain, that one does not know whether it is right to occupy oneself with it, or wrong to think of anything else. It searches the known circle of our sciences and finds nothing; it pursues the seductive phantom-images of the marvelous <note>original: "Wunderbaren"; in this context, it refers to the supernatural, the occult, or mystical phenomena that fell outside the rational sciences of the Enlightenment.</note>, and also finds nothing. Yet it cannot resolve to desist and say: it is in vain! I want to rest — — the great peace of the grave; wait and hope!

If we had a single truth: No one would doubt it!

Yet we can diminish our errors, unmask disguised untruths, and do as many an author does who improves his work by crossing things out. The opposite of every school-truth <note>original: "Schulwahrheit"; refers to the rigid, dogmatic truths taught in traditional academic or ecclesiastical institutions.</note> can sometimes lead us to something true — — at least to something new.

---

<page-num>353</page-num>
<header>Conclusion. 353</header>
<sig>3</sig>
<meta>catchword: say</meta>

This has long been the path of my intellect, and the eradication of school-belief <note>original: "Schulglaubens"; uncritical acceptance of established institutional or academic dogma.</note> is the main purpose of this small work. I too, as a seeker, have run through the circle of the sciences; I too was long imprisoned in the labyrinth of the marvelous. —

I leave behind for those who, like me, have found no solutions in either, that which I have discovered through frequent negation of generally accepted propositions, what I have excavated piece by piece and covered in sludge from many a secret source, adapted to all known and unknown systems, and carried around in my head for thirty years.

I ask my independent-thinking readers to read these fragments in their entirety with indulgence, patience, and some effort, to test them without prejudice, and to hew <note>The author uses a stonemason's metaphor here: "zuzuhauen."</note> these rough boundary stones <note>original: "Gränzsteine"; markers used to define the limits of a property or territory.</note> of my intellect and set them further.

But to those whom preconceived opinion, fear of unfamiliar ideas, habitual authoritative pronouncements, laziness or weakness prevent from judging these metaphysical attempts <note>original: "metaphysische Versuche"</note> impartially, I leave them to do with them what they will, with the assurance: that since I have said everything I know, I will say nothing more until I have something new to

## Conclusion.

I find myself saying. But so that they might not take my future silence for contempt, I want to help them in advance to accuse and condemn me according to the rules of criticism:

My style is bad, trivial, and, due to the frequent repetition of the same words, unpleasant to the ear; for I had never written in my mother tongue, and have primarily endeavored only to be clear in such difficult investigations. I appear to be a <term>Pantheist</term> <note>A person who believes that the universe is identical with divinity; here, the author clarifies he believes everything is created by God out of Himself, rather than everything simply being God.</note>, yet not because I make everything into God, but because I let God make everything out of Himself — or I am even a <term>Spinozist</term> <note>A follower of Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), whose philosophy was often equated with atheism or fatalism in the 18th century because he identified God with the substance of the universe.</note>, although I do not, like Spinoza, hold God to be the soul of the world and the world to be His body. — At the very least, I seem to be a not entirely good Christian, because I do not believe in eternal punishments in hell, and I suspect that God can make everything good again which He did not make evil <note>The author is expressing a belief in "Universal Reconciliation" (Apocatastasis), the idea that all souls will eventually be saved, which was a controversial and often "heretical" view at the time.</note>. Furthermore, I am a poor chemist and an unlearned physicist; partly because I truly am both, and partly because I do not hold everything to be eternally true that is in fashion in this century and is carried about as truth — finally, I am a miserable metaphysician, because I cannot think my way into the <term>Monaden</term> <note>Monads; in the philosophy of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, these are the fundamental, indivisible "simple substances" that make up the universe and are said to have no spatial extension.</note>.

> “That God could neither think of evil first, nor foresee the thought which the creative abuse of the unfathomably free will had to have for itself alone;”
> “That He did not want to fathom what He had determined to be unfathomable; and that He relied on His omnipotence to be able to make everything better;”
> “That God would not be omnipotent if He could not make everything better, and not all-good if He did not want to make everything better—so that I would rather doubt His omniscience than His power and goodness;”

# Creation through Numbers and Words.

## Something about Magic, Kabbalah, and Secret Societies.

God said: "Let there be light!" and there was light; this remains forever the most perfect model of a sublime expression, and no poet can devise a more beautiful image of a creating deity than the representation of a commanding master whose voice contains within itself everything that his will demands. Plato painted this image artistically by representing it as a <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: *Dreyfaltigkeit*. In this Neoplatonic context, the author refers to a triad of thought, word, and deed/work rather than the later Christian theological Trinity.</note> consisting of thought, word, and work; but the <term>Yetzirah</term> <note>original: *Jezirah*. Referring to the *Sefer Yetzirah* or "Book of Formation," a primary text of Jewish mysticism.</note> 1) seems to make the matter even more tangible.

Kabbalah is an ancient Jewish tradition of mystical biblical interpretation.</note> science, and they are permitted to read it only at a mature age.

Even though it has become unintelligible to the point of nonsense through the errors of copyists and the "improvements" of scholars, through additions and omissions; yet, after countless readings, I have perceived in it the seed of everything that one might call <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>original: *Cabala*</note> and Magic.

It begins as follows:
> "Through 32 paths or means God created his world; (these were) the 10 numbers and the 22 letters. —"

These 10 numbers are called <term>Sephiroth</term> <note>original: *Sephiroth*. In Hebrew, this term is related to the word for counting or sapphire.</note> in the original text, and the Jews understand them as 10 special names of God, the use of which is said to constitute the most distinguished and secret branch of the Kabbalah; but I believe I can assert that they mean nothing more than the 10 numbers, for *Sephiroth* literally means numbers or digits. *Yetzirah* says of these Sephiroth: they are 10 ways to count things; there are 10 and not 9; — 10 and not 11; one should note this well—one can correct, experience, or conjecture everything through them, their pursuit is without limit and without end etc. etc.

# Creation through Numbers and Words.

Here opens a wide and rich field of ingenious conjectures and poetic relationships: Just as water, it was thought, seems to contain original seeds of plants and vermin, so this divine water could have contained the seeds of all things within itself; — Just as all words and languages are formed from breath through 22 letters, so all worlds and forms could have been made from this primal matter through 22 means, similar to letters, or these means could have been letters themselves and composed a creative language, which [combines] matter and

he dedicates the **Shin** <note>The Hebrew letter *Shin*.</note> to fire, because this hissing sound disperses the breath.

I remember having read in a <term>Rosicrucian</term> <note>original: *rosenkreuzerischen*. The Rosicrucians were a secret philosophical society of the 17th and 18th centuries focused on alchemy and mysticism.</note> writing the following ingenious representation of a watery creation: God spoke, and the resistance acted upon his breath, much like a cold wall on warm breath—it became water!

Once one has water, everything can afterwards be developed from it. <term>Thales</term> <note>Thales of Miletus (c. 624 – 548 BCE), a pre-Socratic Greek philosopher often cited as the first to suggest water as the primary substance of the universe.</note> was already of the opinion that everything arose from water, and our newer microscopic experiences regarding <term>infusoria</term> <note>original: *Infusionsthiere*. Microscopic organisms found in organic infusions; at the time, they were seen as evidence of life arising from water.</note> and plants can add a great probability to this system, which was embellished by <term>Maillet</term> <note>Benoît de Maillet (1656–1738), whose book *Telliamed* proposed that the Earth was once covered by ocean and that all land animals, including humans, evolved from sea creatures.</note>.

3) Everything is made through numbers, measure, and weight <note>A reference to the biblical Wisdom of Solomon 11:20: "But thou hast ordered all things in measure and number and weight."</note>, and the Platonic proportions between music and the world system <note>This refers to the "Musica Universalis" or Music of the Spheres, the ancient idea that the movement of celestial bodies creates a form of music based on mathematical ratios.</note> have been admired by the greatest minds of his time, presumably because they understood Plato better than we do.

4) Of everything I have ever experienced in the secret sciences, nothing has seemed more striking to me and nothing has remained more inexplicable than the Kabbalah <note>original: *Cabala*</note> of Father Tertius, known in Regensburg, of which Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811) was a famous writer of the German Enlightenment who documented his travels through Germany and Switzerland.</note> spoke in his travel descriptions. # Creation through Numbers and Words.

In this way, the first kind of Kabbalah arose, which treats letters as numbers,

---

vowels of the question are transformed into numbers, and from this series, through ever-decreasing additions, an inverted triangle is formed. From this triangle—by means of an extremely difficult calculation in which one must keep a multitude of exception rules in mind—a second series of numbers is drawn, which, when transformed back into letters, provides an answer. This answer is always fitting, and even if it does not come true, its exact correspondence with the question, and the peculiar circumstance that it emerges in verse, remains utterly marvelous.

I had already encountered this Kabbalah in Italy <note>original: *Welschland*</note> and in two German cities, though without being able to test it; but a friend whom I can trust as myself learned it from the Capuchin monk, merely to be able to judge its truth. From this friend, I know reliably that it is practiced without deception and according to rules whose infinite complexity is unfathomable. Of course, the calculator must know, if not the exact words, then the subject of the question; he must understand the meter of the Latin verses; he often rejects seven or eight letters—but according to specific rules—before hitting upon the right one. The flow of the verse and the meaning guide him as well; yet the choice, at least of the first letter, is entirely random, as it depends on the unthinking, arbitrary selection...

# Creation through Numbers and Words.

—a number is determined, and all subsequent letters are forced according to rules.

As for the prophetic value of these answers, I must confess that, despite their oracle-like ambiguity, very few have proven true.

This **Kabbalah** <note>original: *Cabala*</note> answered all questions regarding secret sciences in an incomprehensibly riddling way; however, it answered theological questions as if a **Capuchin** <note>A member of the Capuchin Franciscan Order of friars, known for traditionalist and sometimes simple, pious preaching.</note> had spoken through them. Yet, I owe it to the truth to confess that as often as we consulted it in the medical field, it guessed—to our greatest astonishment—not only the illnesses of persons whom the calculator certainly could not have known, but also proposed appropriate and often unknown remedies with the best success.

I know of another **Kabbalah**, which applies to all languages; and although it often answers very untruly, it is still more strange than that of the **Capuchin**, for it has far less arbitrariness in the selection of letters, and yet answers just as appropriately regarding the meaning.

Should there be a secret relationship between letters and numbers in a universal fundamental language, something of which exists in all languages, and which could at the very least effect the appropriate connection of the answer to the question? Could these types

5) The Orientals and Greeks had the common superstition of summing up the numbers contained in the names of various opponents, comparing these sums with one another, and concluding from this which one would overcome the others. <note>This practice is known as *isopsephy*.</note>

...where a letter of a word is taken as the starting letter of another word; the **Merkavah** <note>original: *Marcabah*; Hebrew for "Chariot," referring to the mystical study of Ezekiel's vision of the divine throne-chariot.</note> and **Bereshit** <note>original: *Bereschid*; Hebrew for "In the beginning," the first word of the Torah, referring here to the mystical study of the Creation.</note>, the first of which explains the vision of **Ezekiel**, the other specifically explains the first book of **Moses** <note>The Book of Genesis.</note>. Their invention belongs to the clever category of **acrostics** <note>puzzles where the first letters of lines form words</note> and **anagrams**. The true **Kabbalah** transforms letters into numbers and assigns an inner meaning to the latter. It is, so to speak, the grammar of **magic**, and relates to it as numbers do to letters.

Since I may say to my credit that I have never rejected a matter for the proud reason that I could not comprehend it, I am also not ashamed to confess that I have sacrificed much time and effort to the hope of learning, through extraordinary means, that which would be especially worth knowing.

The urge toward the marvelous resembles the youthful desire of a sailor going out on discoveries. For him, the most improbable hope outweighs the certainty of all dangers. **Vespucci** <note>Amerigo Vespucci, the Italian explorer after whom America is named.</note> and **Columbus** had no more

I too have followed such deceivers and have paid the price to warn against them. I thus venture to express my own opinions on a subject that is viewed by almost everyone with exaggerated contempt or admiration.

Accordingly, electricity in ignorant times and deceitful hands might have become witchcraft; conversely, sympathy and perhaps exorcism <term>Geisterbannung: the practice of summoning or banishing spirits</term> would become school sciences as soon as one could teach the means to effect them.

If a magic ever existed, it was the separation of evil from God; at least the idea of the magic circle, whether as tradition or by chance, seems to harmonize with the only possibility of a separation from infinite space. 6) In the seeds of the animal and plant kingdoms, the most distinguished forces seem to lie accumulated: Through the mixing and displacement of these germs, we can disturb the laws of nature and effect lasting changes in the species of animals and plants. miracles have been pitted against miracles, and professional jealousy <note>original: "Brodneid"—literally "bread-envy," referring to the competitive rivalry between practitioners for their livelihood</note> may well have often driven the distinction between good and evil magic too far.

The Christian religion, and especially the Roman Catholic one, is undeniably built upon a magical foundation more than any other. The mystery of the Incarnation, the miracles of Christ and His disciples, and the sacraments are the containment of the most noble heavenly powers within earthly forms. What <term>Clement of Alexandria</term> and <term>Origen</term> <note>influential early Christian theologians who sought to bridge the gap between Greek philosophy and Christian faith</note> said regarding the secret teachings of the earliest Christians proves to me—who possesses concepts of magic that are quite compatible with religion—that the Church in its beginnings practiced many magical customs, especially since I see the remnants of them in the Roman Catholic Church. The ordination of priests, and particularly of bishops; the preparation of <term>Chrism</term> <note>original: "Chryſma"—consecrated oil used in religious rites</note>; the consecration of churches, during which the 22 letters of the alphabet are drawn with the bishop's staff upon a floor strewn with ash; holy water; exorcism; and especially the Mass, the origin of which I have never been able to discover—all of these are ceremonies that bear the characteristics of a magic no longer practiced by magicians.

<column-break/>

The <term>Gnostics</term> <note>early Christian mystical sects that emphasized "gnosis" or secret spiritual knowledge</note> seem to me to have primarily developed this secret doctrine and finally, in the third century, to have mixed and corrupted it with Indian and Egyptian mysteries. We know the Gnostics only through their enemies, who branded them as heretics, and through the wretched talismans of their last and worst sect, the <term>Basilidians</term> <note>followers of Basilides, known for their complex cosmology and use of amulets like the Abraxas stone</note>. But I cannot imagine that people who constituted the most learned part of the Christians should truly have held as many contradictory opinions as <term>Augustine</term> and other Church Fathers impose upon them. It is to be wished that we could find some of their writings, which, however, were all eradicated with great diligence.

After the arts and sciences fell into decay during the Middle Ages, magic also degenerated into the grossest superstition.

Nevertheless, in our enlightened century—as stupid and loathsome as it was—it has come to the fore once again. Secret societies have given it a new form, adorned it, and, after a long wait, presented it in the dark to the young, well-nourished imagination of heated <term>catechumens</term> <note>original: "Catechumenen"—traditionally those being prepared for baptism, but here referring to new initiates into secret societies</note>.

# 380 Creation through Numbers and Words.

My experience and my conscience cannot warn enough against these seductive schools.

As long as Freemasonry <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> deceived the expectation of unfulfilled promises with social pleasures; as long as it earned the protection of rulers and the respect of observers through charitable deeds and quiet simplicity: then it was still worth the effort to invest in this lottery, to investigate its unknown origin, and to marvel at the strange hieroglyphs <note>the symbols and emblems used on Masonic tracing boards</note> of the first three degrees as monuments of another world.

But since Masonry has become a worn-out cloak for all kinds of fraud—since hidden superiors <note>original: "verborgene Obere"—a reference to the "Unknown Superiors" often cited in high-degree Masonic systems like the Strict Observance</note> have dared to unconditionally subject the will of their pupils to unknown laws through punishable vows—since most states, for political reasons, can no longer tolerate secret associations and gatherings—since a certain society <note>likely a reference to the Illuminati, which was suppressed in Bavaria shortly before this text's likely era</note>, which wanted to make the human race happy in its own particular way, has made its members quite astonishingly unhappy, and has entangled a multitude of innocent victims of slander in the persecution

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

of this institution—now that I foresee that young people, by entering secret associations, expose themselves to the danger of losing not only time and money, but also their civil happiness <note>one's legal standing, reputation, and career prospects within the state</note>; now I believe it is my duty to assure upon my honor that the matter does not deserve to have one's peace and welfare staked upon it. 8)

8) The only fraternity that perhaps worked effectively was the Lodge of the United Friends <note>original: "les Amis réunis"</note> in Paris, of which various brochures have spoken with the most ridiculous ignorance. It preserved and collected the most complete history of Masonry, kept a faithful register of all the follies, fictions, and charlatanries that pertain to it; and in its archives, one found everything that money, effort, and the most extensive connections could discover for the honor of truth. The results of these discoveries were a few hundred false degrees, the biographies of a couple dozen impostors, and the certainty that all known systems have as yet found nothing.

# 382 Creation through Numbers and Words.

Beloved seeker, only consider the following very simple observation:

Is magic a fiction or an impossibility? Then one is being deceived. But is it true? How dangerous might it then be?

Only those who accept religious traditions can believe in spirits without being irrational; but what terrifying warnings and prohibitions does one not find in these traditions alongside the traces of a possibility of coming into communion with spirits? — —

— and if the Fall of the Spirits <note>original: "Geisterfall" — refers to the theological concept of the rebellion and descent of angels</note> consisted in the invention of evil magic, and the human fall in the misuse of good magic (for idle pride and a curious bite of an apple truly could not have been the cause), what kind of purification would even the weakest attempt to repeat such crimes bring upon itself? I am convinced that a parricide <note>one who murders their own father</note> might get off more lightly, for he would only have laid hands on a human being, whereas the former would have sought to lay hands on the Creator. But fortunately, I consider the repetition of these crimes to be impossible. Since God, according to my principles, has foreseen and fortified everything in the material world, I cannot believe that He ever

<meta>catchword: ever</meta>

ever allowed the unchangeable laws of nature, which bind everything, to be disrupted; and in this sense, I said in the *Metaphysical Heresies* in the article <term>Providence</term> <note>the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power</note>: “that the essence of things (the simple undivided <term>Substance</term> <note>the fundamental 'stuff' or essential nature of reality</note>), is kept safe in forms that protect it from all abuse.”

Almost everything is magic! We too are magicians in many respects, especially in procreation <note>original: "Kinderzeugen"</note>. But an irregular desire drives us to want to make that which we neither can nor should make — we wish to practice that magic which is entrusted to nature. — Would we not cause great disorder if we could? 9)

# 385 Musical Art, Melody
### and
# Musical Expression.

Musical art consists of three essential main parts; these are: harmony, melody, and rhythm <note>original: "Zeitmaaß" — literally "time-measure," referring to tempo and meter</note>.

Musical harmony is a science of the division of tone, of the essentially coherent connections of its parts with one another, and of the joining together of several distinct combinations of these parts.

The division of tone encompasses not only the known and defined fractions of the <term>octave</term> <note>the interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency</note>, but also all others that are possible on an infinitely divisible scale.

The essential connections of these fractions among themselves determine their relat-

<sig>Bb</sig>
<meta>catchword: ionship</meta>

<summary>The text transitions from a moral critique of magic as a 'theft' from God to a formal treatise on music theory, defining harmony as a mathematical science of dividing and connecting tones.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, Prometheus, music theory, harmony, melody, rhythm, octave, tone division</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of music theory from page 385. The previous page established harmony as a mathematical science of dividing tones. This page transitions into the more elusive nature of melody, distinguishing it from the rigid rules of harmonic science.</meta>

# 386 Musical Art, Melody
proportional unions, which are called **chords**, and the pleasant-sounding transitions from one tone to another.

The combinations of several distinct chords among themselves provide the rules of **counterpoint** <note>the technique of combining different melodic lines</note>, of **figured bass** <note>a system of musical shorthand used to indicate chords above a bass note</note>, and the arrangement of various voices and instruments.

Melody is more difficult to define than harmony. All tones of the former are harmonic, connected and ordered by harmony—everything it contains seems to belong to the latter, and yet melody is something entirely different from harmony, because harmony without melody becomes dry and tiring. However little the essence of melody may be tangible, it is nonetheless the most felt and precious part of the musical art. One cannot call it a science, for it has no fundamental principles; it cannot be taught to anyone; it is an art innate to us. Within it lies a heavenly mystery, into which our ear, but not our reasoning power, is initiated. There are no rules for inventing a song, and no reasons why one pleases us more than another. It can be beautiful without expressing anything; it is a physical truth, a physical pleasure—the only one that charms without a specific need and is enjoyed without exhaustion;
<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<column-break/>

# 387 and Musical Expression.
for, if anything in the musical art tires, it is harmony.

Melody is the freest of all arts, the inventor of her own thoughts and the creator of unconsidered beauties.

She gives the harmonic material a pleasing form, just as the outline of a body represents the beautiful proportions of its <term>dimensions</term> <gloss>me</gloss> <note>original: "Durchmesser"—literally "diameters" or "measurements." The gloss "me" refers to the hyphenated syllable in the original German.</note>. But she has no model for this, unlike the visual arts; her line of beauty is even more indefinite than those which the latter sometimes capture, and she is guided within it merely by chance or by inspiration.

As difficult as it seems to penetrate the nature of melody, I nonetheless flatter myself to have discovered something essential about it. It has a single and constant path, which always moves back in a circular fashion. **Return** seems to me to constitute the essence of melodies; for it is the return into the abandoned chord from foreign tones that had been introduced—the return to the most prominent thought, the repetition of which the ear desires 1)—and finally the return to the <term>root note</term> <note>original: "Grundton"—the fundamental note or tonic of a scale.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of music theory from page 387. # 388 Musical Art, Melody
[tone], forming the whole melody and its beauties.

I define melody, therefore, as follows: it is an artful application of harmony, returning to the tonic <note>original: *Grundton*; the home key or root note of a musical piece</note> that has been left behind, by traveling through many other tones; for the more chords one wanders through, and the longer the resolution of their complexities is delayed by newly introduced tones, the more beautiful the melody is. Furthermore, the more distant the transition from one key to another is—namely, the more chords are omitted through which one should have passed to arrive at this new key—the more artful the melody becomes.

The pleasure that melody awakens in us is infinitely more charming than the rest <note>or "repose"</note> that harmony provides to our ear; but the true causes of this enchanting pleasure are not so easy to fathom.

---

...the motif is introduced, then multiplied by the various instruments that repeat it.

The formula of the aria, whose second part usually returns to the first, and even more so the **rondeau** <note>a musical form with a recurring leading theme</note>, are built entirely upon this return, and prove its pleasing effect.

<column-break/>

# 389 and Musical Expression.
Bb 3
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

The highest and most essential qualities of beauty are: **unity** and **variety**. True musical art possesses and follows these preeminently. It has only one tonic, which gives it all its varieties; only one main theme, which it applies in various ways; and when it finds different voices and many kinds of instruments, it shows its greatest skill in uniting them. 

But one of the most mysterious essences is the tone itself, and in it we must seek the cause of the pleasure that the ear connects with the *return*. Every tonic is a unity in itself; when it is divided by harmony, it demands—like everything that is divided—to return to unity. This happens through the return to the tonic. The regular feeling for harmonic proportions, which is inherent to the human ear, expects this reunification, senses its necessity, and knows how to appreciate its correctness. The expectation of the return is a feeling of truth <note>original: *Wahrheitsgefühl*</note> that melody grants us. Through it, we feel truth and essence, and such feelings are precious and rare.

The return to the tonic is, as it were, a logical conclusion drawn from many harmonic relationships. Just as the conclusion of a **syllogism** <note>a logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two previous premises, e.g., "All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal"</note> based on...

# 390 Musical Art, Melody

...returns to the main concept of the first premise, so melodic return repeats the primary tone of the first harmonic relationship. It is a logical conclusion that communicates itself to the feeling—the truest thing imaginable, and consequently the most desirable.

**Charm** <note>original: *Reiz*; in this context, it refers to a sensory stimulus or "tickle" that engages the nerves</note> can also contribute much to the pleasure afforded us by the art of music and especially melody. I have shown that it is a repeated pressure that strengthens one’s vital force <note>original: *Schnellkraft*; literally "quick-power," referring to the elasticity or resilience of the spirit and nerves</note>. The increase or greater sensing of our powers gives us pleasure; thus, the frequent pressure of the sustained beat, the return, and all musical repetitions can cause a pleasure specific to this charm. Even the **trill**, which makes two successive notes that do not form a chord agreeable to the ear, seems to work this little magic through its hammering pressure upon our sensitivity <note>original: *Irritabilität*; an 18th-century physiological term for the body's capacity to respond to stimuli</note>.

**Fear** and **hope** also mix their effects into the play of complications and harmonic obstacles which the melody creates for itself in order to return through more diverse tones. The greater the distance from the tonic <term>tonic: the "home" note or root key of a musical piece, providing a sense of rest</term>, the more our innate feeling for harmonic unity fears losing the goal of its expectation; yet the greater...

<meta>catchword: our</meta>

---

# 391 and Musical Expression.

...our fear was, the more pleasant the surprise when we see the lost tonic once again in an approaching resolution.

**Time** stands in a special connection with music, whose recurring repetitions are based entirely on the great points in time: the **past**, the **present**, and the **future**; for the course of a melody is so constituted that one never entirely loses the memory of the tonic, and one always expects the return of its presence.

This musical duration has its own measure of time <note>original: *Zeitmaas*</note>, which expresses itself in three ways:

1) Through the degree of speed with which the whole is driven, and this is what is called **tempo**. It indicates the emotion <note>original: *Affect*; the specific mood or "passion," such as sadness or joy, that the piece is intended to evoke</note> in which the piece should be played;

2) through the division of the whole into certain intervals of time, and this is the **measure** <note>original: *Takt*; the beat or meter</note>; and

3) through a division of these time intervals into smaller parts, and this is called **rhythm**.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author’s psychological analysis of music, shifting from the "charm" of melodic return and the "trill" to the structural role of tempo and rhythm. The author is establishing a link between musical meter and the physical responses of the human body.</meta>

<page-num>392</page-num>
# Musical Art, Melody
<meta>catchword: Counter-</meta>

The more uniformly and unchanged the composer pursues the chosen tempo <note>original: *Zeitmaaß*; the measurement of time or pace</note>, the more comprehensible and impactful his music is. Yet, tempo and rhythm do permit a species of variation that even constitutes a beauty, which we call **contrast**. Tempo contrasts on a large scale when the first part of a piece is played slowly and the second quickly—rhythm contrasts on a small scale through the alternation of short and long tones.

Musical rhythm is an imitation of poetic meter <note>original: *Silbenmaaß*; literally "syllable measure"</note>, which has an excellent influence on song. The more striking the length and brevity of the syllables of a language are, the more beautiful its rhythm, because it provides a stronger contrast. For this reason, the Italian language <note>original: *welsche Sprache*; a historical German term for Romance languages, specifically Italian in a musical context</note> is more advantageous for music than French, and Neapolitan is superior to all others; for one believes they are hearing fragments of **Greek verse** when hearing it spoken from a certain distance.

We have said that "return" <note>the repetition of themes or tones</note> in music is a charm that affords us pleasure. The **beat** <term>beat: *Takt*; the regular pulse or rhythmic unit of the music</term> is also a return, a charm—it is a repeated pressure that excites within us a 

---

<page-num>393</page-num>
# and musical Expression.
<sig>Bb 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: art</meta>

**counter-pressure** which acts from the inside out; for as soon as our thinking power has recognized the uniform return of the intervals established between the tones, it not only enjoys the frequent testing of a truth found through its observation, but it fills every interval with a movement that is a reaction of our musical feeling. This movement is animated by the mental representation of the proven interval, acts upon the whole body, and explains the **involuntary beating of time** <note>tapping one's foot or hand to the music</note> which masters those spectators who surrender themselves entirely to the influence of the music.

The beat can also make a physical impression on the muscles of the heart, if they are very irritable <note>in the 18th-century medical sense: highly responsive to stimuli</note>. I believe that the often-repeated uniformity of the beat can bring about a correspondence with the **pulse**—namely, that the pulse, stimulated by the impulse toward **assimilation** <note>original: *Verähnlichung*; the process of becoming similar or synchronizing</note>, can become faster or slower in order to reach such a state that its beats coincide with one, or two, or a half of these expected intervals. Therefore, I am convinced that the beat, when it is uniform and supported by a striking rhythm, is one of the most powerful means through which the musical

# Musical Art, Melody
<meta>catchword: Spi-</meta> <note>original: Ge-; referring to Gemüthsstimmung on the next page</note>

...art can act physically upon the passionate movement of the human soul <note>original: *Gemüth*; refers to the mind, soul, or emotional disposition</note>.

So high a value is placed on expression in music that I must here share my thoughts on this confused subject as well:

There is a musical expression which one should, however, call taste rather than expression. It consists of a kind of caricature <note>original: *Carikatur*; here used in the sense of an artistic exaggeration or a stylized heightening of traits rather than a mocking drawing</note> that heightens the **Piano** and the **Forte**, subjects the strict beat of the accompanying instruments to the extravagant tempo of the lead voice, and appears in the latter as an inspiration that communicates itself to the listener when he shares the same conception of the piece's execution as the performer. This tasteful expression is without rules; it is as indeterminate as the line of beauty <note>A reference to the "Line of Beauty," an S-shaped curve theorized by the artist William Hogarth as the basis of grace and variety</note>; but darker and more fluctuating, because it is felt with more uncertainty and imagination than the beauties of the visual arts; for its ideal rests on no reality, but rather on the effort of the player or singer to make intelligible to his listeners what he thinks during a composition. This musical expression expresses the

<page-num>395</page-num>
# and musical Expression.
<meta>catchword: she</meta> <note>original: sie</note>

state of mind of the performer and not the meaning of the piece, which actually means nothing other than what convention or imagination wishes to assign to it.

One must therefore clearly distinguish a performance beautified by the performer's emotion <term>emotion: *Affect*; in 18th-century theory, an "affect" was a specific, categorized emotional state that music aimed to represent or provoke</term> from the harmful and vain endeavor of the composer to paint passions and to subordinate the inherent beauties of music to a foreign, secondary purpose.

That a musical expression of passions is possible is something the tales of the Greeks concerning the wonders of their musicians have told us, and the sentimentalities <note>original: *Empfindeleien*; a likely reference to the *Empfindsamer Stil* or "sensitive style" of the mid-18th century, which emphasized intimate, subjective emotion</note> of our century have quite gladly believed. But can we be certain whether the Greeks, who loved their fatherland so much, did not exaggerate the praise of these wonders? Can we judge correctly the reality of an art whose means have been lost, which was perhaps based only on a practiced imagination, and whose attempts mostly fail with us? — It has much in common with sorcery, and its credibility depends on the enthusiast.

Imitation of nature is indisputably the main purpose of the visual arts — but is music

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of musical expression. Having previously distinguished "tasteful" expression from "objective" expression, the author now challenges the idea that music should mimic the natural world or specific words—a practice often called "word-painting."</meta>

<page-num>396</page-num>

# Musical Art, Melody
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

is it a plastic art <note>original: *bildende Kunst*; usually referring to the visual or formative arts like painting or sculpture</note>, and what can it imitate from nature? Perhaps the few primary tones of a great passion? However, this scale is very short and indeterminate; at most, it provides a fitting *intonation* and broken-off cries.

Tempo <term>Zeitmaaß: literally "time-measure," referring to the speed or rhythm of a piece</term> can indeed sometimes attempt to paint the *emotion* <note>original: *Affect*; in 18th-century theory, this refers to a specific, identifiable emotional state like rage, sorrow, or joy</note>; but it has only two means to do so: slowness and speed. Yet rage and jubilant joy can be expressed in no other way than with the same speed—sadness and contented calm both move slowly; how can one distinguish such opposite *emotions*?

Storms and winds, thunder and lightning, roaring seas and murmuring brooks, roaring lions and bleating sheep, thus remain the frequent and unfortunate subjects that imitative music tends to choose. It presents these as a *soprano* playing Alexander the Great <note>A reference to the practice in Baroque opera where high-voiced singers, often castrati, portrayed powerful masculine heroes</note>, or as a child playing *Goliath*. Why then does one force melody to leave the sweet tone of its own truth, only to howl after foreign sounds that are, to it, barbaric?

Even the most successful of such word-playing *expressions* <note>The author is likely referring to "madrigalisms" or text-painting, where the music literally mimics the lyrics (e.g., a rising scale on the word "ascend")</note> seem to me a misfortune for music. They appear to me like a word,

---

<page-num>397</page-num>

# and musical Expression.

that has been cut into a diamond; the diamond certainly serves to express the word, but in doing so, the diamond has been ruined.

If every word had its own true tone in which it always had to be spoken, and if it were possible to arrange words as melodically as rhetorically; then one could give music an expression that would produce the most wonderful effects.

Although I am a declared enemy of our so-called musical *expression*, I do not at all deny the possibility of a far better one: On the contrary, I will now endeavor to share some advice for inventing it:

Expression is more inherent to no art than to oratory <term>Redekunst: the art of rhetoric or public speaking</term>, and yet it has no other means for it than hieroglyphs <note>The author uses "hieroglyphs" metaphorically to describe words as arbitrary symbols that only have meaning because we agree on them</note>, with which convention and habit associate a meaning.

Thus, the determination of meaning, agreement—*conventional* meaning—could become the only powerful means of assigning a true and strong expression to music.

If the *convention* of language can make a merely hieroglyphic word entirely identical with a concept that in itself has nothing in common with it...

<summary>The author critiques the "unfortunate" tendency of music to imitate natural sounds or literal words, arguing that such "word-painting" ruins the inherent beauty of the music. He suggests that musical expression should instead rely on "convention"—arbitrary but agreed-upon meanings, similar to how language functions.</summary>

<page-num>398</page-num>

# Musical Art, Melody
<meta>catchword: One</meta>

can do, it is indeed much easier to unite two feelings with one another through habitual agreement <note>original: *gewohnte Uebereinkunst*; the author is referring to the Enlightenment-era psychological theory of "associationism," where ideas become linked in the mind through experience</note>, since they are of the same essence.

Two feelings that are frequently excited at the same time lie together in the memory and must, therefore, always awaken each other. One can fuse the most foreign things together through the pairing of ideas. If, for instance, a person’s nourishment from childhood were accompanied by dinner music <term>Tafelmusik: literally "table music," instrumental music performed during aristocratic banquets or festive meals</term> and they were told: this musical piece signifies the beef, the partridges, or the tart you are eating; this other piece signifies this or that wine—then such a person would certainly attribute no other expression to music than a savory one; for the feelings of taste and hearing would awaken inseparably for them, because they rest in the memory chained to one another.

But this pairing habit would work even more easily if it had to connect the music with sensations that have more affinity with it than eating and drinking.

I am therefore convinced that the currently meager relationships between music and our emotions <note>original: *Gemüthsbewegungen*; literally "movements of the soul," a standard term for human feelings or affects</note> could be infinitely expanded if more and more precise meanings were established.

---

<page-num>399</page-num>

# and Musical Expression.
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

One should assign a particular passionate meaning to every tone, every chord, and every transition; then the expression would know which tone it should preferably make the dominant one, which chord it should lay as the foundation, and which transition it should seek.

Our tempo <term>Zeitmaas: literally "time-measure," referring to both the speed and the rhythmic structure of a piece</term>, which already knows how to distinguish between violent and gentle passions in general, could express all sub-types of these through rhythm, if a specific poetic meter <term>Silbenmaas: literally "syllable-measure," referring to the rhythmic patterns of ancient poetry like dactyls or iambs</term> were dedicated to each of them; and habit would finally, through its powerful hand, impose even the most arbitrary expressions upon us as truths.

The ancients <note>Referring to the classical civilizations of Greece and Rome</note> undoubtedly possessed such precise conventional definitions; they presumably constituted their various modes <term>Modos: the musical scales of ancient Greece, which were believed to have specific moral and emotional effects on the listener</term>, of which we know nothing more than that each excited its own primary passion. Moreover, the language of the Greeks had more accent and more prosody than ours; consequently, their rhythm could be more striking. The intonations of their orators were purer and more definite; for they had their fundamental pitch given to them, and their declamation <note>The art of reciting or speaking in a dramatic or formal manner</note> had a type of song-like quality, as we can surmise from the instruments that accompanied their tragedies.

<summary>The author argues that musical meaning is a product of habitual association rather than natural imitation, suggesting that a systematic "language" of music could be built by consistently pairing specific chords and rhythms with specific emotions. He points to the ancient Greeks, whose musical modes and rhythmic prosody achieved a level of emotional precision that modern music has lost.</summary>

<page-num>400</page-num>

# Musical Art, Melody
<meta>catchword: yet</meta>

But even if we possessed all the advantages of the Greeks <note>The author refers to the Ancient Greek concept of "Ethos," where specific musical modes were believed to directly cause specific emotional or moral states in the listener.</note> and better means to give our music a specific expression, I would still hardly be able to reconcile myself with the latter.

I see how much constraint, mutilation, and false means "expression" has brought into music: It dictates the tone and the turn of thoughts to musical invention, even though this creative force wishes to have nothing dictated to it. It commands her <note>In German, "Invention" (*Erfindung*) is a feminine noun, so the author personifies it as a female creator.</note> to leave the main idea—no matter how successful it may be—unfinished, to seek out others whose novelty is not at her command, and to break these off again as soon as her meaning changes. Through this, expression banishes unity and repetition, the two essential beauties of music. It changes the meter whenever it pleases, and thereby harms music itself; for meter undoubtedly acts upon the physical movement of the heart, but only when it hammers upon it continuously. Expression even desecrates the purity of tones through strangled and whiny sounds that are meant to represent an affect <note>original: *Affect*; in 18th-century theory, an "affect" was a specific, categorized emotion (like rage, sorrow, or joy) that a composer sought to trigger in the audience.</note> choking the song, and it disturbs the security of the meter through the swaying *tempo rubato* <term>tempo rubato: literally "stolen time," a technique where the performer subtly speeds up and slows down, deviating from a strict beat for emotional effect</term>, in which I always imagine a tightrope walker who deliberately balances incorrectly just to give the spectators the joy of seeing that he

<meta>catchword: Something</meta>

does not break his neck after all. The fondness for expression leads to all these abuses: It seems to me, therefore, to be a secret enemy of the art of music, and for that reason, I hate it.

Music has a heavenly truth for and within itself, which is far more noble and beautiful than passions or the imitation of them. It has its own reality, its own beauty, which needs nothing foreign—and perhaps even something resistant to it—in order to please. Unity and repetition are its essence; whatever hinders these two is musically false.

In a word: I have a certain animalistic love <note>The author uses "animalistic" (*animalische*) here to mean "physical" or "visceral"—a direct, bodily response to the sound itself rather than an intellectual interpretation of an emotion.</note> for the reality of music, which is too intense for me to sacrifice it to the Platonic adoration of its shadow <note>A reference to Plato’s "Allegory of the Cave," where people mistake shadows on a wall for reality. The author argues that pure music is the reality, while "expression" or "imitation" is merely a pale shadow.</note>.

<page-num>402</page-num>

# Something about Animals.

Whether animals are not more perfect in their own way than we are seems to be a very possible thing, precisely because we are more *perfectible* <note>The author uses the term "perfectible" to mean "capable of being improved or perfected." In 18th-century philosophy, this was often seen as a uniquely human trait—the ability to learn and change—whereas animals were seen as "static."</note> than they: for the further one has to go on the path that leads to perfection, the further one is removed from it.

Whether the essence of animals is less perfect than our own is another question. They are certainly unhappier—for they are slaves: but Aesop <note>Aesop (c. 620–564 BCE) was a Greek storyteller credited with a number of fables now collectively known as Aesop's Fables. Tradition describes him as a physically deformed slave who gained his freedom through his wit.</note> was also a slave, and many a lady of his time may have held him and his fables to be stupid because he was hunchbacked, and his animals had more sense than the lady.

How many learned serfs from Egypt and Greece must have been sold in Rome by those who could not speak with them? One must, above all, understand someone if one wishes to judge him. An unknown king in chains looks like any other prisoner, and fate is no proof of merit.

The animals perhaps know us more than we know them, because they understand us better than we [understand]

---

<page-num>403</page-num>

<header>Something about Animals.</header>
<sig>Cc 2</sig>

them. The effort to make one another understood is great on both sides, and the skill is small. Presumably, in such conversations, the animal considers its master more unintelligible than the annoyed master considers his suffering animal; perhaps the animals say to one another: "You are a great man!" in the same sense as we say to someone: "You are a great donkey!" <note>The author is playing on the irony of human insults; while humans call someone a "donkey" to imply stupidity, he imagines animals using "man" as a similar insult for a lack of understanding.</note>

Our philosophers, moved by various correct decisions and the wonderful precautions of animals, have nonetheless granted them two Latin predicates with contemptuous generosity; these are: *Instinct* and *Expectatio casuum similium* <note>original: "Expectation of similar cases." This is the philosophical idea that animals don't "reason" but simply expect a result based on past experience—for example, a dog expecting food when it hears a bell.</note>.

The former errs less than we do:—yet it is not the intellect of animals— —It is a law of wisdom, placed into their bodily structure for its preservation. But the second thing is likely only a translation of the result of memories and comparisons; for the expectation of similar cases is experience, and the latter constitutes our entire wisdom.

Animals have senses, often better than our own, consequently they have feelings and concepts; they have memory and the power of comparison, because they distinguish similar cases, and consciousness,

<page-num>404</page-num>

<header>Something about Animals.</header>

...because they understand the art of biding their time to their advantage. I would like to know, then, what power of the soul we might possess exclusively that would not fall under this **animal reason**? One will say: "our soul-powers are far nobler, more ingenious, and greater;" but do we know all of theirs? Can we judge correctly of beings so little known to us? Human skill is proportionate to human need. In this consists our perfection, and consequently, alas, also in the multitude of our needs. Want and necessity make one more skillful than wealth or contentment. Thus, if animals have fewer needs than we do, they require less industry; if they possess all that they need, then they are doubly more perfect than we are.

Just as an animal cannot judge the sublimity of the many arts and sciences required for distant seafaring, so too do we know little of what knowledge birds require when they migrate from one pole to the other at specific intervals.

Our arts are basically nothing more than overcome difficulties. They are noble, great, and wonderful to us—we who admire a solo violinist and pay him more dearly than other

<column-break/>

<sig>Cc 3</sig>

despised servants who nourish us; but I suspect that among the animals there are no solo violinists. <note>The author is making a satirical point about human values, noting that we often value entertainment and "unnecessary" arts over the essential labor of those who provide our food.</note>

> "Animals have no sciences, no philosophy, jurisprudence, mathematics <note>The original uses "Mathesis," a Latin-derived term for the mental discipline of mathematics.</note>, nor medicine."

Why not? To be sure, they fight more often over a bone or a female than over a truth; however, perhaps they have found as much of the latter as they need; and we—we boast of such sciences that merely quarrel over the truth!

> "Animals have no arts."

Yet beavers build their dwellings, birds their nests, spiders produce their webs, insects the most wonderful casings—and can we make wax or honey?

The most probable advantage of man over animals, it is said, is his **perfectibility** <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "perfectibility" (Perfectibilität) was the capacity for self-improvement and learning. Thinkers like Rousseau argued this was the defining difference between humans and animals.</note>; however, is the possible measure of theirs known to us? Can a Greenlander <note>In the 1700s, "Greenlander" was a common term in European philosophy to describe the Inuit people, often used as a stereotypical example of "natural" or "primitive" man in contrast to "civilized" Europeans.</note> estimate the distance that a scholar finds between Newton and a peasant?

<header>Something about Animals.</header>

Animals are even perfectible <note>The author uses the term "perfectibel," a key Enlightenment concept referring to the capacity for self-improvement and learning.</note> for us, even though we have so few points of contact with them; and for that reason, the training of the most skillful pointer <note>original: "Hühnerhundes," a type of hunting dog used for birds.</note> is perhaps more wonderful than the education of the greatest philosopher. In both cases, the pupils contribute the most to their own development.

The only personal advantage of man over animals that is clear to me is his hands. It seems more his body than his soul gives him an essential and unique advantage over them. But is the soul of a cripple born without hands a spirit of a lower class than the soul of a sleight-of-hand artist?

> "The great advantage of man," I will be
> "told, consists in the knowledge of God;
> "and animals know nothing of Him!"

The busyness of our religious intolerance is truly to be wondered at! Every human has a sincere impulse to form a concept of God as best he can. These efforts must be just as diverse as the people themselves, and yet they are condemned by one another. One should imagine the diversity of religious services like a great orchestra, tuned to the praise of the Highest, in which each

To nations we hardly know, and even to those not yet discovered, one denies the possibility that they have found no God, and yet one does not permit them to invent a form of worship for themselves.

But one is not satisfied with probing the consciences of men; one also wants to fathom the hearts of animals. It is claimed that they cannot possibly know anything of a God, and that these poor creatures are the only true atheists that can exist.

Our pride has made this opinion into an article of faith, and I fear that great offense will be taken at my doubt regarding it. Yet our theologians <note>original: "Schriftgelehrten," meaning "scribes" or "scripture-scholars."</note> must allow the young ravens to call upon the Lord <note>A reference to Psalm 147:9: "He giveth to the beast his food, and to the young ravens which cry."</note>; and since they do not take it ill of these birds of prey, I would dearly like to grant my beloved dog the same advantage. > “Animals surely won't have a
> ”religion?”

Why not? The purest religion takes place in the heart and has the fewest rituals. How can we see into the hearts of animals? And, assuming they had rituals, would we understand them? Can a dog ever dream that a Mass is a divine service?

> “So could animals perhaps even
> ”have a morality?”

I see that they, just like us, are guided by punishments and rewards, and I suspect that especially the bees and ants, because they work socially, may have laws.

Animals express almost the same passions as we do; they are cruel and noble, proud and humble, angry and patient, sad and joyful, faithful and false, envious and affectionate, just as we are.

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>409</page-num>
<header>Something about Animals.</header>

That we perceive fewer traces of misdeeds among them than among ourselves does not mean they are incapable of committing them; but one could just as easily conclude from this that they are better than we are.

If greed and property lead us to most evil acts, then animals at least lack this powerful spur to evil; and if a loving feeling is the true impulse that steers the will toward the good, then we cannot deny animals a moral goodness either.

They even seem to love their enemies — mankind. The dog beaten half to death, the beast of burden falling under its load, the hunted dying stag, and the lamb on the slaughter bench look at their executioners with a gaze in which patience, melancholy, and friendliness are poured out.

I believe that all animals, even the wild ones, would be affectionate if the tyrant of creation <note>A common Enlightenment-era rhetorical label for Man, who rules over the rest of nature with "tyrannical" force.</note> knew how to reconcile their justified mistrust. In Rome, a lion nestled at the feet of a slave who had been thrown to him, because this man had once pulled a thorn from its paw in Africa <note>The author refers to the classical legend of Androcles and the Lion.</note>.

# Something about Animals.

[...load, what kind of opinion] can they have of ours, if they judge our behavior toward them alone? What animal pursues, tortures, and eats all others? What animal kills another for his mere pleasure and pastime? Can the innocent mail-horse <note>original: "Postgaul"; a horse used to pull mail coaches, often driven to exhaustion</note> dying under the whip not more strictly deny the morality of his master than the ship-pulling convict <note>original: "schiffziehende Missethäter"; refers to a galley slave or criminal sentenced to hard labor towing vessels</note>?

Who, then, are the animals? What kind of beings dwell in their forms?

The Brahmins <term>Braminen: Members of the high Hindu caste; the author refers to their belief in the transmigration of souls into various living forms</term> consider them as inns for wandering souls.

Jewish Kabbalists <term>Cabbalisten: Practitioners of Jewish mysticism who often discussed the "gilgul" or reincarnation of souls into animals as a form of correction</term> hold them to be prisons for evil spirits, and an ancient Egyptian tradition says that the gods, pursued by Typhon <note>Typhon: A monstrous being in Greek mythology, here identified with the Egyptian god Set; the myth suggests the gods hid in animal forms to escape him</note>, fled to Ethiopia and hid themselves in animal shapes.

For many reasons, I may not decide whether "becoming-animal" <term>Thierwerdung: The transformation of a spiritual being into an animal form</term> belongs to the first or second fall of spirits; however, I do not doubt that it is a purification for spirits.

We have seen that animals have similar soul-powers and passions to ours; that they are, at most in regard to the body, worse off than we are; that only their fate makes the great difference which so strikes our pride—

<meta>catchword: out-</meta>

<page-num>411</page-num>
# Something about Animals.

—[out], and that this fate is very unhappy because they are slaves and have very evil masters.

Yet this dominion, which Providence has entrusted to us, seems remarkable and seems to indicate that the history of animals is different from our own. But at the very least, every human being should consider that it is harder to rule than to obey; that it carries greater duties; that animals can set in motion and exercise the two driving wheels of our morality—self-will <note>original: "Eigenmacht"; the capacity for independent action or power</note> and love—just as much as if they were human; and that we should therefore also show through them whether we are essentially good or evil.

I close with a great word that I heard from an old woman: A peasant was beating his animal most cruelly, and she said to him: "Oh, just consider what you are doing! Man should be the animals' God."
