# A Method of Faith

->for<-

## Those in Need of Faith.

I wish that one could establish a method of teaching the Christian religion in which those propositions that one *must* believe would be distinguished from those that one *can* believe. Through such a clarification, one would forestall that skepticism which makes everything suspicious by proving that some articles of faith imposed upon us are very doubtful.

At the present time, one should make it easy for people to remain Christians and share with their understanding a *critique of pure theology* <term>Kritik der reinen Theologie: A reference to the philosophical style of Immanuel Kant, suggesting a rational boundary-setting for religious claims</term>.

I would therefore want it to be determined: what one is essentially bound to believe in order to be a Christian; what is good to add to this faith; and what truly remains disputed.

<column-break/>

<page-num>413</page-num>
<header>A Method of Faith for Those in Need of Faith. 413</header>
<meta>catchword: mer (from the word 'immer' or 'immerwährend')</meta>

Those articles which one *must* believe are indisputable: a single God; the incarnation of Christ; His death and resurrection—and our own resurrection and an eternal life through Him; the necessity of baptism and faith proclaimed by Him; His morality and the truth of all that the Scripture—especially the New Testament, but even more so He Himself—has said of God, of Himself, and of those doctrines which immediately concern religion.

Those propositions which we should *strive* to believe, because they would further calm, comfort, and uplift us, seem to me to be primarily the following:

The divinity of Christ; for the greater He is, the more we have to expect from His power; the divine truth of the Scripture, because it is our guide—not as a literal inspiration from God <note>original: "wörtliche Eingebung"; the author rejects the idea that every word of the Bible was dictated by God, favoring a more general divine guidance</note>, for this is neither stated nor believable, but as a work of Providence <term>Vorsehung: The protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power</term>, which we can trust to have certainly ensured the unadulterated security of the essentials in such an important revelation; furthermore, the power of prayer, especially in the name of Jesus; for even if confidence in it could be exaggerated, it would al-

<summary>The author proposes a "critique of pure theology" to categorize Christian doctrines into essential requirements and helpful but non-essential beliefs. He defines core faith as centered on God, the resurrection, and Christ's teachings, while viewing literal biblical inspiration as less certain than divine providence.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, articles of faith, theology, biblical inspiration, divinity of Christ, providence, baptism, resurrection, prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of religious doctrines that are not strictly mandated but are beneficial for the believer's comfort. It specifically addresses the Trinity and the distinction between essential faith and theological speculation.</meta>

<page-num>414</page-num>
<header>A Method of Faith</header>
<meta>catchword: sentliche</meta>

...ever more comfort than harm; finally, there is the ancient, profound mystery of the Trinity <note>Original: *Dreyfaltigkeit*</note>, of which Plato and the Brahmins <note>The author refers to the 18th-century understanding of Indian philosophy, suggesting that the concept of a "triad" is a universal philosophical idea found outside the Bible</note> have spoken more than the Scripture itself. Yet, one who cannot conceive that three persons constitute one God can find comfort in the fact that Scripture neither stated this nor commanded it to be believed. Such a person does better to imagine it under the image of light or sound—both of which unite three different and independent things into one essence—rather than in the form of an arithmetic contradiction.

The number of disputed points is infinite. They could be divided into: the Futile, which no one can decide because tradition remains silent on them; and those that seek to explain an ambiguous meaning in the Scripture. Regarding the latter, I believe everyone is permitted to judge, since men just like himself have presumed to decide such matters under threat of damnation. In general, however, one should distinguish between what is expressly commanded and what is merely said—the words of Christ from the words of the Apostles—the clear from the obscure—and the dogmatic from the physical or the historical.

The Christian faith is, in and of itself, very brief. It has, in my opinion, only three es-

<column-break/>

<page-num>415</page-num>
<header>for Those in Need of Faith.</header>
<meta>catchword: Die</meta>

sential objects: baptism, an excellent system of morality, and Christ. The first is an effortless initiation; the second is highly believable because it is self-evident. Only the third is a stumbling block—**to the Gentiles foolishness and to the Jews a scandal!** <note>A paraphrase of 1 Corinthians 1:23. The author highlights that the nature of Christ is the primary challenge for rationalists and traditionalists alike</note> Thus Paul prophesied that the mystery of Christ would be more contrary to human reason than many other extraordinary things that are perhaps more improbable.

The most necessary and difficult thing for a Christian is faith. It is commanded of us above all else, yet it is the thing that can least be commanded: no one can prescribe or impose it upon another; rather, it lies within us. Only a destiny, which theologians call Grace <term>Gnade: In this context, divine influence that enables faith</term>, and our own inner need, can develop it.

Man has a natural urge to believe—a need to find peace through a decision. His motives are: rational conclusions and sentiments. The latter are the most victorious, especially when the former cannot provide very rigorous proof; for in any state of uncertainty, our inner sense <note>Original: *Selbstgefühl*</note> tends to lean toward that which offers hope or pleasure.

<page-num>416</page-num>

# A Method of Faith

<meta>catchword: arouse,</meta>

Skepticism <note>Original: *Zweifelsucht*. Literally a "sickness of doubt," implying a compulsive or pathological state of questioning.</note> has not yet proven that the history of Christ is false or impossible. It has merely scavenged insignificant contradictions and historical errors—which can easily creep into a human-made compilation of oral traditions—and presented them as evidence against the truth of the essential matters. It has made the incomprehensible seem ridiculous, simply because the intellect <note>Original: *Verstand*</note> is just as little able to explain the falsehood of that which it cannot grasp as it is to explain its correctness. But the character of the incomprehensible is inherent in so many things, and the mystery of Christ is made so acceptable to reason <note>Original: *Vernunft*</note> through his moral teachings, that the matter belongs at least to the class of things that *could* exist, even if we do not perceive the manner in which they are possible. In short, the matter is at least uncertain.

When an uncertain matter concerns us closely, when it is bound up with happiness and misfortune, our reason always chooses the safest path and our will the most pleasant. Here, the case is more urgent than in any other. *Whoever believes shall be saved; whoever does not believe shall be damned!* This grave pronouncement, the truth of which is nonetheless not impossible, should therefore arouse in all people the most eager wish

<column-break/>

<page-num>417</page-num>

### for Those in Need of Faith.

<sig>D d</sig>

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

to be able to believe—a wish which, once it has arisen, lays the foundation for faith.

Does it not seem unnatural to resist the desire that our happiness might be real, and to slowly kill our innate sense of hope through torturous doubts and mournful possibilities? Yet so many would rather think themselves unhappy than believe themselves happy. They can prove neither state to themselves perfectly, and yet they choose worry over rest—vexation over joy—and fearsome misfortune over hopeful bliss. Only in this single point do they act against the ordinary course of the rational will. Should one not suspect here a particular and peculiar corruption of the human willingness to believe <note>Original: *Glaubwilligkeit*. The author suggests that humans have a natural inclination to believe, which has been distorted by modern skepticism.</note>?

In common life, would a tired, lost traveler not sooner follow the one who promises to lead him to a magnificent inn, than the one who admits that he himself does not know where he is going?

Not everything probable is true, nor is everything improbable false; and nothing is more...

This feeling is the highest degree of historical faith, and its objects become truths for us when they harmonize with our <term>self-feeling</term> <note>Original: *Selbstgefühl*. 1) That all feelings are based on self-feeling; that our choice is determined by its aversion or inclination, and that faith is nothing other than feeling; regarding this, one should read what I have said in the articles: **Self-feeling**, **Choice**, and especially in the article: **Truth**.

These light and comfortable exercises, when applied to faith, develop it, and they are quite particularly recommended to Christians. In the objects with which it occupies itself, this practice finds just as many reasons to become even more convinced as superstition discovers doubts from its own perspective. Gradually, this conviction grows and bears fruit: it practices itself in works of faith and becomes passionate through doing good: thereby it attains the highest degree of its perfection. The love of one's neighbor leads it to the love of Christ, and this leads to God. This is the point of universal union and the highest happiness!

We see the reward for the persistent effort to believe in the serene joy of true Christians.

Faith is an art that seems impossible to the unpracticed, but it is worth the effort to learn it.

# A Method of Faith

...accustom; I felt a need, and I surrendered myself to the wish to believe.

I found that the pride of our intellect also has its ridiculous side, and I used its own weapons against it.

I exchanged possibilities that cause unhappiness for expectations that promise happiness, and I broke myself of the habit of unbelief.

This is the path I have taken, and now I practice the mental representation of desired truths. These truths, impressed upon us through feeling and habit, will certainly be called prejudices; but are not habitual doubts also prejudices? Who doubts with certainty? And who has certainty? We have none that is not based on feeling: hearing and seeing are sensations, and even a <term>demonstration</term> <note>In 18th-century philosophy, a "demonstration" refers to a formal logical or mathematical proof that moves from premises to a necessary conclusion.</note> is composed of felt experiences—of ideas that we have all received through the senses. Should the inner sensation of the soul have less certainty than the outer sensations of the senses?

<column-break/>

# for Those in Need of Faith.
<sig>D d 3</sig>

Harmony with our <term>self-feeling</term> <note>Original: *Selbstgefühl*. A key Enlightenment concept referring to an individual's immediate, internal awareness of their own existence and emotional state.</note> is the best and perhaps the most certain truth for us! It is a truth that we feel more powerfully than a demonstration: for the latter acts only upon the intellect and persuades it—the intellect finds nothing to object to and must remain silent; but an aroused self-feeling acts upon our whole being and activates our decisive will. — We decide and desire that the matter be true.

If Scripture commands in one place that we search it <note>Likely referring to John 5:39, "Search the scriptures."</note>, and indicates in another that we should <note>Refers to 2 Corinthians 10:5, "bringing into captivity every thought to the obedience of Christ."</note> take reason captive, both passages seem to me to unite in this: that we should exert our reason to form the most believable and comprehensible mental representation of those mysteries that we must believe. — Our intellect should seek to serve our faith, not to dispute it.

I therefore believe I have the right to depart from the opinions of all churches whenever I thereby come closer to the <term>literal sense</term> <note>Original: *Wortverstande*. The plain, grammatical meaning of the biblical text as opposed to later theological interpretations.</note>, or when I [can provide] myself with a comprehensible expla-

# A Method of Faith

...explanation of an inherently hidden matter can facilitate my faith.

For example: I cannot wonder enough that it has occurred to no one to understand the **Words of Institution** <note>The "Words of Institution" are the specific words spoken by Jesus during the Last Supper ("This is my body..."), which established the sacrament of the Eucharist.</note> of the **Lord's Supper**, the triple interpretation of which has caused a triple schism in the church, in a literal sense. Christ did not say: "This bread is God or my person;" or "I am hidden within it;" or "it signifies me;" but rather: "this is my body—this wine is my blood!"

Now, a person's body and blood are neither his person nor his dignity, nor can they contain or signify such things; I therefore cannot see why one has chosen such forced interpretations rather than the clear literal meaning of the words—why one does not want to believe that the bread *is* the body—and the wine *is* the blood of Christ, since He Himself said it so clearly in this way and no other.

If one indeed wanted to quarrel, the material for investigation would have remained: in what way and manner can bread and

<column-break/>

# for Those in Need of Faith.
<sig>D d 4</sig>

wine be the body and the blood of Christ?

On this point, I have particular thoughts which I wish to share with those who occupy themselves with the permitted inquiry into the **Incarnation** <note>The "Incarnation" (Menschwerdung) is the Christian doctrine that God became human in the form of Jesus Christ.</note> of Christ.

That this Being, the first and most noble among all, was not begotten from sinful seed is a pronouncement of Scripture. A germ was necessary to form his body. The two purest of all substances are, without fail, wine and grain. <note>The author is proposing a "physicalist" theory: since Jesus is the "bread of life," he suggests his physical body was literally composed of the purest biological elements of grain and grapes.</note> Could one not assume that God, his Father—for He had no other—mixed a seed of wine and grain in order to develop them in Mary's womb into an **incorruptible body**? <note>An "incorruptible body" refers to a physical form that is not subject to the natural processes of decay and rot.</note> Could one not further believe as a result: that Christ, the Creator and Sustainer of the world, placed a physical connection with his body and blood into the bread and into the wine...

# Method of Faith, for Those in Need of Faith.
<sig>Dd 5</sig>

...placed <note>The author is likely referring to a divine or spiritual substance "placed" within the elements of the Eucharist.</note>, which the power of faith can appropriate for our body?

Through this hypothesis, many passages of Scripture that deal with the communion of the body of Christ, a new spiritual body, and the like, have become much more understandable to me than they are to others.

---

The work of **redemption** <note>The "work of redemption" (Erlösungswerk) refers to the process by which humanity is saved from sin through Christ.</note> is likely the most incomprehensible part of Christ’s story.

The wrath of God, a necessary reconciliation of the same, the merit of the suffering of an innocent being—these are pronouncements of Scripture that I can least reconcile with my concepts of the **Deity** <note>original: "Gottheit"</note>.

I wish that these expressions might be merely the consequences of that conception which the Jews had formed of a fearsome God. This people was guided by very material concepts. Temporal punishments and rewards were almost the only motives that Moses and their prophets had used to

<meta>catchword: guide</meta>

<column-break/>

guide them. What wonder then, if Scripture has continued to speak in this language, and to present all loving means of improvement as the punitive judgments of an angry God?

The work of redemption seems to be quite particularly connected with the **Fall** <note>The "Fall" refers to the original transition of humanity (and, in the author's view, other spirits) from a state of innocent obedience to God to a state of guilty disobedience.</note> of man and of the spirits. Since the manner in which these great, true crimes against the Deity itself were committed is hidden from man—presumably because the mere, impotent thought of it could be misused—one mystery increases another: yet I am more inclined to believe what I do not comprehend because I know nothing about it, than that which I cannot comprehend because it contradicts my ideas of God. I therefore prefer to imagine the matter as follows:

According to certain traditions, the **apostasy** <note>The "apostasy of the spirits" (Abfall der Geister) refers to the rebellion of angels or celestial beings against God.</note> of the spirits moved the benevolent God, whose perfection could not involve itself directly with evil, to let other powerful beings go forth, who should contest those fallen spirits and perhaps bring them back. These beings were **We**; we were seduced and lost the power of our weapons to them. We became humans, into the...

<meta>catchword: Ma</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the traditional view of a "wrathful God" requiring blood sacrifice, suggesting instead that such language was a concession to the ancient Jews' material understanding. He proposes a mystical alternative: that humans were originally celestial beings who fell during a cosmic war against rebellious spirits.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, wrath of God, Jewish law, Moses, Fall of Man, apostasy of spirits, pre-existence of souls, spiritual body, Eucharist</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s alternative explanation of the work of redemption. On the previous page, the author expressed difficulty with the traditional concept of an "angry God" requiring blood sacrifice, suggesting instead that biblical language adapted to the material understanding of the ancient Jews. Here, the author proposes a more "mechanical" or spiritual-physical theory of how Christ’s death and resurrection actually function to rescue humanity from the power of fallen spirits.</meta>

# Method of Faith | for those in need of faith.

...enclosed in matter; death came over us through sin, and now we first had to be brought back.

Since we had not been sufficient to resist the fallen spirits, a greater, more powerful being had to be sent out from God to perform our work and, above all, to redeem us.

This being was the first after God and through whom He had made everything. It <note>The author uses "it" or "this being" to refer to the pre-existent Christ.</note> had to come to us, for the prisoner cannot go out and meet his redeemer. Christ had to be born and die because perhaps no other way leads over into the **region of corruption** <note>original: "Region der Verwesung"; refers to the realm of decay or the state of death.</note>, where those spirits might dwell whom He was to combat. — There, He presumably had to conquer that which these spirits had stolen from us and which gave them power over us. There He had to reveal Himself to the deceased and set those limits to death that make our **resurrection** <note>The physical and spiritual rising from the dead.</note> possible. He had to die a violent death, for His sinless, incorruptible body could not be killed by any disease: His body had to rise again; because it was perhaps the **seed** <note>original: "Saamenkorn"; a metaphor for Christ's body being the "first grain" that allows a new crop of resurrected bodies to grow.</note>

<column-break/>

was—for the **transfiguration** <note>original: "Verklärung"; the process of a body becoming radiant, spiritualized, and divine.</note> of human bodies, which alone could be transplanted from the land of death into the land of life.

And how much good did His human life not effect beyond this through example, miracles, and spiritual means?? Perhaps He improved a multitude of corruptions in material nature during His presence here, without our knowing it, and had other commissions that are unknown to us. But it has been revealed to us that through His **incarnation** <note>original: "Menschwerdung"; God becoming human.</note> He reconnected the thread that was broken between God and man, and that through His body He established the resurrection of our own.

In this way, I explain to myself the **work of redemption** <note>original: "Erlösungswerk Christi"</note> of Christ. I believe that through it all people have been redeemed from the main consequence of their spiritual fall—which was an indefinite death and bound them to the first fallen spirits for an indefinite time—and that it has been made easy for them to purify themselves from those human sins they commit here in this life.

To the two preceding **hypotheses** <note>Likely referring to his theories on the Eucharist and the nature of Christ's victory over death.</note> I attach no other value than their novelty and that they

**Vocabulary:** Method of Faith, Redeemer, Christ, Region of Corruption, Resurrection, Seed-grain, Transfiguration, Work of Redemption of Christ, Hypotheses.

<summary>The author presents a unique theory of redemption where Christ’s death is a strategic entry into the "region of corruption" to defeat fallen spirits and reclaim what was stolen from humanity. He views Christ’s resurrected body as a "seed" that enables the future transfiguration and resurrection of all human bodies.</summary>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page concludes the second volume of the work. It follows the author's discussion of a physical-spiritual theory of the resurrection, where Christ’s body acts as a "seed" for human transfiguration. The author now shifts from theological explanation to a personal justification for publishing these thoughts, acknowledging their own history as a skeptic and their hope for a gradual growth in faith.</meta>

# Method of Faith.

...to make my faith easier for me. I do not demand that these ideas should be comfortable for anyone else; I only wish that they may not be offensive to anyone.

Regarding this matter, and especially the benefit or harm of this small, fleeting writing, I have consulted a couple of conscientious and learned men. They are of the opinion—though without sharing my view on most points—that at least a public **confession** <note>original: "Bekenntniß"; a formal statement or acknowledgment of one's religious convictions.</note> of the change in my well-known **way of thinking** <note>original: "Denkungsart"; a person’s characteristic mindset or philosophical outlook.</note> could be a duty for me, and an encouraging call for others. Furthermore, I declare that I have written primarily for returning <term>freethinkers: individuals who traditionally reject dogma and authority in favor of reason, but who are now seeking a path back to faith</term>, as I once was, and not at all for theologians. I ask all <term>orthodox believers: those who adhere strictly to the established doctrines and traditional teachings of the Church</term> for their forgiveness, however, if I do not agree with them in everything. I wish and hope to believe more in time. One does not believe as one *wants* to, still less as others want one to, but rather as one *is able* to believe.

---

->End of the second and final small volume.<-

<summary>The author concludes the volume by explaining that their unconventional theological views are intended to assist former skeptics in returning to faith. They emphasize that belief is a matter of personal capacity and honesty rather than forced adherence to dogma.</summary>
